**51,8,4b** Or how, after forty [...], is Luke found to be lying — as they say, blaspheming against their own head — since he states, “It was on the fortieth day that they carried him up to Jerusalem, and from there [they returned] to Nazareth”?

**51,9,1a** And those who are in the grip of ignorance are unaware of the Holy Spirit’s power; for it granted each of the evangelists to relate, for each time and season, the things that were fulfilled in truth.

**51,9,1b** And Matthew, having related only his birth in the Holy Spirit and his conception without a man’s seed, said nothing about the circumcision, nor anything about the two years, of what befell him after the birth, but narrates the coming of the magi,

**51,9,2** as the true word of God testifies to us, with Herod asking the magi the time and ascertaining exactly the time of the star that appeared, and with the magi’s answer, that it was from two years old and under; so that this time is not the one treated by Luke.

**51,9,3** But Luke narrates what came before [the] two years, while Matthew, having told of the birth, leapt to the period of two years and made known what happened after [the] two years.

**51,9,4** That is why Herod too, having reckoned this out after the magi had turned aside by another road, supposed that among the multitude of the children [the] newborn himself would also be found and would be slain together with them.

**51,9,5a** For he ordered the children within the borders of Bethlehem to be killed, those from two years old and under, up to the very day on which the magi had been with him.

**51,9,5b** From this, then, to whom will it not be evident that when the magi came the child had been born two years before?

**51,9,6a** For the narrative itself also makes the whole exactness plain.

**51,9,6b** For Luke tells us that the infant was wrapped in swaddling bands immediately upon his birth and lay in a manger and in a cave, because the lodging had no room.

**51,9,7** For there was a registration at that time, and those who had been scattered from the time of the wars that took place under the Maccabees had been dispersed to every part of the earth, and very few remained dwelling in Bethlehem; therefore in one of the copies of the evangelists it is called the city of David, while another calls it a village, because its land had dwindled to a small extent.

**51,9,8** But when the decree of Augustus the emperor was issued, and those who had been scattered were obliged to come by their families to the place in order to be registered, the crowds streaming in filled the place, and there was no room in the inn because of the throng.

**51,9,9** Afterwards, when the registration was over, each went away to wherever on earth he had his settled dwelling; and so there came to be room in the land.

**51,9,10** And coming, so to speak, in memory of what had happened there, when [the] first year had been completed and the second fulfilled, the parents came there from Nazareth as to this festival.

**51,9,11a** Hence the coming of the magi too took place at such a juncture, when Mary and Joseph were perhaps no longer anywhere in their lodging of that time, the one occasioned by their presence for the registration, which Luke mentions.

**51,9,11b** For they did not find Mary in the cave in which she had given birth; but, as the gospel records, the star, it says, led them as far as the place where the child was.

**51,9,12** And on entering the house they found the babe together with Mary — in a manger no more, in a cave no more, but in a house; so that the exactness of the truth might be known, and the interval of the period of two years, that is, from the time he was born until the magi arrived.

**51,9,13a** And on that night, two years after the birth, the angel appeared and told him to take the mother and the child into the land of Egypt.

**51,9,13b** Therefore Joseph no longer returns to Nazareth, but flees with the child and his mother into Egypt and spends another two years there; and so again, after the death of Herod, the angel [...] turns him back to Judaea.

**51,10,1** For it is in Herod’s thirty-third year that the Lord is born; in the thirty-fifth the magi came; in the thirty-seventh year Herod dies and his son Archelaus succeeds to the kingdom, and he spent nine years on the throne, as has already been said by me elsewhere.

**51,10,2** So, having heard about Archelaus, he withdrew, and came and settled in Nazareth, and from there again he went up every year to Jerusalem.

**51,10,3a** You see how great an exactness there is in the divine gospels concerning each event.

**51,10,3b** But those who are carried along by ignorance, having darkened their own mind, do not know the purpose of each saying, but simply lift up their mouth against the holy [evangelists] and let loose their tongue, saying nothing in truth, but estranging themselves from life.

**51,10,4** Hence, at any rate, Luke, after narrating the first events, again tells how in the twelfth year he went up to Jerusalem, so as to leave no room for those who supposed that he had appeared in the world simply as a mature man and had come to John at the Jordan, as was supposed by Cerinthus and Ebion and the others.

**51,10,5** For the serpent is terrible: going crookedly, it does not stand fast in one opinion, but to some it seems that he was born of intercourse and the seed of a man, to others that he simply appeared as a man.

**51,10,6a** Therefore the holy evangelists, narrating everything in detail, are exact.

**51,10,6b** And Luke, quite naturally, as though carrying the mind up from below to what is above, says, “Now Jesus was beginning to be some thirty years old, and was the son, as people supposed, of Joseph.”

**51,10,7** But the supposing is not the truth; Joseph was indeed in a father’s place, because it had so seemed good to God, but he was not a father, he who had had no union with Mary,

**51,10,8a** except only in that he was called husband on account of the betrothal, being an old man of some eighty years, more or less, with six sons by his first, real wife, and having taken over this charge — as I have also set out more precisely concerning this somewhere else.

**51,10,8b** How, then, was the one who had no union his father?

**51,10,8c** That is impossible.

**51,11,1a** But you will say to me: How then was he called her husband, if he did not have her?

**51,11,1b** But whoever raises this doubt does not know the provisions of the law: that from the time a woman is named to a man, whether she is a virgin or is still in her father’s house, such a woman is called the wife of the man to whom she has been named; for this reason the holy angel says, “Do not be afraid to take your wife.”

**51,11,2** And lest anyone should suppose that some error [is] present in the gospels — for the mystery is amazing and the narrative surpasses man, though to the sons of the Holy Spirit alone it is all smooth and lit up —

**51,11,3** [he says], “He was some thirty years old, being reckoned the son of Joseph, of Heli, of Matthan,” and he carries the descent up to Abraham, where Matthew too began, but goes beyond Noah and reaches Adam, in order to point to the one formed first, who was sought out through the one who came from his lump, that is, from the holy Virgin Mary.

**51,11,4** For he came for the sake of that one formed long ago, and of those begotten by him who wish to inherit eternal life; and having gone beyond Adam he says, “of God.”

**51,11,5a** From this, then, it was perfectly plain that he was God’s Son, yet came in the flesh through Adam’s seed by succession.

**51,11,5b** But the deceived again did not receive enlightenment; rather they contradicted the word, deceiving themselves [and loving the lie] above the truth.

**51,11,6a** And they said: See, a third gospel, the one according to Luke.

**51,11,6b** For this was entrusted to Luke, who was himself also one of the seventy-two who were scattered at the Saviour’s word, but who through Saint Paul turned back again to the Lord and was charged to preach his gospel.

**51,11,7a** And he preaches first in Dalmatia and Gaul and in Italy and Macedonia.

**51,11,7b** But the beginning was in Gaul, as Paul himself also says in his epistles about certain of his followers: “Crescens,” he says, “in Gaul”; for not in Galatia, as some who have gone astray suppose, but in Gaul.

**51,12,1a** But I will proceed to the matter at hand.

**51,12,1b** For although Luke carried the genealogies up from the lower to the higher, and had already given an indication of the coming of God the Word from above, joined at the same time with his dispensation in the flesh, in order to turn the error away from those who had gone astray, they did not understand.

**51,12,2** Therefore, later, the Holy Spirit compels John — who declined to proclaim the gospel out of reverence and humility — in his old age, after ninety years of his life, after his return from Patmos, which took place under Claudius Caesar, and after he had spent a good many years in Asia, to set out the gospel.

**51,12,3a** And he had no need to speak in detail about the coming in the flesh; for it had already been made secure.

**51,12,3b** But as one walking behind certain people, and seeing them gone on ahead and having given themselves over to the roughest places, wandering and thorny, he took thought to call them back to the straight road, and, to make them safe, to proclaim to them like a herald and say: Why do you go astray?

**51,12,3c** Where are you turning?

**51,12,3d** Where are you wandering, Cerinthus and Ebion and the rest?

**51,12,3e** It is not as you suppose.

**51,12,4a** Yes, Christ was plainly born according to the flesh; for see, I myself confess that the Word became flesh.

**51,12,4b** But do not suppose that he exists from the time he became flesh; for he is not from Mary’s times alone, in the way that each of us has existence from his birth, and before being born has none.

**51,12,5** But the holy God the Word — God’s Son, Jesus Christ our Lord — is not from the times of Mary only, nor from the times of Joseph only, nor of Heli nor of Levi nor of Zerubbabel nor of Shealtiel nor of Nathan nor of David, nor from Jacob nor from Isaac nor from the times of Abraham nor of Noah nor of Adam, nor from the fifth day nor from the fourth day nor from the third, nor from the second, nor since heaven and earth came to be, nor since the world existed,

**51,12,6a** but “In the beginning the Word was, and the Word was in God’s presence, and the Word was God.

**51,12,6b** All things came to be through him, and without him not one thing came to be that has come to be,” and so on.

**51,12,7a** Then: “There arose a man, one sent from God; John was his name.

**51,12,7b** He came for witness, to bear witness concerning the light, that all might believe through him.

**51,12,7c** That one was not the light, but was to bear witness concerning the light.

**51,12,7d** There was the true light, which enlightens every man coming into the world.

**51,12,7e** In the world he was, and through him the world came into being, and the world did not know him.

**51,12,7f** He came to his own, and his own did not receive him.

**51,12,7g** But to as many as received him he granted the power to become God’s children, who were begotten not of blood and of flesh, but of God.

**51,12,8a** And the Word,” he says, “became flesh, and dwelt among us.

**51,12,8b** John bears witness and has cried out, saying, ‘This was he of whom I spoke to you’”; and that “of his fullness we have all received.”

**51,12,8c** And he says, “I am not the Christ, but a voice of one shouting in the desert.”

**51,13,1** And when he has narrated all these things, he says, “These things took place in Bethabara”; but in other copies, “in Bethany, beyond the Jordan.”

**51,13,2a** And after this he shows that the disciples from John said to him, “Rabbi, where are you staying?

**51,13,2b** And he said, ‘Come and see’; and they went, and stayed with him that day.”

**51,13,3a** And on the morrow: “It was about the tenth hour; Andrew, the brother of Simon Peter, was one of the two who followed him; he first finds his own brother Simon and says to him, ‘We have found the Messiah,’ which is translated Christ.

**51,13,3b** He brought him to Jesus.

**51,13,3c** Jesus, looking at him, said, ‘You are Simon the son of Jonah; you shall be called Cephas,’ which is translated Peter.

**51,13,4a** On the morrow he wished to go out into Galilee, and he finds Philip, and Jesus says to him, ‘Follow me.’

**51,13,4b** Now Philip was from Bethsaida, of the city of Andrew and Peter.

**51,13,4c** Philip finds Nathanael and says to him, ‘The one of whom Moses wrote in the law, and the prophets too, we have found — Jesus, son of Joseph, the man from Nazareth.’

**51,13,4d** And Nathanael said to him, ‘Can anything good be from Nazareth?’

**51,13,4e** Philip says to him, ‘Come and see.’

**51,13,5a** And Jesus, seeing Nathanael coming to him, says of him, ‘Behold, truly an Israelite, in whom there is no guile.’

**51,13,5b** Nathanael says to him, ‘How do you know me?’

**51,13,5c** Jesus answered and said to him, ‘Before Philip called you, when you were under the fig tree, I saw you.’

**51,13,5d** Nathanael answered him and said, ‘Rabbi, you are the Son of God, you are the King of Israel.’

**51,13,6a** Jesus answered him and said to him, ‘Because I told you that I saw you under the fig tree, do you believe?

**51,13,6b** You shall see greater things than these.’

**51,13,6c** And he said to him, ‘Truly, truly, I say to you, you will behold heaven standing open, and God’s angels going up and coming down upon the Son of Man.’

**51,13,7** And on the third day a marriage was held at Cana in Galilee,” and so on — in order to show that after the temptation’s forty days, and after the return from the temptation itself and the setting out towards Nazareth and Galilee — related by the other three evangelists — he had come back again to the Jordan;

**51,13,8a** as will also be shown from the word of John.

**51,13,8b** He spoke thus when he saw him coming on the next day: “Behold the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world.”

**51,13,8c** And again on another day, when he saw him walking about, he declared, “This is the one about whom I told you: he who comes behind me has come to rank ahead of me, because he was prior to me”; “and John testified,” it says, “saying, ‘I have beheld the Spirit, in a dove’s form, coming down and coming upon him.’”

**51,13,9** But “testified”, and “This is the one I told you of”, are said as of another, a different time already gone by, in order to show that this time was not the same as that time of the baptism, but another, distinct from it.

**51,13,10** For he does not come to John immediately from the temptation, but first into Galilee, and then from Galilee to the Jordan, which was this [...] to John; therefore he says, “This was the one I told you of,” and further the gospel says, “And John testified, ‘I have beheld,’” as of a matter that had happened at some earlier time.

**51,14,1a** After this the beginning of the call of Peter and his companions is indicated, starting from Andrew.

**51,14,1b** For Andrew went up to be with him, being one of the pair who had followed him — men who were John’s disciples, yet still stayed on in Galilee and [at] times gave their leisure to John.

**51,14,2** And straightway, Andrew having remained for that day (the hour was about the tenth), it so happened on the same day that he met his own brother Simon and said the words already quoted, “We have found the Messiah,” and led him to the Lord, and the rest, as the narrative runs, that he said to him, “You shall be called Cephas.”

**51,14,3a** “And on the morrow he wished,” it says, “to go out into Galilee, and he finds Philip, and Jesus says to him, ‘Follow me.’

**51,14,3b** Now Philip was from Bethsaida, of the city of Andrew and Peter.”

**51,14,4** And you see that it gives us this to consider: that of the pair who followed, men under John’s instruction, he has told the name of one only, that it was Andrew, but of the other he has not told it;

**51,14,5** so that we may consider that this one whose name he has not told — because of their being from the same place, sharing the same dwelling, the same trade, the same work — was either John or James, [at any rate] one of the sons of Zebedee.

**51,14,6a** For it was necessary that these be summoned first and Philip after them, following the sequence set down in the gospels: first Peter, next Andrew, then James, then John, and after these Philip.

**51,14,6b** But let us leave off speaking of these matters for now; for the course of this subject is long.

**51,15,1** But we must return to the question [...], which is to be understood: that just as, while already disciples of John, [...] both working at their own trade and giving heed to their discipleship, so too, having spent a first day with Jesus, they went away on the next and again occupied themselves with fishing, as the sequences of the other gospels have it.

**51,15,2** For after Jesus’ going out on the next day, the narrative immediately states, “On the third day a marriage took place at Cana in Galilee, and Jesus’ mother was there; and Jesus too was invited, with his disciples, to the marriage.”

**51,15,3a** And from both the words of this detailed account and its subject it must be understood that he had taken other disciples along with him as well, who stayed with him — perhaps those about Nathanael and Philip, and some others.

**51,15,3b** For Andrew and his companions had gone away; but those who stayed at his side were themselves also invited to the wedding.

**51,15,4a** And after doing this first sign he went down to the region of Capernaum and settled there.

**51,15,4b** And from then on he begins to work other signs there, when he healed the hand of the man who had it withered,

**51,15,5** and also Peter’s mother-in-law — Peter being from Bethsaida but having taken a wife from Capernaum, these places not being far apart in distance — he released her from the fever, and when she was healed she served; so that this sequence is [...].

**51,15,6a** And after this, when he had gone back once more to Nazareth, the place of his upbringing, it was then that he read the book of the prophet Isaiah, he himself thereupon forestalling them and saying, “No doubt you will say this proverb to me: ‘Physician, heal yourself; whatever signs we have heard were done in Capernaum, do here too in your own country.’”

**51,15,6b** And do you see how the sequence of the truth stands?

**51,15,6c** “And because of their unbelief,” it says, “he did not perform any [sign].”

**51,15,7a** And passing on from there to Capernaum he settled there again.

**51,15,7b** And on coming to the parts by the sea, as Matthew relates, he caught sight of Simon Peter and Andrew, his brother, as they cast their nets, and next of James and John, Zebedee’s sons, and he called them definitively; and they, throwing aside their nets at last, followed him.

**51,15,8** But Luke also indicates the exactness of the matter — that they now followed him without turning back, no longer putting off the call — when he says, “As he came to the lake of Gennesaret, he saw Simon and Andrew mending their nets, and he went up into the boat which belonged,” it says, “to Simon Peter and Andrew” (and this indicates that, since he was already known to them, they received him because of their acquaintance), and having gone up he sat down;

**51,15,9** when he says to Peter after the teaching, “Put out into the deep and let down the nets,” and they said, “Master” (for those who had earlier heard [about] him, “Behold, the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world,” already called him Master because of John’s testimony, and because they had stayed with him that one day).

**51,15,10** And afterwards they went out for the second haul; they were astonished at the great number of fish, and Peter said, “Go out from me, since I am a man who is a sinner, Lord”; for it may be that he also felt remorse because he had been called earlier and had gone back to the fish and the whole work of fishing.

**51,15,11** But he said, “Do not be afraid,” encouraging him, that he had not been given up but could still hold fast to the call — for, it says, “from now on you will be catching men”; at which point they also beckoned to their fellows in the second boat to come and join in hauling in the same catch.

**51,15,12** For they were partners, as it says, with Simon; just as has also been shown by me above from this account: of [the] two who followed him [and] had heard from John [...], of which two one was Andrew, [as] we said, we have very rightly understood the other to be, again, perhaps one of the sons of Zebedee, because of their working together, their sharing and their partnership.

**51,15,13** And then after this the four, leaving everything — both the boats and simply everything, as it says, having left them on the land — followed him, as Luke’s testimony has it.

**51,15,14** So that it is shown in every way that no crookedness or contradiction is found in the holy gospels, nor with the evangelists, but all things are clear.

**51,15,15a** But there are intervals of time; for from this point on, Peter and John and their companions having now been joined to him and following him, he was teaching in all Galilee and in Judaea, and as the preaching spread wider he performed the rest of the divine signs.

**51,15,15b** So that the sequence is in every way as follows:

**51,16,1** first, when he had been baptized on the twelfth of Athyr according to the Egyptians, as we said, six days before the Ides of November — that is, a full sixty days before the day of the Epiphanies, the day on which he was born according to the flesh,

**51,16,2a** as the testimony of the Gospel according to Luke has it, that “Jesus was beginning to be some thirty years old, and was the son, as people supposed, of Joseph.”

**51,16,2b** For he was in fact twenty-nine years and ten months old when he came to the baptism — thirty years old, but not full; therefore it says, “beginning to be some thirty years old” — then he was driven out into the wilderness.

**51,16,3** From this point, then, the forty days of the temptation appear in sequence, and the two weeks and a little more, and two days, which he spent after coming back from the temptation into Galilee, namely in Nazareth and around its borders,

**51,16,4a** and one day when he came to John, when John declared, “Behold the Lamb of God, he who bears off the sin of the world,” and the following day, [when] “John was standing there again, with two of his disciples, and gazing at Jesus as he walked about he says, ‘Behold, the Christ, the Lamb of God.’”

**51,16,4b** Next it says, “His two disciples heard it and went after Jesus.”

**51,16,5** This day was the eighteenth following the temptation, as we said before, and the first from John, when Andrew and his companions went after Jesus and remained with him for that day (the hour was about the tenth), when Andrew too found his own brother Simon and brought him to Jesus.

**51,16,6a** Then the gospel says, “On the morrow he wished to go out into Galilee, and he finds Philip, and the Lord says to him, ‘Follow me.’”

**51,16,6b** This in turn was the nineteenth day following the temptation, as the gospel’s sequence has it, [...] the call of Philip and of Nathanael.

**51,16,7a** And then on the third day, it says, a marriage took place at Cana in Galilee, after the two days from John mentioned before.

**51,16,7b** If, then, one adds the twenty days to the temptation’s forty, two months are completed, which, joined to the ten months, complete a year, which is the fulfilment of thirty years counted from the Lord’s birth,

**51,16,8** and Christ is found to have performed the first sign, the changing of the water into wine, at the end of the thirtieth year, as you may grasp if you attend closely to the sequences of the gospels.

**51,16,9** From then on, beginning from this first sign, he both works the other signs and teaches the preaching, his wonderful and ineffable love for mankind and his gospel wonder-working being shown to all people, as I have often been forced to state on account of the deceived, whose ignorance makes them dare to contradict the exactness of the gospels, set out in due sequence by the Holy Spirit.

**51,17,1** For a proof of such exactness will leave no room to those who oppose themselves — for I do not say to the truth, since they are not even able to.

**51,17,2** For it is made plain that the beginning of the rest of the history came from the baptism; for thus it is shown that after the day of the baptism the Lord was in the temptation of the forty days in the wilderness, concerning which the Holy Spirit did not take care to give indication through John; for this had already been indicated through the three evangelists.

**51,17,3a** And again, in the other evangelists, there was no care taken about the other things, because each is aided by each.

**51,17,3b** For indeed the truth, gathered from all of them, is shown to be one and not at variance with itself.

**51,17,4a** For from there, immediately after the temptation, as I said, he comes to Nazareth and stays there, with not one disciple yet in his company.

**51,17,4b** And from there he goes down to John, when Peter is at once called through Andrew, then Philip, and through Philip Nathanael.

**51,17,5** And let no one, doubting this too, busy himself with idle questions on seeing that Andrew was the first to meet Jesus, and that Peter came after and was called through him, so as to begin to fret over this as well.

**51,17,6a** For the meeting did take place with Andrew first, who was younger than Peter in years; but later again, when their complete renunciation took place, it began from Peter.

**51,17,6b** For he was the leader of his own brother, and the Lord knew this, since he is God, knowing the inclinations of hearts and recognising who is worthy to be ranked among the first, and he chose Peter to be the leader of his disciples, as has been clearly shown everywhere.

**51,17,7** For afterwards, when they had come and remained by him for the first day, as I have said, and on the second had journeyed, and on the third day the first divine sign had taken place, some being with him (not Andrew, nor Peter, nor John, nor James, but Nathanael and Philip and their companions, and some others, as can be understood),

**51,17,8a** next, after he had come to Capernaum and gone up again to Nazareth, and from there again to Capernaum, and had done some part of the signs, he went up again to Nazareth and read the book of Isaiah the prophet, at the place where it says, “The Lord’s Spirit is on me, for which reason he anointed me to bring good tidings to the poor,” and the rest.

**51,17,8b** And this took place some days after the day of the Epiphanies.

**51,17,9a** And after John had been delivered up, he went down again to Capernaum and from then on settled there, when the complete call of Peter and John and their companions took place, as he came beside the lake of Gennesaret.

**51,17,9b** And thus the whole sequence stands in proportion, there being nothing contradictory, but the whole account of the gospels being most clear and proclaimed in truth.

**51,17,10a** Why, then, did it seem good to these people, [who] have deceived their own mind and vomited out this heresy into life, to reject the Gospel according to John?

**51,17,10b** For we have rightly called their heresy Alogian, because they do not accept God the Word, who came from above and was proclaimed through John.

**51,17,11** For, not understanding the force of the gospels, they say: The other evangelists have told how Jesus went as a fugitive from the face of Herod into Egypt, and after the flight came and stayed in Nazareth, then received baptism and went away into the wilderness, and after this returned, and after returning began to preach,

**51,18,1** but the gospel in the name of John, they say, is lying; for after saying that the Word became flesh and dwelt among us, and a few other things, it immediately says that a marriage took place at Cana in Galilee.

**51,18,2** And those who draw folly upon themselves do not remember that John, after saying that the Word became flesh and dwelt among us — that is, became man — [himself] also says that he came to John the Baptist in the Jordan and received baptism from him;

**51,18,3** [for] John himself bears witness, saying that John the Baptist had said, “This is he of whom I spoke to you,” and again, “I have beheld the Holy Spirit coming down in a dove’s form and resting upon him,” and, “This is he who takes away the sin of the world.”

**51,18,4a** You see that nothing is set out through forgetfulness, but John left out the things treated by Matthew.

**51,18,4b** For there was no longer any need of those things, but of the complete statement, to refute those who supposed that he is called Christ and Son of God from Mary onwards, and that he was formerly a mere man, but received the title of Son of God by advancement.

**51,18,5** Therefore he takes care of the necessary things (all things being great and necessary, but the heavenly ones more abundantly so), making his narrative that of the dispensation from above.

**51,18,6** And they declare the Gospel according to John uncanonical, since it did not tell these things — I mean those concerning the temptation of the forty days — and they do not think fit to accept it, being in error in every respect and blinded in their understanding.

**51,19,1** For the blessed John, coming fourth of the evangelists in succession — first in the call, after Peter and Andrew, together with James his brother, but last in time in proclaiming the gospel — did not trouble to set out what had been well arranged before him, but places before what had been said the things that had not been said, and [so] discourses.

**51,19,2a** For Matthew begins from Abraham, and after the beginning, and two years after the birth, he takes up the narrative again.

**51,19,2b** Mark begins from the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar, [not] narrating [the] interval of the events after the beginning.

**51,19,2c** And Luke brought in a beginning before the beginning, the account concerning Elizabeth and Mary, which took place before [they] were with child.

**51,19,3a** But John, though earlier in the call, yet having preached later than these, makes secure the account of what was before the coming in the flesh.

**51,19,3b** For most of the things said by him were spiritual, the things of the flesh having already been made secure.

**51,19,4** Therefore he gives his narrative spiritually, of the gift that came to us from above, from the Father who is without beginning, [and] was dispensed, by the Father’s good pleasure, in the womb of the holy Virgin.

**51,19,5** And he did not pass over in silence what was of necessity being sought, but, moved by the Holy Spirit, he [introduces] God the Word, who exists before the ages, begotten from the Father timelessly and without beginning, and he made known that he had come in the flesh for our sake, so that from four evangelists we might hold exactly the whole knowledge, both according to the flesh and according to the Godhead.

**51,20,1** For when all the things concerning the baptism and the temptation had been completed, and Jesus had then come and spent [a period] of a few days, as I have often said, in Nazareth and its borders and around Capernaum, [and] after meeting John at the Jordan [...] and having taken some of the disciples along with him on the next day, he performed this first sign in Cana on the third day from John, the twentieth from his return from the temptation, and [begins] the preaching.

**51,20,2** For he neither says that Christ came to a wedding before the temptation, nor did he work any of the divine signs at all [before] he began the preaching — unless perhaps the things which he is said to have done in play when he was a child.

**51,20,3** For he had to have childhood signs too, so that the other heresies might have no pretext to say that [the] Christ came upon him from the Jordan, which is the dove; for they say this because of the numerical value of omega and alpha, which is “dove,” since the Saviour declared, “I am the Alpha, and the Omega am I.”

**51,20,4** Therefore Luke also, in the twelfth year of his birth, brings in Jesus as having said to Mary, who was looking for him, when he was conversing with the teachers in Jerusalem, “Did you not know that I must be in the things of my Father?”

**51,20,5** so that the argument of those who say that he became Son of God from the time of the baptism, when the dove came upon him, which they say is the Christ, might fall, and so that it might be clearly known that God the Word came to dwell here from above, and having come was made flesh from Mary, and at the Jordan the Spirit came down upon him,

**51,20,6** to signify who it is that is attested by the Father, “This is my beloved Son; hear him,” and so that this might become an example for those who were to be enlightened in him, that they will be counted worthy of [the] gift of the Holy Spirit in the washing, and of forgiveness of sins through the grace given by him.

**51,21,1** From then on he began to perform the complete divine signs in the time of the preaching (“[for] this first sign,” it says, “Jesus did in Cana of Galilee”), not before the baptism,

**51,21,2** as I have often said, but when he had returned from the temptation, on the third day after the two which the two disciples of John, who had heard and followed him, stayed with him;

**51,21,3** as, immediately after the two days which they spent with him, it adds, saying, “And he went out into Galilee, and finding Philip he said, ‘Follow me.’”

**51,21,4a** Then immediately, on the third day, a marriage took place at Cana in Galilee.

**51,21,4b** For it was fitting that, having gone away, he should at once, when a wedding was being celebrated, be invited in its honour, for the blessing of the marriage.

**51,21,5** And it says, “On the third day a marriage took place at Cana in Galilee, and Jesus’ mother was there, and Jesus too was invited, and his disciples, those with him, to the marriage”;

**51,21,6a** “and when the wine,” it says, “had run short, the mother of Jesus says, ‘They have no wine.’

**51,21,6b** And Jesus says to her, ‘What have I to do with you, woman?

**51,21,6c** My hour has not yet come.’”

**51,21,7** [...] after he had come from the wilderness, in the sequence of the temptation, and had been taken along to Jerusalem and stood upon the temple’s pinnacle, and from Jerusalem had been carried up to a very high mountain, which is said by many to be Mount Tabor, which is translated Itabyrium; and this is in Galilee.

**51,21,8** For Matthew set down this sequence, who says, “Now when Jesus heard that John had been delivered up, he came into Galilee.”

**51,21,9a** And Luke, having also made known exactly the setting out from the mountain, first speaking of the mountain and of the kingdoms which the devil showed the Lord, afterwards speaks of the pinnacle and Jerusalem, and how he returned to Galilee and Nazareth.

**51,21,9b** And in agreement Matthew too says, “Leaving Nazareth he came to Capernaum.”

**51,21,10** For he went to Nazareth, and thence to the Jordan, to John, and after passing through the Jordan he again set out for his own home, where he had been brought up, to his mother in Nazareth, and having stayed there two days, when Andrew and his companions also stayed with him, he then pressed on to begin the preaching for the sake of the salvation of men;

**51,21,11** and he stayed two days because he had come after an interval, having with him the disciples whom he had already taken along, and, sending away the pair who had followed him, he immediately departed for Galilee, because of the preaching and because of the work of the divine sign accomplished at the wedding.

**51,21,12** For see how the word makes [us] secure, how John the Baptist bears witness, speaking as of a deed already past: “And I myself did not know him; but the one who sent me to baptize told me, ‘The one upon whom you see the Spirit coming down in a dove’s form — he it is.’”

**51,21,13** For in sending John to baptize, the Father bestowed this sign on him, so that, on seeing it, he might recognise the Saviour sent from above to the world, and the benefactor of our souls.

**51,21,14a** Such heretics, then, are overthrown by the truth of the divine Scriptures and by their exactness, and above all by the agreement of the four gospels.

**51,21,14b** For no one of sound mind would reject what has been exactly treated in every respect by the Holy Spirit through the divine gospels.

**51,21,15** For even if they say that the evangelists Matthew and Mark and Luke narrated of the Saviour that after the baptism he was led up into the wilderness and spent forty days being tempted, and after the temptation, having heard that John had been delivered up, came and settled in Capernaum by the sea, but that John lies in not having spoken of these things,

**51,21,16** but straight away from the first, when the Saviour came to John, and the other things which he says he did — they will be shown to have been ignorant in every respect of the exactness of the gospels.

**51,21,17a** For John the evangelist indicates that before John the Baptist was delivered up the Lord, after the days of the temptation, had come [...] to him.

**51,21,17b** For how, once John had been delivered up, was it still possible for the Saviour to come to him again at the Jordan?

**51,21,18a** But they did not know that the other three evangelists narrate the time after John was delivered up, saying exactly, “Now when Jesus heard that John had been delivered up, leaving Nazareth he settled in Capernaum by the sea.”

**51,21,18b** And you see that in truth all things have been said in agreement by the four evangelists.

**51,21,19** For indeed John too is seen narrating again [in] sequence that after the Saviour had performed the first sign and come to Capernaum, and had done some signs there, and gone up to Nazareth and read the book, then, when the Baptist had been delivered up, he came and stayed in Capernaum [not] many days.

**51,21,20** And these are the days after the day of the Epiphanies, and after his coming to Capernaum and to Nazareth, and his going up to Jerusalem for the Passover, and [the] coming again to John where he was baptizing at Aenon [near] Salem.

**51,21,21** For it states thus: “After this he went down to Capernaum, together with his mother and his brothers, and there they remained not many days”; (for he was not yet indicating the permanent residence, of which he spoke later, [that] after John had been delivered up he came and settled in Capernaum by the sea.)

**51,21,22** “And the Jews’ Passover was close (as it says), and Jesus made his way up to Jerusalem and came upon those in the temple selling the oxen and the sheep and [the] doves, and the money-changers seated there.”

**51,21,23** And after he had driven out these same money-changers and those selling the doves, and the rest, and had said, “Take these things away from here, and do not make my Father’s house a house of trade,” and had heard from them, “What sign do you show us, seeing that you do these things?”, and had said to them, “Undo this sanctuary, and within three days I raise it up,” then Nicodemus came to him.

**51,21,24a** And after saying many things it says, “Jesus came, and his disciples, into the land of Judaea, and there he stayed with them and baptized.

**51,21,24b** And John too was [baptizing] at Aenon, close to Salem, because there was much water there; for John had not yet been cast into prison.”

**51,21,25** And when John had said many things — that “he who has the bride is the bridegroom” — the gospel then states, “So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples [than] John (though Jesus himself was not baptizing, but his disciples), he left the land of Judaea and went off again into Galilee.

**51,21,26** And he had to pass through Samaria”; when he had seated himself at the well and conversed with the Samaritan woman, and she brought word of him to those in the city, and the Samaritans came to him and urged him to remain with them, “and he remained there for two days, and far more came to believe on account of his word.”

**51,21,27a** “And when the two days were over he went to Galilee,” “and there was a man in the king’s service whose son lay sick at Capernaum,” when Jesus said to him, “Go; your son lives,” and the man believed, and the child was cured.

**51,21,27b** And the gospel says, “[This] again was the second sign that Jesus did, having come from Judaea into Galilee.”

**51,21,28** “After this came a festival of the Jews (but I take it to refer to some other Jewish festival, either Pentecost or that of the Tabernacles), and Jesus made his way up to Jerusalem,” when, coming to the sheep pool on a sabbath, he healed the man who had been paralysed in sickness for thirty-eight years.

**51,21,29** From this point, then, when the acceptable year had been fulfilled, they set about persecuting him, from the moment he healed the paralytic at the pool on a sabbath; then again it says, “The Jews were persecuting Jesus all the more, since he was not only breaking the sabbath but also calling God his own Father, making himself equal to God.”

**51,21,30a** How then are the heresies not to be condemned, which make the Son deficient in relation to the Father?

**51,21,30b** For “making himself equal,” says the gospel, “to God.”

**51,21,31a** “After these things Jesus went away to the other side of the sea of Galilee, of Tiberias, and a great crowd followed him, seeing the signs which he did on those who were sick.

**51,21,31b** So Jesus went up into the mountain and sat there with his disciples.

**51,21,31c** And the Passover, the Jews’ festival, was [near].”

**51,21,32a** Then, just as the other gospels report, once John had been handed over he came and settled in Capernaum by the sea; as John himself too is found saying, in agreement with the others.

**51,21,32b** For since the Passover falls in March or in April, it is shown on every count that the occasions on which the Saviour comes to John after the temptation are different ones.

**51,22,1** And the same people again accuse the holy evangelist — or rather the gospel itself — on the ground, they say, that John stated that the Saviour kept two Passovers within a span of two years, while the other evangelists tell of one Passover.

**51,22,2** And the ignorant are unaware that it is not merely two Passovers that the gospels acknowledge, as we have shown on every side, but they speak of two earlier ones, and the very one in which the Saviour suffered is another Passover, so that from the time of the baptism and the start of the preaching there come to be three Passovers, over three years, up to the cross.

**51,22,3** For it is in the forty-second year of Augustus, emperor of the Romans, that the Saviour is born, in the consulship of that same Octavius Augustus for the thirteenth time and of Silanus, as the consular lists of the Romans record.

**51,22,4** For it is entered in them thus: “In the consulship of these — I mean Octavius for the thirteenth time and Silanus — Christ was born on the eighth day before the Ides of January, thirteen days following the winter solstice and the addition to light and day.”

**51,22,5** And this day the Greeks — I mean the idolaters — celebrate on the eighth day before the Kalends of January, which among the Romans is called the Saturnalia, among the Egyptians the Kronia, and among the Alexandrians the Kikellia.

**51,22,6** For on the eighth day before the Kalends of January this division takes place, which is the solstice, and the day begins to grow as the light receives its increase, and it completes the number of thirteen days up to the eighth day before the Ides of January, until the day of Christ’s birth, a thirtieth of an hour being added to each day;

**51,22,7** as the wise Ephraim among the Syrians also bore witness to this account in his commentaries, saying, “For thus was the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ dispensed — his birth according to the flesh, that is, his perfect incarnation, which is called the Epiphany — at a distance of thirteen days from the start of the light’s increase; for it was needful that this too should become a figure of the count made up of our Lord Jesus Christ himself and his twelve disciples, since he completed [the] number of the light’s thirteen days of increase.”

**51,22,8a** And how many other things have taken place, and are taking place, to establish this account and to bear witness to it — I mean, to the birth of Christ!

**51,22,8b** For indeed the leaders of the cult of the idols, being compelled to acknowledge some part of the truth, and being deceivers, in order to deceive the idolaters who have trusted them, keep a very great festival in many places on the very night of the Epiphany, so that those who have set their hope on error may not seek the truth.

**51,22,9** First, in Alexandria, in what is [so] called the Koreion; and it is a very great temple, that is, the precinct of Kore.

**51,22,10a** For having kept vigil the whole night, singing to the idol with certain songs and flutes, and having completed an all-night service, after cockcrow they go down, bearing torches, into an underground shrine and bring up a wooden image seated naked [on] a litter, having on its forehead a seal of a cross inlaid with gold, and on its two hands two more such seals, and on its two knees two others, five seals in all, stamped in gold; and they carry the image itself around, circling the innermost temple seven times with flutes and drums and hymns, and after their revelling they carry it back down into the underground place.

**51,22,10b** And when they are asked what this mystery is, they answer and say that at this hour today Kore (that is, the virgin) has given birth to the Aeon.

**51,22,11a** And this also takes place thus in the city of Petra (it is the metropolis of Arabia, which is the Edom written of in the Scriptures), in the idol-temple there, and they hymn the virgin in the Arabic tongue, calling her in Arabic Chaamou, that is, Kore, or virgin, and the one born of her Dusares, that is, the only-begotten of the master.

**51,22,11b** And this takes place in the city of Elusa too on that night, just as there in Petra and in Alexandria.

**51,22,12** From many things we have been compelled to show, because of the disobedient, that the birth of the Saviour in the flesh which took place in Bethlehem is rightly called the Epiphanies, since he was born at the eighth hour and was manifested to the shepherds and to the world through the testimony of the angels; and he was manifested also to Mary and to Joseph.

**51,22,13** And at that hour the star too appeared in the east to the magi, two years before their arrival in Jerusalem and Bethlehem, when Herod also inquired exactly [of] the magi themselves the time of the star that had appeared, and heard from them that it was from two years old and under; and it was this very expression that supplied the name of the Epiphanies, since Herod spoke “of the star that appeared.”

**51,22,14a** Therefore, when they said, “Where is the king of the Jews who has been born?

**51,22,14b** For we saw his star in the east, and we have come to do him homage,” Herod considered the matter as not concerning the mere name of a human king.

**51,22,15** For having considered the matter, and wondering that many kings had been born in Jerusalem — first Saul of the tribe of Benjamin, second David of the tribe of Judah, and his son Solomon, and Solomon’s son Rehoboam, and his sons in succession — and that never at the birth of any one of these had a star appeared, nor had there been a coming of magi to come and worship the king who was born, except then only — therefore, having reflected on this, he aimed his mind at the knowledge of the truth, learning that this sign pertains not to any man, but only to the Lord,

**51,22,16a** and therefore he no longer asked about an earthly or human king, but about Christ, saying to the scribes and priests, “Where is the Christ born?”

**51,22,16b** And he heard from them, “In Bethlehem of Judaea”; and by inquiring of these he learned the place, and of the magi the time.

**51,22,17a** For indeed the magi themselves came to Bethlehem after two years, on the selfsame day of the Epiphanies, and presented their gifts — the myrrh and the gold and the frankincense.

**51,22,17b** For on the very day of the Epiphanies the beginnings of many signs of the manifestation took place.

**51,22,18a** And this was the day, as I said above and am often compelled to say, in the consulship of Octavius Augustus for the thirteenth time and of Silanus, on the eighth day before the Ides of January, thirteen days from the growth of light and day — a growth that runs from the winter solstice, that is, from the eighth day before the Kalends of January, until the very day of the nativity and the Epiphanies, because of the figure already made plain, the Saviour himself and his twelve disciples making up the number thirteen.

**51,22,18b** It is in the forty-second year, then, of Augustus, emperor of the Romans, that the Saviour is born, in the consulship

**51,22,19** recorded above, twenty-nine years after the union of Augustus with the Jews; and Augustus reigns thirteen years before Judaea was completely joined to the Romans.

**51,22,20** And from the time Augustus began to reign, for four years of his reign, more or less, there was friendship of the Romans towards the Jews, and an alliance was sent, and a procurator appointed, and portions of tribute paid to the Romans [...] for five years, more or less, until Judaea was completely handed over and became tributary to them.

**51,22,21** When the rulers from Judah had ceased and Herod, from the Gentiles — a proselyte, to be sure — had been appointed, and then Christ had been born at Bethlehem in Judaea and had come to the preaching, the anointed leaders from Judah and Aaron having ceased, who had lasted until Alexander, an anointed leader, and Salina, also called Alexandra — in their time the prophecy of Jacob was fulfilled, “A ruler shall not fail from Judah, nor a leader from his thighs, until he comes for whom it is laid up, and he it is whom the nations await,” which is the Lord who was born.

**51,22,22** All these things were being brought to completion, beginning from the birth in Bethlehem, which took place in the forty-second year of Augustus’ entire reign, which was after the fifth year of the procuratorship of Antipater, the father of Herod, when there was friendship of the Romans towards the Jews and portions of gifts, and after the procuratorship of Antipater from the sixth year of Augustus to his ninth, Herod having been appointed from the tenth year and a portion of tribute being paid until the thirteenth year, the fourth year of the reign of Herod, appointed by Augustus;

**51,22,23** and after the fourth year of Herod, Judaea having then been completely handed over, until the thirty-third year of Herod, when Augustus had completed forty-two years, [and] all Judaea had been subdued, as I said, after it had been made to pay tribute to the Romans for twenty-nine years, and after Antipater, Herod’s father, had been appointed procurator, and after Herod had been appointed king over Judaea by Augustus in the tenth year of the same Augustus.

**51,22,24a** 1. And these things took place in the consulship of Octavius Augustus for the thirteenth time and of Silanus the consul, as I have often said, which consulship the consulships set out below succeeded in order, thus:

**51,22,24b** 2. Lentulus and Piso.

**51,22,24c** 3. Lucius Caesar and Paulus.

**51,22,24d** 4. Vindicius and Varus.

**51,22,24e** 5. Lamia and Servilius Nonius.

**51,22,24f** 6. Magnus Pompeius and Valerius.

**51,22,24g** 7. Lepidus and Arruntius.

**51,22,24h** 8. Caesar and Capito.

**51,22,24i** 9. Creticus and Nerva.

**51,22,24j** 10. Camillus and Quintillianus.

**51,22,24k** 11. Camerus and Sabinus.

**51,22,24l** 12. Dolabella and Silanus.

**51,22,24m** 13. Lepidus and Taurus.

**51,22,24n** 14. Flancus and Silanus.

**51,22,24o** 15. The two Sexti.

**51,22,24p** 16. Pompeius Magnus and Apuleius.

**51,22,24q** 17. Brutus and Flancus.

**51,22,24r** 18. Taurus and Libo.

**51,22,24s** 19. Crassus and Rufus.

**51,22,24t** 20. Tiberius Caesar for a second time and Drusus Germanicus for a second time.

**51,22,24u** 21. Silanus and Balbus.

**51,22,24v** 22. Messala and Gratus.

**51,22,24w** 23. Tiberius Caesar for a third time and Drusus Germanicus for a third time.

**51,22,24x** 24. Agrippa and Galba.

**51,22,24y** 25. Pollio and Veter.

**51,22,24z** 26. Cethegus and Varus.

**51,22,24aa** 27. Agrippa for a second time, and Lentulus Galba.

**51,22,24ab** 28. Gaetulicus and Sabinus.

**51,22,24ac** 29. Crassus and Piso.

**51,22,24ad** 30. Silanus and Nerva.

**51,23,1a** And you see that it is a period of thirty years.

**51,23,1b** Therefore we have taken pains to set out the consulships exactly in their order, so that those who go through them may see that nothing has been falsified in the divine teaching of the truth, but that all things have been proclaimed by the church with exactness.

**51,23,2** For who, having counted the consulships in their sequence, which cannot err, will not condemn those who suppose that there is a disagreement in the number of the years celebrated by the evangelists?

**51,23,3** For this reason too the earlier heresies, those of Valentinus and of certain others, fell, which fabulously recorded the thirty aeons, which they thought to liken to the years of the Saviour, so that, forsooth, they might be able to record the mythmaking of the aeons and principalities they have.

**51,23,4** For the Only-begotten, God the Word from above, who is impassible, is found suffering in the thirty-third year of his coming in the flesh, [having taken] flesh [and] having undertaken to suffer for us, so that he might cancel the bond of death that stood against us.

**51,23,5** For after that consulship, which is indicated as falling in his thirtieth year, there was another consulship, called that of the two Gemini; then another consulship, that of Rufus and Rubellio; and thus, while the consulship following that of [Rufus and] Rubellio was in force — afterwards it came to be known as that of Vinnicius and Longinus Cassius — [in his thirty-third year, being] Tiberius Caesar’s eighteenth, the Saviour suffers on the thirteenth day before the Kalends of April.

**51,23,6** And the error of all these has been refuted, since the teaching according to the truth is clearly found, that not only two years’ cycles of the feast of the Passover are contained in the gospels, but three as well.

**51,24,1** For his birth having taken place in January, namely on the eighth day before the Ides of January — which according to the Romans is the evening of the fifth of January dawning into the sixth; according to the Egyptians the eleventh of Tybi; according to the Syrians, or Greeks, the sixth of Audynaeus; according to the Cypriots, or Salaminians, the fifth day of the fifth month; according to the Paphians the fourteenth of Julius; according to the Arabs the twenty-first of Aleom; according to the Cappadocians the thirteenth of Atarta; according to the Athenians the fifth of Maimakterion; according to the Hebrews the fifth of Tebeth;

**51,24,2** for here too what was spoken by the prophet had to be fulfilled, “The ark of God came in to us” (he means the perfect incarnation of Christ) “on the fifth of the fifth month,” as it was then accomplished among the Hebrews; which had to be fulfilled first by the

**51,24,3** reckoning of the Hebrews, which many of the Gentiles too have followed — I mean the Romans — who celebrate it on the fifth, in the evening, dawning into the sixth, while the Cypriots keep the very fifth day of the month, and the Egyptians by nature and the Salaminians keep the month itself as the fifth, just as the Hebrews also rank it fifth from the new year kept among them —

**51,24,4** he passed through, then, the twenty-nine consulships mentioned before, full, and in the thirtieth consulship — I mean [...] — around the [elev]enth month he came to John and received baptism in the Jordan river in the thirtieth year of his fleshly birth, six days before the Ides of November,

**51,24,5** that is, according to the Egyptians the twelfth of Athyr; according to the Greeks the eighth of Dius; according to the Salaminians, who are also the Constantians, the sixth of the third month, Choiak; according to the Paphians the sixteenth of Apogonicus; according to the Arabs the twenty-second of Angathalbaeith; according to the Macedonians the sixteenth of Apellaeus; according to the Cappadocians the fifteenth of Arateta; according to the Athenians the seventh of Metageitnion; according to the Hebrews the seventh of Marheshvan,

**51,24,6** as the holy Gospel according to Luke bears witness to my account, as I have often said, speaking in some such way as this: “Now Jesus was beginning to be some thirty years old, and was the son, as people supposed, of Joseph”;

**51,24,7** from this point, from the twelfth of Athyr, “as he was proclaiming the Lord’s acceptable year,” in keeping with what was said beforehand in Isaiah the prophet, “The Lord’s Spirit is on me, for which reason he anointed me to bring good tidings to the poor; he has sent me to herald release for prisoners and sight again for the blind, to herald a year of the Lord that is acceptable, and a day of requital.”

**51,25,1a** For in truth he proclaimed a year of the Lord that was acceptable, that is, not spoken against.

**51,25,1b** For during the first year following [the] thirtieth year of his coming in the flesh he preached, and all welcomed him, and neither Jews nor Gentiles nor Samaritans spoke against him, but all heard him gladly.

**51,25,2** It is in this year that he goes up to Jerusalem, after his baptism and his passing through the temptation’s forty days, and the twenty that we spoke of earlier, up to the first sign, and after the choosing of his own disciples;

**51,25,3** having returned from the temptation to the Jordan and crossed over the sea of Tiberias, going on to Nazareth he evidently went up to Jerusalem, and in the middle of the feast he cried out, saying, “If anyone is thirsty, let him come to me and let him drink,” and thus he departed to Nazareth, and into Judaea, and into Samaria, and into the region of Tyre.

**51,25,4** And when the first year was completed he goes up again to Jerusalem, and from then on they were seeking to seize him at the feast, and they were afraid — the feast at which he said, “I am not going up to this feast.”

**51,25,5a** For he was not lying — perish the thought!

**51,25,5b** “For when the feast was half over he came,” it says, “and went up to Jerusalem.”

**51,25,5c** “And they were saying, ‘Is this not the one whom they were seeking to seize?

**51,25,5d** And look, he speaks openly; can it be that the priests have learned that this is the Christ?

**51,25,5e** But we know where this man is from.’”

**51,25,6a** For since he was speaking to his brothers in a mysterious and spiritual way, they did not know what he was saying.

**51,25,6b** For he was telling them that he was not going up into heaven at that feast, nor to the cross, so as to complete at that time the dispensation of his passion and the mystery of salvation, and to rise from the dead and go up into heaven — all of which he was fulfilling by his own authority.

**51,25,7** And after this, when the two-year period after the baptism and his birth had been completed, from the month of November and from the month of January and beyond, then in the thirty-third year of his sojourn in the flesh, after he had passed the two consulships which we mentioned, that of the two Gemini and that of Rufus and Rubellio, then in the third consulship,

**51,25,8** in its third month, after January and February, in the month of March, the impassible God the Word, suffering in the flesh for our sake yet remaining in impassibility, completes the mystery of the passion, as Peter says, “put to death in the flesh, but made alive in the spirit.”

**51,26,1** And he suffers on the thirteenth day before the Kalends of April [...], since they had overstepped one [...] evening, that is, at the night-time midpoint of the moon’s fourteenth day.

**51,26,2** For they were ahead of time and ate the Passover — so the gospel tells us, and we have said it often; so they ate the Passover two days before the [...] eating, that is, on Tuesday evening, which ought to have been done on Thursday evening; for on this reckoning Thursday was the fourteenth.

**51,26,3** And he is arrested late on that same Tuesday, which was by night the eleventh of the moon, the sixteenth day before the Kalends of April; at the dawning of Wednesday, by night the twelfth, the fifteenth day before the Kalends of April; Thursday, by day the thirteenth but by night the fourteenth, the fourteenth day before the Kalends of April; the day before the Sabbath, by day the fourteenth, the thirteenth day before the Kalends of April; the Sabbath, by day the fifteenth, the twelfth day before the Kalends of April; at the dawning of the Lord’s day, by night the fifteenth —

**51,26,4** which was an illumination of Hades and of earth and of heaven, and [an equality] of night and day, because of the fifteenth of the moon and the course of the sun; for both the resurrection and the equinox [took place] on the eleventh day before the Kalends of April, and in this matter [the Jews] went astray and made a leap of one day, as I said before.

**51,26,5** Now the reckoning of their minute calculation has certain hours [...], which comes round every three years and, among them, differs by one day,

**51,26,6** for to the course of the moon, after the three hundred and fifty-four days, they add another four hours each year, so that in three years there is one day.

**51,26,7** This is why among them five intercalary months are completed in fourteen years, because from the course of the sun, of three hundred and sixty-five days and three hours, the [...] one hour is taken away; for when the hours are added, there then come to be 365 days [...] less one hour,

**51,26,8** whence these men, multiplying the 14 years six times, through the 84th year set one intercalary month in the eighty-fifth, so that there are thirty-one months in the eighty-five years, which by exact reckoning ought to be thirty-one months and 21 days and 3 hours.

**51,27,1** Because of this, then, having gone wrong at that time, they not only got ahead by the two days in their confusion, having eaten the Passover, but also added the one overstepped day [...], going wrong themselves in every way; but the dispensation of the truth worked everything out for us most exactly, for our salvation.

**51,27,2** Hence the Saviour himself also, having completed the Passover, went out to the mountain after he had eaten it, “having desired with desire.”

**51,27,3** That Jewish Passover, too, he ate with the disciples, doing nothing different but acting himself in the same way as those who were keeping it, so as not to abolish the law but to fulfil it.

**51,27,4** And so, after he had passed the thirtieth year, when he was baptised, and after the thirty-first, having preached a full year that was acceptable and not opposed, and having preached another year that was opposed and under persecution and met with resentment, and after this having passed another year from the day of his birth, that is, of the Epiphany,

**51,27,5** which falls on the fifth of the month of January as it dawns into the sixth, and according to the Egyptians on the eleventh of Tybi, for seventy-four whole days, as we said before, up to the thirteenth day before the Kalends of April [...], and on the twenty-fourth of Phamenoth according to the Egyptians he completed thirty-two full years and seventy-four days from the Epiphany.

**51,27,6** And having risen on the twenty-sixth of Phamenoth according to the Egyptians, which was the equinox when the night had passed, and the equal night after the twenty-fifth, and the eleventh day before the Kalends of April [...], so that all may be found to be lying who are not sons of the truth.

**51,28,1** At the very outset Valentinus first [...], having staged his drama and supposing thirty aeons for us from the thirty years [...] of the Saviour’s upbringing and of his coming to manhood in stature, not knowing that he did not spend thirty years only,

**51,28,2** but was baptised in the thirtieth year, when he had completed twenty-nine years and ten months — on Athyr the twelfth, as I said before, the sixth day before the Ides of November — and after this preached [...] an acceptable year of the Lord, and after the [first] year another year that was opposed, and seventy-four days that were opposed, ever since he was baptised [sixty days] before his birthday,

**51,28,3a** so that all the years of his presence in the flesh up to the passion are, from his birth, 32 years and 74 days, and from the beginning [...] of the consulship of Silanus and Nerva, two years and 134 days.

**51,28,3b** And here the aforementioned Valentinus has been refuted, and the many who rave in his fashion.

**51,28,4** And refuted too are those who reject the Gospel according to John, whom I may rightly call Alogi, since they reject the Word of God, God the Word of the Father, proclaimed through John, who descended from heaven and achieved salvation for us [through] his whole presence in the flesh,

**51,28,5** since from the consulships, and from the years, and from the testimony of the prophet Isaiah, and from the Gospel according to Luke, and from that according to John, and from that according to Matthew, and from that according to Mark, and in a word from every side, the misguided have been refuted,

**51,28,6** in that he not only kept one Passover after the beginning of the preaching, within the space of a year’s time, but also completed the periods of three consulships, less a little, after the baptism by John.

**51,28,7** And the argument of the inconsequent has collapsed, being wholly of error [...] and folly and ignorance, which not only does not know its own life but also falsely and vainly takes the field against the truth.

**51,29,1** For we have also found it written somewhere, circulating [in] these writings, that the Word who is from God was born around Augustus’ fortieth year — a point that escaped the writer, or else, once the numeral expressed by beta was rubbed out and the mu alone was left, it made the years just 40; for it was in the forty-second year of Augustus that he was born.

**51,29,2** And he asserts that [he was conceived] on the twelfth day before the Kalends of July — or of June, I am unable to say — in the consulship of Sulpicius Cammarinus, Betteus Pompeianus being consuls,

**51,29,3** [and] this too I have considered: that those who stated the day of the conception, and when Gabriel brought the good news to the virgin, said [this because of] the supposition of some who say in a tradition that he was born at seven months.

**51,29,4** For we have found, from this date up to the eleventh of Tybi and the eighth day before the Ides of January, when the Theophany truly took place and he was born, a period of seven months by the course of the moon, less four days.

**51,29,5a** So then, if you should find [these things] written somewhere in marginal notes, do not be led astray in your knowledge.

**51,29,5b** For in reality the sure birth of Christ is on the eleventh of Tybi.

**51,29,6** But some say that he was carried in the womb ten months less 14 days and eight hours, so that it is nine months and fifteen days and four hours, alluding to what is said in Solomon, “compacted in blood in a period of ten months.”

**51,29,7a** Nevertheless, from every quarter [it has been shown that] it was on the eleventh of Tybi, according to the Egyptians, that the aforementioned birth of the Lord in the flesh occurred.

**51,29,7b** And about that same eleventh day, thirty years later, the first sign took place in Cana of Galilee, when the water became wine.

**51,30,1** This is why in many places, even to this day, this happens because of the divine sign that took place then, as a testimony for the unbelieving, as springs and rivers in many places that are changed into wine bear witness —

**51,30,2** the spring of Cibyra, the city of Caria, at the hour at which the servants drew and he said, “Give it to the master of the feast”; and the spring in Gerasa of Arabia likewise bears witness; [for we] ourselves have drunk from the spring of Cibyra, and our brothers from the spring in Gerasa that is in the martyrium.

**51,30,3a** And many in Egypt too bear witness to this concerning the Nile.

**51,30,3b** This is why on the eleventh of Tybi according to the Egyptians all draw water and store it, in Egypt itself and in many lands.

**51,30,4** For on this reckoning it turns out that, after Athyr the twelfth, once he had departed and undergone temptation for forty days, and had come to Nazareth and remained about two weeks and three days, he went down to the Jordan to John and spent there a first and a second day, and returned to Nazareth and stayed likewise a first and a second day, and went out on the third to Cana of Galilee,

**51,30,5** so that the days from the baptism add up to 60 in all — forty of the temptation, and the two weeks at Nazareth [and two days], and the further two — and that on the third the sign of the water took place at the wedding.

**51,30,6a** Afterwards he came to Capernaum and did other works, as we have often said, and went up once more to Nazareth and read the book of Isaiah the prophet.

**51,30,6b** That is why they say, “Do here also such signs as we have heard that you did in Capernaum” —

**51,30,7** and later, having come again to Capernaum, he crossed from there to the lake — or sea — of Gennesaret, [and] Peter and those with him were chosen for good, and so from there he carried through the whole completion of the preaching.

**51,30,8** For according to the sequence, as we have said, after the 40 [days] and the further two weeks and two days [at Nazareth], he was with John for a first day and the next; and when he had turned back [from] him to Nazareth and remained there from the tenth hour till evening, and had gone out on the next day and met Philip — which makes two days —

**51,30,9** then the gospel shows the unalterable character of its exactness because of the first two days, which he stayed after coming, [saying,] “On the third day a wedding took place in Cana of Galilee,” which was a symbol of the church,

**51,30,10** for on the third day of his work within the heart of the earth, a work he carried out in the earth after the passion, he arose and entered into marriage with Cana; for Cana is interpreted as ‘she who has acquired.’

**51,30,11a** And who is she who has acquired, but the heiress, of whom the psalmist said in the fifth psalm, “For her who inherits,” and what follows?

**51,30,11b** For this is truly a blessed marriage, having taken its occasion from that type.

**51,30,12** For there was a wedding there, a perceptible one, in Cana of Galilee, and water truly become wine; and he was invited for two reasons: that [through] marriage he might draw together into self-control and gravity the watery slackness of those in the world who revel wildly, and that he might make good what was lacking, for gladness, [through] the wine that relieves toil, and through grace,

**51,30,13a** so that in every way he might stop the mouths of those who rise up against the Lord, and might show that he himself is God, together with the Father and his Holy Spirit, supplying the water to the vine [and] colouring it within the vine into wine for the gladness of men.

**51,30,13b** But in another place we have related these things at greater length; here I have run through the account as in a digression.

**51,30,14a** Nevertheless, the Saviour keeps two Passovers after the beginning of the preaching, and at the third he suffers, so that what I have already stated in detail about days and months and consulships is fulfilled.

**51,30,14b** And on every side the misguided argument of these people has fallen to pieces, since the gospels are in harmony with one another and neither evangelist holds anything contrary to the other.

**51,31,1a** But I will come back again to the matter before us.

**51,31,1b** Luke again says, bearing witness to what I have said in many parts and in many ways, that “it came to pass on the second-first sabbath,” in order to show that the first sabbath is the one appointed from the beginning and spoken of by the Lord at the making of the world, coming round in its cycle every seven days from then until now, and the second sabbath the one appointed by the law.

**51,31,2** For it says, “You shall take for yourself a sheep a year old, without blemish, male (which was the antitype of the Saviour), from the tenth of the month, and it is to be kept until the fourteenth, and on the fourteenth day, in the evening, you shall sacrifice it; and it shall be a sabbath for you, a holy day, and for seven days you shall eat unleavened bread, and the seventh day you shall make holy.”

**51,31,3** And you see that such a holy day of the sheep is termed a second sabbath, coming after the first sabbath, being hallowed as a sabbath, whether it be the Lord’s day or whether it fall on the second or the third day of the week.

**51,31,4** And as it comes round again within seven days, the second is called first, so as to show from this that not only John indicated a period of time of two years and three feasts of the Passover, but also Luke and the others.

**51,31,5** For thus the law says, “You shall number for yourself seven weeks from the beginning of the sheaf, from putting the sickle to the harvest, and you shall make the seventh week a holy day of the Lord,” indicating Pentecost.

**51,31,6** For after the sacrifice of the Passover, within three days, that is, three days after the sheep, it commanded the sheaf to be brought in, so as to signify the blessed sheaf rising from among the dead after the third day, the earth bringing it forth

**51,31,7** and he in turn recovering it from the earth at the resurrection [from] the tomb, and remaining with the disciples the forty days, and at the completion of Pentecost bringing it into the heavenly places to the Father —

**51,31,8** the firstborn of the firstborn, the firstfruits that are holy, the sheaf which he grasped from Mary, the armful clasped in an embrace in God, the womb’s fruit, the firstfruits of the threshing floor.

**51,31,9** For the sickle brings firstfruits to God no longer after Pentecost: “For the Lord no longer dies; death no longer has dominion over him,” as it is written.

**51,31,10a** And you see how many mysteries of God the law prefigured and the gospel fulfilled.

**51,31,10b** And from what source are we not able to show the clearness of these things?

**51,31,10c** But so as not to draw the discussion out at length, we must turn back again to the sequence.

**51,31,11** And it is evident from the ears of grain, and from the harvest, and from the disciples, that John and Luke and all of them narrate all these things as coming after the temptation of the forty days.

**51,32,1** But again such people are not ashamed to arm themselves against what was said by the holy John, thinking that perhaps they may be able to overturn the truth, not knowing that they are arming themselves against themselves rather than against sound teaching.

**51,32,2** And they say these things against the Revelation, scoffing in this way: “What good to me,” he says, “is the Revelation of John, when it tells me of seven angels and seven trumpets?”

**51,32,3** Not knowing how necessary and beneficial such things have been for the correctness of the preaching.

**51,32,4** For whatever was dark and enigmatic in the law and in the prophets, these things the Lord arranged through the Holy Spirit, for our salvation, to reveal “to his servant John,” proclaiming here, as spiritual and plain, the things that were dark there, [...] and he was dispensing the same things to us spiritually.

**51,32,5** And at that time he makes the tabernacle of skins — skins dyed red, and of hyacinth blue, and the rest — so as to show that the tabernacle there was indeed a tabernacle, but one awaiting the most perfect tabernacle of Christ.

**51,32,6** For skin comes from a body, being dead, as a shadow of a living body, to show that the tabernacle of God is bodies; for since God dwells in holy bodies, the saying is fulfilled, “I will dwell in them and walk among them.”

**51,32,7** There was going to be some error, then, among the faithful, unless the book had been revealed to us spiritually, so that we might learn that there is no need of trumpets, but [...] know that the whole work of God is spiritual,

**51,32,8** so that we may not take it after the fashion of the Jewish trumpets of bronze or of silver, but may understand spiritually that the preaching of the church is from heaven, as he also said somewhere else, “In that day they will sound with the great trumpet.”

**51,32,9a** For the prophets were trumpets, and the great trumpet is the holy voice of the Lord in the gospel.

**51,32,9b** This is why it was given to angels too to reveal to us: “For the trumpet will sound,” it says, “and the dead will rise.”

**51,32,10** But if the trumpets of the angels are scoffed at among you, you people, because they are written in the Revelation, then the trumpet spoken of by the holy apostle is scoffed at as well: “For the Lord,” he says, “will come down from heaven at the last trumpet, and the dead will rise on the last day, at an archangel’s voice.”

**51,32,11a** Since Paul, then, is in accord with John the holy apostle in the Revelation, what sort of contradiction remains?

**51,32,11b** And how will each error not at once be refuted, since God has given testimony [on behalf] of the saints in each case?

**51,33,1a** Then some of them again seize upon this saying in the same Revelation and say in contradiction, “He said again, ‘Write to the angel of the church that is in Thyatira,’ and no church of Christians is there in Thyatira.

**51,33,1b** How then did he write to one that did not exist?”

**51,33,2a** And such people are found compelling themselves, out of the very things they proclaim, to make confession on behalf of the truth.

**51,33,2b** For should they say, “Now there is no church in Thyatira,” they show that John prophesied it beforehand.
