**62,8,3** For there was no need to say it, since there was no dispute; for from “God” and “light” the one perfection of the same authenticity, of the Father with the Son and of the Son with the Father, has been clearly declared;

**62,8,4a** and the whole derangement of your error has been dissolved.

**62,8,4b** For the Father is Father, the Son is Son, the Holy Spirit is Holy Spirit, being a Trinity, one Godhead, one glorification, one lordship, [one God], to whom be glory, honour and might, to the Father in the Son, to the Son in the Father, together with the Holy Spirit, for ever and ever. Amen.

**62,8,5** And having again shaken off this heresy, like a libys or a molurus or an elops or one of the most fearsome reptiles, which nevertheless cannot do harm with their bites, and having trampled it down by the power of the holy Trinity, as we go on again to the next ones let us call upon him as helper of our poverty and smallness, [so that] we may be helped worthily to [...] the things said and done among each one and to make the refutation of them.

### Sect 63: Origenists

Against the first Origenists, who are also the shameful ones, the forty-third, but the sixty-third in the series.

**63,1,1** Some are called Origenists, but this sect does not exist everywhere; and I think that this one too, which is now being described, [sprang up] next in order after these heresies.

**63,1,2a** And they are called Origenists, but we do not know at all clearly for what reason; whether they [received their] being from Origen the writer, who is called Adamantius, or from someone else, I do not know.

**63,1,2b** Nevertheless, this is the name that has come down to us.

**63,1,3a** And the heresy [that] is practised among them is, as it were, likened to that of Epiphanes, whom I described earlier, above, in the heresies relating to the Gnostics.

**63,1,3b** And these people read various Scriptures of the New and the Old Testament.

**63,1,3c** And they reject marriage, and lewdness does not cease among them.

**63,1,3d** And some have said that it arose around Rome and Africa.

**63,1,4a** And they defile their own body and mind and soul with licentiousness.

**63,1,4b** For the men are in the guise of monks, and the women who are with them in the guise of nuns.

**63,1,4c** And they are corrupted in their bodies, fulfilling their desire, but employing (to put it more decently) the practice of the son of Judah called Onan.

**63,1,5** For just as he was joined in body to Tamar and fulfilled his desire, but did not complete the act to the depositing of seed for the begetting of children granted by God, but wronged himself — in the way in which [he] did evil — so these too have relations with the women reckoned [...], performing this unlawful practice.

**63,1,6** For what is prized among them is not purity, but a hypocritical purity so called in name, and a zeal that goes only so far as that the woman corrupted by the one who is reputed [...] should not conceive, either so as not to bring the begetting of children into the world, or so that they may not be detected by men, since they wish to be held in honour on account of the discipline of purity reputed among them;

**63,1,7** this, however, is their practice; but others make it their ambition to accomplish this same abomination not through women, but in other ways as well, defiling themselves with their own hands.

**63,1,8** And likewise they imitate the aforementioned son of Judah, defiling the earth with their unlawful practices and foul drops, and rubbing their own emissions into the ground with their feet, so that, supposedly, their seed may not be snatched by unclean spirits to be received for the conceiving of demons.

**63,2,1a** And they use, as I said, various Scriptures of the Old and New Testament and certain apocrypha, above all what are called the Acts of Andrew and the rest.

**63,2,1b** And these very people have often boasted of doing this quite openly.

**63,2,2** And they accuse, forsooth, those in the church who keep the so-called beloved women, the women brought in to live with them, as though they too did this, [but] in secret, out of shame before men, so that they may [...] be joined to the wickedness, yet in outward show, for men’s sake, pride themselves on the name.

**63,2,3** And some people also told us about certain men who had died before, alleging that they too did this, as having heard it from the women who had been violated by them;

**63,2,4** among whom they also brought forward the name of a bishop, who had administered the office of bishop for a good many years in a small city of Palestine, and had had such women serving him — I mean women brought in to live with him; but we know that this very man was also one of the confessors.

**63,2,5** Nevertheless we did not believe those who said this and claimed that they had heard it from the women; for the pressure of the depravity of those who spoke prompted us now to believe, and now not to believe, the evil report about the aforementioned aged bishop after his death.

**63,2,6a** For the charge that reached him came about in some such way as this.

**63,2,6b** A certain man of this sort was detected in sin with a woman, and when he was being convicted by us, he put forward this defence: that the woman who had been corrupted along with him (although she was already advanced in years and of an aged time of life) had told him of the practice of this depravity, how he had taught him to have intercourse, but to scatter his foul emissions outside upon the ground.

**63,3,1** And this is the foul practice of these people, which deceives their mind and has been blinded by the devil.

**63,3,2** I have not thought it necessary to bring forward the testimonies of these things through which they fall, lest, through the very points on which we wish to be exact in order to turn people away from the unlawful practice of each heresy, we should seem rather to be urging on towards evil the unstable and those ever slack of mind, who desire to hunt out wickedness for themselves rather than goodness.

**63,3,3** Rather, to ward off this dreadful and reptilian heresy, I will set out a few things out of many.

**63,3,4a** Where did you get, you people, the notion of your unlawful practice?

**63,3,4b** For first of all, taking the whole matter together, to whom will it not be plain that your teaching is of demons, and that the evil contrivance that has been devised is of people deceived and corrupted in mind?

**63,3,5** For if conceiving is altogether evil, then it is not because of the begetting of children but because of being joined to a body; how then, overcome by pleasure, are you joined to a body?

**63,3,6a** And if being joined to a body is not evil, neither is it evil, once joined, to bring to completion what has come into being.

**63,3,6b** For either the man who has renounced [must] not till the things upon the earth — even as “Abel was a shepherd of sheep, while Cain worked the ground”.

**63,3,7** But if someone does till the earth, like Noah, who “became a husbandman and planted a vineyard”, he did not plant it so that it should not bear the cluster, but he planted, “and drank of its fruit and became drunk”, as it is written.

**63,3,8** And it was permitted to grant pardon to the old man; for, being well-pleasing to God, he did not undergo drunkenness through dissipation, but, carried along by grief, he had perhaps become stupefied, and, unable to bear it on both counts because of his weakness and old age, he fell into weakness — not so that he might be mocked by his son.

**63,3,9** But he who mocked received the curse upon himself, so that those who hurl insult at their fathers may be punished, and the reasonings within us that rise up against the knowledge of God and his well-ordered ordinance.

**63,4,1** For even if marriage has not been glorified as virginity has, and virginity stands above it (and virginity that is true is called glorious and virtuous, not virginity that is defiled), marriage too is honourable, [if one] directs the things well created by God towards the begetting of children, not towards shame, and does not otherwise handle the conduct of the fellowship of marriage appointed by God.

**63,4,2** For virginity is truly that which is praised by the holy apostle, “The virgin and the unmarried woman is anxious about the Lord’s affairs, how she will please the Lord, so as to be holy both in body and in soul”, to show that the single life does not admit of any charge of transgressions even being suspected.

**63,4,3** But we know [...] Abraham too begetting children, while he was a friend of the Lord, and Isaac and Jacob and those who followed in order; and they neither defiled themselves with unlawful deeds, touching foul things and [...], nor set themselves in opposition to the begetting of children through honourable marriage, well ordained by God,

**63,4,4** nor did those among them who practised purity and virginity corrupt the contest and alter it into something else, as though slipping into the virtuous manner of the contest by trickery.

**63,4,5** And Elijah did not readily enter cities at all, nor did he keep company with women, but he went on living in deserts; and so did Elisha and John, and all who [displayed] this great character of the imitation of the angels and rightly, according to the gospel ordinance of the Lord, made themselves eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven.

**63,4,6** And so, though we have much to say about these people and could refute their mind, which is mocked by the devil, from many proofs of the divine Scripture, we are content with these few words;

**63,4,7** for since it is plain to everyone that the practice among them is not a thing of understanding, nor is such knowledge from God, but the mockery and the fall of their unlawful practice come about [from] the working of demons.

**63,4,8** And thrusting this one aside too, like the dreadful club-snake called the viper, which is short in body but by its venom breathes out a dreadful breath and blows its destruction upon those who come near, having crushed it, let us go on to the next ones, calling upon God as helper for the fulfilment, in God, of the promise of our whole undertaking.

### Sect 64: Origen (Adamantius)

Against Origen, who is also called Adamantius, the forty-fourth, but the sixty-fourth in the series.

**64,1,1a** Origen, who was also surnamed Adamantius, follows these next in order.

**64,1,1b** He was the son of Leonides, the holy and blessed martyr; and he himself, having suffered persecution in very many ways in his youth, being a man learned in the education of the Greeks and brought up in the church, became known in the times of the emperor Decius in the city of the Alexandrians.

**64,1,2** For he was an Egyptian by race, but had his dwelling and upbringing in Alexandria, and perhaps he also attended the schools in Athens for some time.

**64,1,3** And he is said to have suffered much on behalf of the holy message of the faith and of Christ’s name, being for his part often dragged about in the city, reviled, and subjected to cruel tortures.

**64,1,4** For indeed at one time, as the story goes, the Greeks shaved him and, seating him at the steps of the so-called Serapeum, the precinct of their idol, bade him give out palm fronds to those going up to perform their unlawful rites and to worship the idol; for the priests of their idols wear such a guise.

**64,1,5a** But he took them and, with a loud voice and a bold mind, neither fearing nor hesitating, cried out, saying, “Come, take, not the branch of the idol, but the branch of Christ.”

**64,1,5b** And there are many brave deeds concerning him which the ancients relate according to tradition.

**64,2,1a** But the things of the prize did not remain with him to the end.

**64,2,1b** For because of the surpassing eminence of his learning and of his earlier education he was slandered out of very great envy, and this provoked all the more those who at that time held the governing authority.

**64,2,2** For with devilish malice the workers of wickedness contrived to bring shame upon the man and to fix such a punishment, having made ready an Ethiopian for him, to abuse his body.

**64,2,3** But he, unable to bear so great a scheme of the devil’s working, broke out in a cry, and when both courses had been set before him, chose rather to sacrifice.

**64,2,4** Yet he did not do this of his own free will, as the common account has it; but since he had in any way agreed to do it, they put incense into his hands and let it fall into the fire of the altar.

**64,2,5** And so he was then cast out from martyrdom by the confessors and martyrs who judged him, and was thrust out of the church.

**64,2,6** And having undergone this in Alexandria, and being unable to bear the mockery of those who reproached him, he withdrew and chose to dwell in the land of the Palestinians, that is, of Judaea.

**64,2,7** Having gone up, at any rate, to Jerusalem, as being so great an expositor and learned man, he was urged by the priesthood to speak in the church; for they say that he had also been counted worthy of the presbyterate earlier, before he sacrificed.

**64,2,8** So when, as I said, they were urging him to speak in the church, and those who at that time administered the priesthood of the holy church in Jerusalem pressed him hard, he rose to his feet and, uttering only the text of the forty-ninth psalm and passing over the whole exposition, as the psalm says,

**64,2,9a** “But to the sinner God said, ‘Why do you recount my ordinances and take up my covenant through your mouth?’”

**64,2,9b** Folding up the book, he handed it back and sat down with weeping and tears, while all wept together with him.

**64,3,1** Some time after this, when many were urging and pressing him, he met a certain Ambrose, one of those distinguished in the imperial courts (some said that this Ambrose was a Marcionist, others a Sabellian), and instructed him, at any rate, to turn away from the heresy, to anathematise it and to take up the faith of the holy church of God; for at that time Origen himself held the orthodox and catholic faith.

**64,3,2** And since the aforementioned Ambrose came from another heresy and, [as] a learned man and one earnest about the reading of the divine Scriptures, because of the depth of the thoughts in the divine books, he asked this same Origen to explain them to him.

**64,3,3** And he, wishing, for that man’s due and at his urging, to become the interpreter of all Scripture, so to speak, set himself to expound it, and in Tyre of Phoenicia, as the story goes, for twenty-eight years [...],

**64,3,4** with a surpassing manner of life and with application and toil [...], Ambrose supplying what was needed for food for him and for the swift scribes who served him, and paper and the rest of the expenses, and Origen carrying through the toil over the Scripture both in sleepless nights and in the most intense application.

**64,3,5** Hence, when he had first zealously striven to gather together the six translations — of Aquila, Symmachus, the Seventy-two and Theodotion,

**64,3,6** and of the fifth and the sixth edition — [he published the books], setting beside them each Hebrew word and the [Hebrew] letters themselves together; and in parallel, directly opposite, using a second column, he made yet another arrangement, of the Hebrew wording but in Greek letters; so that these are, and are called, the Hexapla,

**64,3,7** [and] that in addition to the Greek translations [there are] two parallel columns together, of the Hebrew in its own form through [Hebrew] letters and of the Hebrew through Greek letters, so that the whole Old Testament exists through what are called the Hexapla and through the two forms of the Hebrew words.

**64,3,8** All this undertaking together the man had zealously pursued with toil; but he did not carry his fame through to the end unquenched.

**64,3,9a** For his wide learning turned out for him to a great fall.

**64,3,9b** For out of that very aim, wishing to leave none of the divine Scriptures unexplained, he entangled himself in the inducement of sin and expounded deadly words; from him come those called Origenists — not

**64,3,10** the first ones, those of the shameful practice [...]; for I cannot say concerning them, as I have also said already, whether they took their origin from this Origen, who is also Adamantius, or had some other founder, [himself also]

**64,3,11a** called Origen.

**64,3,11b** And they say that this Origen too devised [something] for himself against his body.

**64,3,11c** [For] some say that he cut a nerve, so as not to be troubled by pleasure nor to be inflamed and set on fire in the motions of the body.

**64,3,12a** But others say not, but that he devised to apply some drug to the parts and to dry them up.

**64,3,12b** And others dare to attribute yet other things to him, such as that he also found some medicinal herb for the sake of memory.

**64,3,13** And we have not altogether believed the extravagant things said about him; nevertheless we have not omitted to tell what is said.

**64,4,1** And the heresy that sprang from him, existing first in the country of the Egyptians, is now [found] among the very most eminent and among those who are reputed to have taken up the solitary life, among those who in fact withdraw into the deserts and have chosen poverty; and this one too is dreadful, and more wicked than all the old ones, holding views like theirs together with them.

**64,4,2a** For even if it does not prepare those who are its disciples to commit shameful acts, yet at any rate it hurls against the Godhead itself a dreadful suspicion, more dreadful than shamefulness.

**64,4,2b** For from him Arius too took his pretexts, and those who came after, the Anomoeans and the others.

**64,4,3** For this man, having ventured, forsooth, to speak about the first principles, asserts in the first place that the Only-begotten Son is unable to see the Father, nor indeed can the Spirit behold the Son, nor yet angels the Spirit, nor men the angels.

**64,4,4a** And this is his first fall.

**64,4,4b** For he will not have the Son be of the essence of the Father, but introduces him as wholly alien to the Father, and created as well.

**64,4,4c** And he means to say that he is called Son as by grace.

**64,4,5a** And there are other, greater falls of his as well.

**64,4,5b** For he says that the human soul pre-exists, and that these souls are angels and powers above, but that, having transgressed in sins, they have for this reason been shut up in this body for punishment.

**64,4,6a** And that they are sent [down] by God for punishment, so that they may undergo a first judgement here.

**64,4,6b** For this reason, he says, the body is also called demas, since the soul is held bound within the body — conjuring up the fabulous notion of the Greeks of old.

**64,4,6c** And on these matters he sets out [other] fables as well.

**64,4,6d** For he says that we call it soul, psyche, for this reason: because it has been cooled from above.

**64,4,7a** And he forges proofs from the divine Scriptures to suit his own mind, though they neither stand so nor were so delivered.

**64,4,7b** For the prophet’s saying, he says, “Before I was humbled I went astray”, is the word, he says, of the soul itself, as having gone astray above in heaven before being humbled in the body;

**64,4,8** and the saying “Return, my soul, to your rest” is that of one who has acted nobly here in doing good and returns to the rest above because of the righteous dealing of his labour.

**64,4,9a** And there are many other such things to tell.

**64,4,9b** And he says that Adam lost that which was after the image.

**64,4,9c** From this, he says, the Scripture also signified the garments of skin; for, he says, [the phrase] “He made them garments of skin and clothed them” is, he says, the body.

**64,4,9d** And great is the mockery that he chants.

**64,4,10** The resurrection of the dead he makes deficient, at one point establishing it in word, at another denying it most completely, and at other times [saying] that a part of it rises.

**64,4,11a** And further he allegorises whatever he can: paradise and its waters, and the waters above the heavens, and the water beneath the earth.

**64,4,11b** These things and things like them he does not cease to babble.

**64,4,11c** And we have already gone through such matters concerning him, having made mention of them in certain other places as well.

**64,5,1** But it will do no harm now, in the sect that concerns him, to relate once more about the same cause and subject, and to make the refutation from the very things put forward by him.

**64,5,2** For great was the absurdity that later came about in him, and his pursuit of a counterfeit notion that had stepped outside the truth.

**64,5,3** For he seemed to speak against all the sects before him and to overthrow each one, but later he himself also spewed out upon the world this sect, and no ordinary one.

**64,5,4a** And so first I shall set out what was said by him, to expose his counterfeit and fabricated notion, and then I shall also show what is to be said against him by our lowliness.

**64,5,4b** And these are they, as many as he introduced to the world on the first Psalm —

**64,5,5a** for in every Scripture, always slipping over the wording, he fell away on the essential points.

**64,5,5b** But since the bulk is great (for he is said to have composed a great treatise on each Scripture, as I said), [...] he did not refrain from saying whatever seemed good to him.

**64,5,6** And in all that he has said in homilies and by way of prefaces on morals and on the natures of animals and of other things, keeping a middle course, he often gave graceful accounts;

**64,5,7** but in all that he laid down as doctrine, both about the faith and about higher contemplation, he is found the most absurd of all who were before him and after him, except for the obscene practices in the sects.

**64,5,8** For he himself seemed to have taken up an ascetic life, as has also been made plain by me above; and so some say that his chest collapsed from the excess of his discipline, from fasting and from abstaining from things that have life.

**64,5,9** Come then, let us set out his very words from the first Psalm, [together with] the speculations in it as well, which he laid down as doctrine, in his own words, so that no one may say that what we say against him is slander.

**64,5,10a** And it is not at all that only in the first Psalm did he fall away from the truth, but, as I have often said, in every exposition as well.

**64,5,10b** But because of the bulk of his treatise, gathering here from the aforementioned Psalm, let us uncover from one or two or three passages the whole of his depravity concerning the faith, and let us take care to speak against them as necessity requires.

**64,5,11** And the text of each passage follows at once, so that you may know, my hearer who loves the good, that he most plainly made the Son of God a creature in his doctrine, and may know from his audacity concerning the Son that he introduced the Holy Spirit as a creature of a creature.

**64,5,12a** Let us then read the Psalm from the beginning, up to the passage itself, as Origen himself said it:

**64,5,12b** The beginning of Origen’s writings on Psalm 1.

**64,6,1** The divine words say that the divine Scriptures are shut and sealed with “the key of David”, and perhaps also with a seal, of which it is said, “the engraving of a seal, holiness to the Lord”, that is, with the power of God who gave them, the power signified by the seal.

**64,6,2** Now, about their being shut and sealed, John teaches in the Revelation, saying, “And to the angel of the Philadelphian church write: Thus says the holy one, the true one, he who holds the key of David, who opens and none shall shut, and shuts and none shall open: I know your works; see, I have set before you a door standing open, which no one can shut.”

**64,6,3a** And a little after: “And I saw at the right hand of the one seated upon the throne a book, written inside and on the back, sealed up with seven seals.

**64,6,3b** And I saw another mighty angel crying out in a great voice, ‘Who is worthy of opening the book and of unloosing its seals?’

**64,6,4a** And none in heaven, nor upon the earth, nor beneath the earth, could open the book or look at it.

**64,6,4b** And I wept, because no one was found worthy to open the book or to look at it.

**64,6,5** And one of the elders says to me, ‘Weep not; see, the lion that is of Judah’s tribe, David’s root, has conquered, so as to open the book and its seven seals.’”

**64,6,6a** And about their being sealed only, Isaiah speaks thus: “And all these words shall be to you as the words of this sealed book, which, if they give it to a man who knows letters, saying, ‘Read these things’, he will say, ‘I cannot read, for it is sealed.’

**64,6,6b** And this book will be put into the hands of a man who does not know letters, and one will say to him, ‘Read this.’

**64,6,6c** And he will say, ‘I do not know letters.’”

**64,6,7** For these things must be thought to be said not only of the Revelation of John and of Isaiah, but also of all divine Scripture, which, as is acknowledged even by those able to understand divine words only moderately, is filled with riddles and parables, with dark sayings and with other varied kinds of obscurity, hard for human nature to grasp.

**64,6,8** Wishing to teach this, the Saviour too, since the key was with the scribes and Pharisees, who did not strive to find the way to open, says, “Woe to you lawyers, for you have taken away the key of knowledge; you yourselves did not enter, and those who were entering you do not allow to enter.”

**64,7,1** These things have been said by us by way of preface, since we are constrained by your abundant love of learning, and put to shame by your kindness and your modesty, holy Ambrose, to go down into a very great contest, one admittedly beyond us and beyond our capacity.

**64,7,2** And that you brought me to this, though I long drew back because I knew the danger not only of speaking about holy things but much more of writing and leaving them to those after us, charming me in every way in friendship and with an exhortation from the divine — of this you will be my witness before God, together with my whole life, when I am examined about what I have dictated and with what purpose this was done.

**64,7,3a** And in places we hit the mark, but in places either we force things too violently or [...] we seem [...] to say something.

**64,7,3b** But we have traced out what is written, not despising the well-spoken saying “When you speak about God, you are judged by God”, nor the saying “To speak even the truth about God is no small danger.”

**64,7,4a** We ask therefore — since nothing can be good apart from God, and above all the understanding of divinely inspired Scriptures — that you, approaching the God and Father of all things through our Saviour and High Priest, an originated God, ask that it be given to us first to seek rightly, since the promise of finding lies before those who seek; for perhaps those who do not proceed to this by the way are not counted by God among seekers at all.

**64,7,4b** Thus far the passages from Origen.

**64,8,1** And the beginning of what I have to say against this braggart, wise in seeming, searcher of things unsearchable and expounder of heavenly things, who has filled the world with babble, as one better than we has also said of him, is first about the originated God.

**64,8,2** That there are many identical expressions and many words sharing one name in the world is plain to anyone.

**64,8,3** And if the expression were used by someone else, one could say that this too was said in a right sense; but since we have found him in many places wickedly estranging the only-begotten God from the Godhead and essence of the Father, and the Holy Spirit as well, for this reason it is clear, from his having said originated God, that he defines him as created.

**64,8,4a** For as to some wishing to deceive us with sophistry and to say that originated is the same as begotten, [this] is not to be accepted; [...] to say of God, but only of the creatures.

**64,8,4b** For originated is one thing and begotten is another.

**64,8,5** With regard, then, to the created or originated God spoken of by him, we first ask: in what ways was the God honoured by you with this expression created, and how is he to be worshipped, if he is made?

**64,8,6** For take away the blame laid by the holy apostle upon those who make a god of the creation, and show me a created God who is worshipped according to the godly faith, which worships not the creation but the Creator, and the sequence of your argument, which has strayed from the godliness of the fathers, will be reasonable in your case.

**64,8,7a** But you cannot establish this.

**64,8,7b** And even if you dared to filch it from somewhere and force it, not even so will you be able to divert the sound reasoning of the godly into such worthlessness, you god-smitten man.

**64,8,8a** For both sense and reason fight against you.

**64,8,8b** For nothing created is to be worshipped, as I have said.

**64,8,8c** But if it is to be worshipped at all, then, since many other created things exist, it would make no difference for us to worship all things together with the one created being, since they are fellow servants and have fallen under the same course of naming.

**64,9,1a** Let us look through the four gospels, by which God the Word, on his coming, ordered our whole life, to see whether Christ has anywhere stated, “God created me”, or else, “My Father created me”.

**64,9,1b** And let us see whether the Father too declared in one of the gospels, “I created the Son and have sent him to you”.

**64,9,1c** But on this let it rest thus far for now.

**64,9,2** For we have often set out much by way of testimonies against those who bring in the creature.

**64,9,3** But it will do no harm, in the present place too, to show how easily the argument is grasped, and to say to this would-be philosopher: How, sir, can he be created who declares, “I am in the Father, and in me is the Father, and the two of us are one”?

**64,9,4a** And how can he be of another kind who has equal honour with the Father?

**64,9,4b** For “no one knows the Son except the Father, nor the Father except the Son”, and “He who has seen me has seen the Father.”

**64,9,5a** Again, in the same way, taking up the sequence once more, we shall speak from his own words of all that he puts in doubt concerning the resurrection.

**64,9,5b** From a single passage let us demonstrate the whole of his opinion and uncover the unbelief of the view his doctrine holds.

**64,9,6** [For] although much has often been said by him on this at great length, and he has babbled of it in many books, I shall nevertheless set out the refutation from what he expounded in the first Psalm against those who have believed in the resurrection of our sure hope.

**64,10,1a** And it is thus: he says, “Therefore the ungodly will not rise in judgement.”

**64,10,1b** Then (as is his habit, making a display from the versions): Likewise, he says, Theodotion, Aquila, Symmachus.

**64,10,1c** Then he adds, mockingly, against the sons of the truth:

**64,10,2** From this, he says, the simpler of the believers take their start and think that the ungodly will not attain the resurrection and will not be deemed worthy of the divine judgement; but what they understand by the resurrection, and what sort of judgement they imagine, they do not make at all clear.

**64,10,3** For even if they seem to make declarations about these things, scrutiny will expose them as unable to keep what follows consistent, since they have not grasped the manner of the resurrection and of the judgement.

**64,10,4a** If, then, we ask them whose the resurrection is, they answer, he says, that it is of the bodies which we now wear.

**64,10,4b** Then, when we ask further whether it is of the whole substance or not, they say, before examining the matter, that it is of the whole.

**64,10,5** But if we raise a further difficulty, humouring their simplicity and nothing about the substance being in flux [...], inquiring whether the blood that has flowed away in bloodlettings will rise with us, and the flesh wasted away in illnesses, and all the hairs we have ever had, or only those at the end, at our departure,

**64,10,6** then, being hard pressed, at times they stumble at the examination being made, thinking that one must leave it to God to do what he wills about these things, and at other times, seeming to make a concession, they say that the hairs at the end of this life will rise with us.

**64,10,7** But the more spirited of them, so as not to be compelled by their argument to gather together the blood, that which has often been discharged from our bodies, and the flesh that changes in illnesses [into] sweat or matter of whatever kind, say that our body as it is at the end rises.

**64,11,1a** These are the paltry phrases of the aforementioned would-be philosopher, raising difficulties against the truth, which I have of necessity set out by way of demonstration for any who wish to learn the whole mind of his unbelief concerning the resurrection.

**64,11,1b** For indeed he has other things too, after others, many [...] things, in this sequence on the Psalm.

**64,11,2** For he says, “Therefore the ungodly will not rise in judgement”; and from this he lays hold, and further still, [against] those who define the resurrection as sure and believe in the firm hope of the resurrection of the dead, as though they were simple folk, uttering many wicked things and setting forth a sophistical notion,

**64,11,3** nothing persuasive, but wholly a matter of syllogism, to the ruin of those persuaded by him [...]; from the things that befall us by nature he tried to overthrow the confession of the hope of this same resurrection of ours in truth.

**64,11,4a** But I, not wishing in daring to be better than those who have already laboured well and most justly overthrown all the rhetorical villainy he devised, since I am but a small man, thought it right to be content with what was said against this same Origen by the blessed Methodius in his discourse On the Resurrection, which I shall set out here word for word.

**64,11,4b** And what Methodius wrote is as follows, as he himself arranged it:

**64,11,4c** An epitome of Origen’s words, from the writings of Methodius.

**64,12,1** From this the simpler of the believers take their start and think that the ungodly will not attain the resurrection [...] — what they understand by the resurrection [...].

**64,12,2** For even if they seem to make declarations about these things, scrutiny will expose them as unable to keep what follows consistent [...].

**64,12,3a** If, then, we ask them whose the resurrection is, they answer that it is of the bodies which we now wear.

**64,12,3b** Then, when we ask further whether it is of the whole substance or not, they say, before examining the matter, that it is of the whole.

**64,12,4** But if we raise a further difficulty, humouring their simplicity [...], whether the blood that has flowed away in bloodlettings will rise with us, and the flesh and the hairs we have ever had, or only those near the departure,

**64,12,5** being hard pressed, they run for refuge to God’s [being free] to do what he wills; but the more spirited of them, so as not to be compelled by their argument to gather together the same blood which has often happened to be discharged from our bodies [...], say that our body as it is at the end will rise.

**64,12,6** And we raised the further difficulty, because bodily nature is changeable, and things such as these: just as foods are taken up into our body and change their likenesses,

**64,12,7** so too our bodies are changed within carnivorous birds and wild beasts and turn into parts of their bodies; and again those, being eaten by men or other animals, are changed in turn and become the bodies of men or of other animals.

**64,12,8a** And as this happens over and over, the same body must of necessity frequently become part of several men.

**64,12,8b** Whose body, then, will it be in the resurrection?

**64,12,8c** And so it will come about that we fall into a depth of senseless babble.

**64,13,1** And after these difficulties they take refuge in all things being possible to God, and they bring forward passages of the Scriptures which, on the ready reading, can suggest what they understand:

**64,13,2a** for example the one in Ezekiel, “And the hand of the Lord came upon me, and led me out in the Spirit and placed me in the middle of the plain, and this was full of human bones.

**64,13,2b** And he led me round about them in a circle; and lo, very many over the surface of the plain, and lo, these were exceedingly dry.

**64,13,3a** And he said to me, ‘Son of man, will these bones live?’

**64,13,3b** And I said, ‘Lord God, you know these things.’

**64,13,3c** And he said to me, ‘Prophesy, son of man;

**64,13,4a** and you shall say to them: You bones that are dry, listen to the word of the Lord.

**64,13,4b** Thus says Adonai the Lord to these bones: See, I am bringing into you a spirit of life, and I will set sinews on you and bring flesh upon you and stretch skin over you, and I will put my spirit upon you and you shall live, and I will set you upon your land, and you shall know that I am the Lord.’”

**64,13,5a** This text, then, they use [as] the more persuasive.

**64,13,5b** And they [gather] other gospel texts too, such as “There will be the wailing and the gnashing of teeth”, and “Fear the one who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna”, and the one in Paul, “He will give life to your mortal bodies through his Spirit that dwells in you.”

**64,14,1** But every lover of truth, applying his mind to these matters in the same way, must contend about the resurrection, both to preserve the tradition of the ancients and to guard against falling into the babble of poor notions, at once impossible and unworthy of God.

**64,14,2** This, then, is to be understood on this subject: that every body held together by a nature which takes certain things from outside into it by way of food and discharges other things in place of those brought in — as do the bodies of plants and of animals — never has the same material substrate.

**64,14,3** Therefore the body has not wrongly been called a river, because, strictly speaking, perhaps not even for two days is the first substrate in our body the same, although Paul, say, or Peter is always the same (not in respect of the soul alone, whose substance in our view neither flows nor ever has anything brought into it), even if the nature of the body is in flux, by reason of the form that characterises the body being the same,

**64,14,4** so that the features presenting the bodily quality of Peter and Paul also remain the same, the quality by which scars from childhood also remain on bodies, and certain other peculiarities, [such as] moles, and besides these anything else of the kind;

**64,14,5** this form, the bodily one, by which Peter and Paul are given their form, is put about the soul again in the resurrection, changed for the better — not by any means this substrate arrayed at the first.

**64,14,6** And just as from babyhood to old age the form is [the same], even if the features seem to undergo much variation, so we must understand that the form in the present is the same as the one to come, though the alteration to what is finer will be as great as can be.

**64,14,7** For the soul, existing in bodily places, must use bodies suited to those places.

**64,14,8** And just as, if we had to live in the sea, having become water creatures, we would by all means need to have gills and the rest of the make-up of fish, so, since we are going to inherit heaven’s kingdom and to be in places that differ, we must use spiritual bodies;

**64,14,9** not that the former form is made to disappear, even if its change is to something more glorious, just as the form of Jesus and of Moses and of Elijah was not other in the transfiguration than what it had been.

**64,15,1** Do not stumble, then, if someone should say that the first substrate will not then be the same, when reason shows those able to attend that not even now can the first substrate be the same for two days.

**64,15,2** And it is worth noting too that what is sown is of one kind and what rises of another:

**64,15,3** for “a natural body is sown, a spiritual body is raised”, and again the apostle adds, teaching that we are to put off almost [all] the earthly quality while the form is preserved in the resurrection, “And this I declare, brothers: flesh and blood are not able to inherit God’s kingdom, nor does corruption inherit incorruption.”

**64,15,4** For perhaps there will be about the saint [a body] held together by that which once gave form to the flesh, but flesh no longer; rather, what was once characterised in the flesh will be characterised in the spiritual body.

**64,15,5a** And in answer to the words of the Scriptures which our brothers cite, these things may be said.

**64,15,5b** And first [to] those in Ezekiel, in so far as the simpler ones wish [...] upon them: according to these words there will not even be a resurrection of flesh, but only of bones and skins and sinews.

**64,15,6a** At the same time we must also point out to them that they are carried away, as people who have not understood what is written.

**64,15,6b** For if bones are named, it is not at all these bones that must be understood, just as they are not in “Our bones were scattered beside Hades”, and “Scattered are all my bones”, and “Heal me, because my bones are troubled”, since it is plain that the bones are not spoken of in the common sense.

**64,15,7a** At any rate it is added to the passage, “They say, ‘Our bones have become dry.’”

**64,15,7b** Do they say, “Our bones have become dry”, wishing them to be gathered together and to rise?

**64,15,7c** But that is impossible.

**64,15,7d** But they might say:

**64,15,8a** “Our bones have become dry”, having been in captivities and having lost all vital moisture; at any rate they add, “Our hope is lost, we are undone.”

**64,15,8b** Therefore the promise of resurrection is that of the people, from the fall and from the deadness, as it were, in which they had been made dead when they were handed over to their enemies because of their sins.

**64,15,9a** And sinners too are said by the Saviour to be “tombs full of dead men’s bones and of all uncleanness”.

**64,15,9b** And it befits God to open the tombs of each one and to bring us out of the tombs made alive, just as the Saviour drew Lazarus out.

**64,16,1a** As to “There will be the wailing and the gnashing of teeth”, this difficulty must be put to them: that, as in this life the Creator fashioned every member for some use, so too he fashioned the teeth for cutting up solid food.

**64,16,1b** What use, then, have those who are punished for teeth?

**64,16,1c** For surely, by their own account, they will not eat when they are in Gehenna.

**64,16,2a** And it must be shown that not everything ought to be taken according to the letter.

**64,16,2b** At any rate, “The teeth of sinners”, it says, “you have broken”, and “The Lord has crushed the jaw-teeth of the lions.”

**64,16,2c** And who is so witless as to imagine that God, maiming the bodies of sinners, breaks only their teeth?

**64,16,3** Just as, then, anyone who wished these things to be so would be hard pressed and dragged to allegory, so we must inquire into the gnashing of teeth of those who are punished, since the soul has a power of chewing, which at the time of its conviction concerning its sins will grind its teeth through the striking together of the teeth of what it devised.

**64,16,4** And “Fear him who is able to destroy both the soul and the body in Gehenna” perhaps teaches that the soul is bodiless, and perhaps also shows that it will not be punished without a body; on which matter, in discoursing on nature, we have spoken of the form and of the first substrate.

**64,16,5** And the apostle’s saying too, “He will give life also to your mortal bodies” — since the body is mortal and does not share in the true life — can show that the bodily form we have been speaking of, being mortal by nature, “when Christ, our life, is manifested”, itself also changes from being “a body of death”, made alive through the life-giving Spirit, having from [fleshly] become spiritual.

**64,16,6** And “But someone will say, How are the dead raised, and with what kind of body do they come?” plainly shows that the first substrate will not rise.

**64,16,7a** For if we have taken the illustration rightly, we must observe that the seminal principle in the grain of wheat, having laid hold of the matter lying beside it and having passed through the whole of it, [and] having encompassed it with the same form, imposes the powers it has upon what was once earth and water and air and fire, and, having overcome their qualities, changes them into that quality of which it is itself the maker; and so the ear is filled out, differing exceedingly from the original grain in size and shape and variety.

**64,16,7b** From the same Proclus.

**64,17,1** Such, then, in summary, are the things that Origen eagerly pursued in undertaking his treatise on the resurrection, displaying a most intricate speculation; and consider also what follows from them in order.

**64,17,2** For it remains to take up, in addition to what has been said, the testimonies from the Scriptures as well, so that, as a statue [that has] all its parts in proportion, [so] this argument, having received [proportion], may be wholly and fully constructed, lacking nothing of what goes to make up shape and beauty.

**64,17,3a** We must say, then, how the Scriptures agree with these things, strengthening man towards the completion of a better way of life.

**64,17,3b** For if anyone is able to understand these things accurately, without straying from what is needful, he will know that the resurrection must not be taken of this body, since it cannot remain unchanged through eternity, but of the spiritual body, in which the very character that is preserved even now in this one will be kept, so that each of us may be the same in form as well, as was also said by Origen.

**64,17,4a** For it is thus that he lays down the resurrection will be.

**64,17,4b** For since the material body is in flux and never remains by itself even for a short while, but is added to and taken away from around the form that characterises the man, by which its shape is also held together, it is necessary, he says, to expect that the resurrection must be preserved for the form alone.

**64,17,5** And so that you may not say, I do not understand — for he treated this obscurely — I shall explain the meaning of these things to you more clearly as follows.

**64,17,6a** For surely you have already seen the skin of an animal, or something else of the kind, filled with water, how, if it is emptied a little and filled a little, it always shows the same form.

**64,17,6b** For whatever the container is like, what is inside must also take its shape from it.

**64,17,7** For come: as the water drains away, if someone should pour in as much as flows out, not allowing the skin to be emptied of water all at once, is it not necessary that what is added should appear such as the former also was, because the container too, around which the water flows in and flows out, is the same?

**64,17,8a** And indeed whoever wishes to liken the body too to these things will not be put to shame.

**64,17,8b** For in the same way the things brought in from food in place of the flesh that has been driven out will be changed into the shape of the containing form; and as much as is spread through the region of the eyes resembles the eyes, as much as through the face, the face, and as much as through the other parts, those parts; so that each man appears the same, not because the flesh that first underlay him is in him, but because of the form, by which what is added is given its form.

**64,17,9a** And so, if we are not the same even for a few days in body, but in the form that is in the body (for this alone has stood fast from birth), much more shall we not then be the same in the same flesh, but in the form that is now [and] always preserved in us and abides.

**64,17,9b** For what the skin is there, the form is here; and what the water is there in the image, that here is what is added and taken away.

**64,17,10** Just as, then, though the body now is not the same, the character is nevertheless preserved the same in the same shape, so too then, though the body is not the same, the form, increased to greater glory, will be shown no longer in a corruptible body but in an impassible and spiritual one, just as that of Jesus was at the transfiguration, when he went up the mountain with Peter, and that of Moses and that of Elijah, who were seen with him.

**64,18,1** Let these matters, then, have been examined thus far; for this, in sum, is the meaning of Origen’s doctrines.

**64,18,2** But if one of those who dispute, putting forward the body of Christ, because he has been called “the firstborn of the dead” and “the firstfruits of those who have fallen asleep”, should say that, just as he rose, so one must expect the [bodies] of all likewise to rise as his did, so that, just as Christ has been raised, “so God will bring with him those who have fallen asleep through Jesus”, and the [body] of Jesus has been raised having both the flesh it had and the bones, as Thomas too was persuaded, we shall say:

**64,18,3** but the body of Christ was not “of the will of a man”, “pleasure joining in sleep”, “conceived in iniquities and carried in the womb in sins”, but “of the Holy Spirit and the power of the Most High and a virgin”, whereas yours is sleep and pleasure and filth.

**64,18,4a** And for this reason the wise Sirach too: “For when a man dies,” he declares, “he will inherit creeping things and beasts and worms”; and [David], in Psalm eighty-seven, “Will you work wonders for the dead, or will physicians raise them up and they give thanks to you?

**64,18,4b** Will anyone tell of your mercy in the grave, and of your truth in destruction?

**64,18,4c** Will your wonders be known in the darkness, and your righteousness in a land forgotten?”

**64,18,5** And other such words are at hand for anyone who wishes to gather them from the Scriptures, which [...], so that we, by recalling them all in great number, may not swell our discourse to many times what has been said.

**64,19,1a** The rest from Methodius:

**64,19,1b** When, then, Proclus had at last stopped, and there was a long silence among those present, who had been thrown down far enough towards unbelief, and I perceived that he had really stopped, gently raising my head and drawing breath like sailors when the wave is at last subsiding, still trembling and in a daze (for I had been struck, let it be said to you, and flooded by the forcefulness of his words), I turn to Auxentius and, calling him:

**64,19,2a** Auxentius, I said, I think the poet’s line “When two go together” was not spoken in vain.

**64,19,2b** For indeed our opponents are two.

**64,19,2c** “Therefore let us both together hold back the might of both.”

**64,19,3** For I choose you as my helper and comrade in the fight against them, lest Aglaophon, with Proclus and Origen, armed against us with their subversive arguments, should sack the resurrection for us.

**64,19,4** Come, then, let us draw up our line against their sophistries, fearing none of the contentious arguments with which the timid are assailed; for in them there is nothing at all sound or firm, but only a comely show of words, contrived to astound and persuade the hearers, not for the sake of truth and benefit, but so that the arguments may seem wise to those present.

**64,19,5** Hence, at any rate, to the many, arguments drawn from probabilities, embroidered at times for beauty and pleasure, are thought more serious than those examined for accuracy, since teachers no longer strive for what is better and worthy of reverence, but to please and to win applause, like the sophists, who choose a fee for their speeches, cheapening wisdom for praises.

**64,19,6** In the old days, then, exposition was altogether brief, since the men of that time were ambitious not to delight but to benefit those present; but since, for the rest, it has been granted to all, for ease, that they may interpret the Scriptures, all have grown dull at doing what is good, filled with conceit, while they have advanced in reasoning, priding themselves, as being clever at knowing everything, ashamed to admit that they need to be taught, but rather contending and leaping forward, as the teachers [...].

**64,19,7** Emboldened from this, at any rate, they have slipped from the reverent and gentle belief that God can do all things as he promised, and have come to empty questionings and blasphemies, not understanding that deeds were not pursued for the sake of words, but words for deeds’ sake, as [in] medicine, by which the sick must be saved, the words being confirmed by deeds, so that, when we have been tuned in all things, the mind may be in harmony with the best words, rendering the character in concord with the tongue, like a lyre, and not unmusical and discordant.

**64,19,8** For one must strive to practise justice with a view to acquiring it in truth, and not with a view to seeming, setting foot lamely upon wisdom and contending for reputation rather than for truth, having put on porches and pretences and the whole costume of play-acting.

**64,20,1** For there are, there are those who, with the onsets of words decked out in tragic style, like women who adorn themselves outwardly with art for deceit, [...], unless one of those still young in the faith looks at them guardedly and soberly.

**64,20,2** Hence one must beware of receiving such an argument into the heart before learning it securely; for deceivers often get the better of the hesitant, just as the Sirens did those who fled from them, hiding from afar their hatred of men with the beauty of their voices.

**64,20,3a** Or how, Auxentius, I said, [does it seem] to you that these things should be put?

**64,20,3b** And he answered: Just as it does to you.

**64,20,4a** Shall we not, then, say that the sophists among the heterodox are by all means imitators of images of truth, not knowing the truth, like painters?

**64,20,4b** For these too attempt to imitate shipwrights and ships and pilots, without knowing how to build ships or to steer them.

**64,20,5** Then, [if] you will, scraping off the colours, shall we persuade the children among them who marvel at pictures of this sort that this ship is no ship, and the pilot no pilot, but a wall adorned outwardly with colours and paintings for delight, and that those who fashioned these things from the colours are imitators of the image of a ship and of a pilot, and not of a ship?

**64,20,6a** The preface is long for one who longs to hear.

**64,20,6b** But useful, I said, my excellent friend.

**64,20,6c** For if someone were to take away the words from the divinely inspired writings, with which these men colour and embroider their own opinion for deceit, presumptuously calling it righteousness and truth though they do not know righteousness at all, how much do you think they will be mocked when stripped of such names?

**64,20,6d** Very much indeed, he said.

**64,20,7a** Which, then, Auxentius, said I, do you wish: to lead the way on this road yourself, or that I should lead?

**64,20,7b** It is right, he said, that you lead; for it is you who are beginning the discussion.

**64,21,1** Well then, it was said — come, let us examine Aglaophon’s meaning part by part, in order, from the beginning — that the soul took up this body which we wear because of the transgression, having passed the earlier ages blessedly without it.

**64,21,2a** For the garments of skin, [he said], are bodies, into which it befell the souls to be shut, so that, carrying a corpse about, they might pay the penalty for what they had done.

**64,21,2b** Or were not these the things first said by you at the beginning, doctor?

**64,21,2c** But come, remind me, if I seem to you not to have remembered anything.

**64,21,3** You have no need of any reminding on this now; for these very things were what we said first.

**64,21,4a** What then?

**64,21,4b** Was not this too said by you in many ways in what followed: that the body becomes for us an obstacle to the apprehension and knowledge of the things that truly are, because of the occupations of adorning and tending it and the other assaults concerning the appetite of the belly, and moreover a cause of blasphemies and of sins of every kind, because the soul by itself, apart from the body, cannot sin at all;

**64,21,5** and that for this reason, so that after its departure from here it may be free of sin and of disobedience in the heavens, where it will also have its dwelling with the angels, it must remain free and naked of body, since this body becomes for it a contributing cause and accomplice of being defiled and of sinning?

**64,21,6a** For without a body it is impossible for a soul to sin.

**64,21,6b** Hence, so that it may be kept sinless for the boundless age, it will no longer take the body that weighs it down to corruption and injustice.

**64,21,7a** Yes, this too was said.

**64,21,7b** What then?

**64,21,7c** Do you think, I said, that this was said well and rightly?

**64,21,7d** And what difference does this make to you, he said?

**64,21,7e** But you are not refuting the argument.

**64,21,8a** None, said I, except that the argument may be examined through what you have said.

**64,21,8b** Well and rightly, he said.

**64,21,8c** And does one who says things contrary to himself and discordant seem to you to lay them down well and rightly?

**64,21,8d** Not in any way at all.

**64,21,9a** For does such a man seem to you to play the part of truth unmusically?

**64,21,9b** Most of all, he said.

**64,21,9c** Then you do not accept one who tunes his harmonies to the false melody of arguments?

**64,21,9d** Not in any way at all.

**64,21,10a** Then neither will you accept anything at all from yourself when you speak unmusically.

**64,21,10b** For having granted that the souls sinned without bodies when they fell from the commandment, and having declared that because of their lawlessness God afterwards gave them the garments of skin, so that, carrying a corpse about, they might pay the penalty — declaring the garments to be the bodies — as the argument proceeds you forget what you laid down at first and say that the soul cannot sin by itself.

**64,21,11a** For it is not at all its nature to do this, but the body has become for it the contributing cause of evils of every kind.

**64,21,11b** Hence, so that it may not be urged on to injustice again, as happened before through the body, it will thus be without it for the ages,

**64,21,12** although you said at first that the soul sinned in paradise before the body, while it was yet blessed and untouched by pain; for to it, when it had transgressed the commandment, sin having already been confirmed through its yielding to the serpent, the body was given as a punishing bond.

**64,21,13a** So either the former or the latter is not right.

**64,21,13b** For either it sinned before the body, and will sin none the less even if it does not receive a body, and the long talk about the body not rising is vain; or it sinned with the body, and the garments of skin cannot be thought to be the body.

**64,21,14** For man is found to have set aside the divine commandment before they were made; and it is for this reason that the garments are made, to cover the nakedness that had come upon them from their sin.

**64,21,15** But do I persuade you, and do you see that you have laid down things that fight against each other, and has this become clear to you, or do you not yet understand, Aglaophon, I said, what I am saying?

**64,21,16a** I understand, he said, and I have no need at all to hear it a second time; but I failed to notice that I had spoken wrongly.

**64,21,16b** For having granted that the garments of skin are the bodies, I must admit that the soul sinned even before it came into the body,

**64,21,17** since the transgression took place before they were made — for it is on account of the transgression that the garments are made for them, and the transgression does not happen because of the garments — and from this admission I must agree that it is not this body that is the contributing cause of wickedness, but the soul itself, in itself.

**64,21,18a** Therefore, even if it does not receive the body, it will sin, since it sinned before too without a body.

**64,21,18b** And it is senseless to claim that the body is unable to return to life on this account, lest it become for the soul a contributing cause of sinning.

**64,21,19a** For it will sin — just as it sinned before the body, so also after laying the body aside, even if it does not take the body back again.

**64,21,19b** Hence for these reasons I must accept neither myself nor another when saying that the garments of skin are the bodies.

**64,21,19c** But if I were to accept it, I must also admit what has been said.

**64,22,1a** What then, I said, Aglaophon: does not that too seem to you not to be right?

**64,22,1b** Which?

**64,22,1c** Saying, said I, that the body was contrived as a bond and fetters against the soul, and that for this reason the prophet called us “prisoners of the earth”, and David “men in fetters”.

**64,22,2a** And he: I cannot answer you offhand, he said.

**64,22,2b** But why do you not converse with someone else?

**64,22,3a** And I — for I perceived that he was blushing, shrinking from being refuted by the argument —: Do you think, I said, that I am attempting to refute you out of envy, and not striving to make the matter before us plain?

**64,22,3b** But, my good friend, do not grow weary of being questioned.

**64,22,4** For you see that our discussion is not about small matters, but about the manner in which one must believe; for I think no evil so great befalls a man as that which comes from things that are necessary, whenever he holds false opinions about them.

**64,22,5a** Come, then, readily to the questions: make things plain, and if I seem to you to say anything untrue, refute me, caring more for the truth than for me.

**64,22,5b** For I think being refuted a greater good than refuting, inasmuch as it is a greater thing to be rid of an evil oneself than to rid another of it.

**64,22,6a** Come, then, let us set the arguments side by side and consider whether they will differ from one another at all.

**64,22,6b** For the matters we are disputing happen to be not at all trifling, but things which it is finest to know and shameful not to know.

**64,22,6c** Well then, you do not believe that the body comes back to life, but I do.

**64,22,6d** Certainly, he said, and for the reasons I have stated.

**64,22,7a** And you, said I, declared it to be a bond and a prison and a tomb and a burden and fetters, but I do not say so.

**64,22,7b** What you tell me is true, he said.

**64,22,8** And indeed you said that it is the contributing cause of licentiousness and error and grief and anger and, in short, of all the other evils, which hinder our soul’s impulse towards what is better, not allowing us to arrive at the apprehension and knowledge of the things that truly are.

**64,22,9a** For even if we attempt to hunt down any of the things that are, a gloom always falls in and darkens our reasoning, not permitting us to look clearly upon the truth.

**64,22,9b** For the inquiry conducted through our ears is filled with deceit, as you said; and full of deceit too is that through sight, and full of deceit that through the other senses.

**64,22,10** You see, he said, Eubulius, how ready I am to praise you whenever you set out the arguments rightly?

**64,23,1** So then, that you may praise me even more: if the body is held among you to be a bond, it cannot be thought to be a cause of wickedness for the soul, nor yet of injustice, but conversely of self-control and discipline.

**64,23,2a** Consider it this way; for so you would follow better.

**64,23,2b** Where do we take those who are sick in body?

**64,23,2c** Is it not to the physicians?

**64,23,2d** Clearly, he said.

**64,23,3a** And where do we take those who do wrong?

**64,23,3b** Is it not to the judges?

**64,23,3c** Necessarily.

**64,23,3d** To pay the penalty, then, for what they have done, I said, justly?

**64,23,3e** Yes.

**64,23,3f** And what is just is altogether fine?

**64,23,3g** He agreed.

**64,23,3h** And one who judges justly does well; for he judges justly?

**64,23,3i** He assented.

**64,23,3j** And what is fine is beneficial?

**64,23,3k** So it appears.

**64,23,4a** Those who are judged, then, are benefited.

**64,23,4b** For their wickedness is removed by the punishments, being checked, just as diseases too are removed by the incisions and drugs of physicians.

**64,23,4c** For when a wrongdoer pays the penalty, his soul is set right, as he casts off the great disease, injustice.

**64,23,4d** He agreed.

**64,23,5a** What then?

**64,23,5b** Do you not say that punishments are justly applied to those punished in proportion to their sins, just as operations are to those under treatment in proportion to their wounds?

**64,23,5c** He nodded assent.

**64,23,6a** Then he who has done things worthy of death has his penalty assessed at death, he who deserves blows at blows, and he who deserves bonds at bonds?

**64,23,6b** He agreed.

**64,23,7a** And the one found guilty, I said, is punished with bonds or blows or some other such penalty, so that he may repent and cease from wrongdoing, being straightened by torments like a warped piece of wood?

**64,23,7b** What you say is most true, he said.

**64,23,8a** For the judge punishes him not for the sake of the past offence but of the future one, so that he may not do it again.

**64,23,8b** Clearly, he said.

**64,23,9a** For the bond plainly removes his impulse to do wrong, not allowing him to do what he wishes.

**64,23,9b** True.

**64,23,10a** Then he is kept from sinning, since the bonds do not allow him to indulge in pleasures at liberty, but restrain him and teach him to honour what is just, until he himself, being admonished, learns self-control.

**64,23,10b** So it appears, he said.

**64,23,11a** Neither, then, does the bond become a contributing cause of offending.

**64,23,11b** So it seems.

**64,23,11c** For it chastens and makes men more just, being a remedy for the soul, harsh and biting, but healing.

**64,23,11d** Clearly, he said.

**64,23,12a** What then?

**64,23,12b** Come, let us examine again what came before.

**64,23,12c** Did you not grant that the body is the soul’s bond because of the transgression?

**64,23,12d** And I grant it, he said.

**64,23,13a** And that the soul commits sin together with the body, if adultery and murder and impiety seem to you to be sin, things which the soul does with the body?

**64,23,13b** He nodded assent.

**64,23,14a** But we agreed that the prisoner cannot do wrong?

**64,23,14b** We agreed, he said.

**64,23,14c** And that he is hindered, being pained by the bonds?

**64,23,14d** Yes.

**64,23,14e** And the flesh is the soul’s bond?

**64,23,14f** He nodded assent.

**64,23,15a** But while in the flesh we do sin, the flesh concurring with us in this?

**64,23,15b** We sin, he said.

**64,23,16a** But the one in fetters cannot sin?

**64,23,16b** And here too he nodded assent.

**64,23,16c** For he is held in a stranglehold?

**64,23,16d** Yes.

**64,23,16e** Not being permitted by the bond?

**64,23,16f** Clearly.

**64,23,17a** But the body is an accomplice in sinning?

**64,23,17b** Yes.

**64,23,17c** Whereas the bond restrains?

**64,23,17d** He assented.

**64,23,18a** The body, then, is not a bond, either according to you or according to anyone else, I said, Aglaophon, but the soul’s accomplice towards either side, whether towards good or towards evil.

**64,23,18b** He conceded.

**64,24,1a** Then, since these things stand so, make your defence, Aglaophon, regarding what you laid down at first.

**64,24,1b** For earlier you said that the body is the soul’s bond and prison and fetters.

**64,24,1c** And do you see how your words are out of tune with these things?

**64,24,2** For how could they agree, if indeed, my excellent friend, the flesh must be thought a bond, while the soul uses the bond as an accomplice and helper in injustice, which is impossible?

**64,24,3a** For if it was given on account of sin as a place of torment, in order that the soul, being pained, might be trained to honour God, how does the body cooperate with it and contend alongside it in doing wrong?

**64,24,3b** For bonds and prisons and fetters and, in short, all such punitive contrivances for correction become restraints upon wrongdoing and sinning for those who are punished.

**64,24,4a** For it is not in order that the wrongdoer may do more wrong that the bond is given to him as an accomplice in wrongdoing, but in order that he may cease from wrongdoing, being tormented through the bonds.

**64,24,4b** For it is for this reason that judges fetter the wicked.

**64,24,5** For they are prevented by the bonds from doing evil even if they do not wish it; and evildoing belongs to those who are at liberty and live unguarded, not to those in fetters.

**64,24,6a** But man first committed murder, in the person of Cain, grew in unbelief, gave heed to idols, and revolted from God.

**64,24,6b** And how was the body given to him as a bond?

**64,24,6c** Or how did God, after man had broken the law before the body, give him the body as a greater accomplice in injustice?

**64,24,7a** And how, even after the making of the bond, is it said, “Behold, before your face I have set life and death; choose life; good and evil; choose the good”, and “If you are willing and will listen to me”?

**64,24,7b** For these things were said to one who has power over himself, and not to one led under bonds and necessity.

**64,24,8** So it is established on every count that [one must] not consider the body fetters or prison or bonds, nor for this reason the souls prisoners of the earth, as though God had condemned them to be bound with earthy fetters.

**64,24,9a** For how could it be, when this cannot be demonstrated?

**64,24,9b** And plainly absurd too is that notion that the body will not be with the soul in the life through the ages because it is a bond and fetters, lest, according to them, we become eternal condemned prisoners of corruption when we are to be in the kingdom of light.

**64,24,10** For since the argument in which they defined the flesh as “the bond of the soul” has been sufficiently undone and refuted, this too is undone: “So that we may not be prisoners because of it in the kingdom of light — if we take it back — for this reason it will not rise.”

**64,25,1a** For what other kind of truth, more evident than what has been said, must still be shown to persuade the disaffected, one which they will tolerate?

**64,25,1b** But this contentiousness of theirs one could refute both from these things and from many others.

**64,25,2a** For in what follows, as the discussion proceeds, we shall show by truths of nature, not by guesses, that it was not on account of our fellowship with the body that Jeremiah termed us “prisoners” “of the earth”, nor in this sense that David said “men in fetters”.

**64,25,2b** For it is worth bringing forward these things too, in which they most plainly appear to fail.

**64,25,3** Having said, then, what is fitting about the garments of skin, and that before they were made the first-formed lived with a body, enjoying deathlessness, and moreover that it is impossible to think the body a bond and a prison, gentlemen of the jury (for I call you judges of the arguments, most excellent Theophilus), I shall turn next, as I promised, to what follows on the matters before us, so that we may see more distinctly what we wish to see.

**64,26,1** God, the Creator of all things, when he had composed the universe in orderly fashion, as one would a great city, and had arranged it by commanding with his word, and each element had been harmonised in concord with it and all things had been filled with the varieties of living creatures, so that the world might grow to full beauty, bringing forth living forms of every nature — stars in the heaven and birds in the air, four-footed beasts on the earth and swimming creatures in the water — last of all, having prepared the universe beforehand as a most beautiful house for him, man,

**64,26,2** a copy resembling his own image, he brought into the world, having fashioned him with his own hands, radiant as a statue in a beautiful temple.

**64,26,3** For he knew that whatever he should fashion with his own hand would of necessity be immortal, as being the work of immortality.

**64,26,4a** For immortal things become immortal by immortality, just as evil things are evil by evil and unjust things unjust by injustice.

**64,26,4b** For doing wrong is not the work of justice but of injustice, nor conversely is doing right the work of injustice but of justice, just as causing corruption is not the work of incorruption but of corruption, nor is making immortal the work of corruption but of incorruption.

**64,26,5** And, in short, of whatever kind the maker is, by the same reasoning the thing made necessarily turns out to be of that kind too.

**64,26,6a** Now God is immortality and life and incorruption, and man is God’s work.

**64,26,6b** And everything made by immortality is immortal; man, therefore, is immortal.

**64,26,6c** That is why he made man himself with his own hand, but the remaining kinds of living creatures he commanded the air and earth and water to produce.

**64,26,7a** And man is most truly said, according to nature, to be neither soul without body nor again body without soul, but that which is composed from the union of soul and body into the one form of the beautiful.

**64,26,7b** Hence it appears from this that man was made immortal, free from all corruption and disease.

**64,26,8a** And one could learn this sufficiently from the Scripture too.

**64,26,8b** For in the case of the other creatures, all that are refashioned through the intervals of time, coming to their prime and ageing, “Let the waters,” it says, “bring forth creeping things of living souls, and winged birds flying over the earth along the firmament of heaven”, and “Let the earth bring forth the living soul after its kind, quadrupeds and creeping things and wild beasts of the earth after their kind”;

**64,26,9** but in the case of man it is no longer, as with them, Let the earth bring forth, or, Let the waters bring forth, or, Let there be lights, but “Let us make man in accordance with our image and likeness, and let them rule the fishes of the sea and the birds of the heaven and all the cattle”, and “God took dust out of the ground and moulded man”.

**64,27,1** And so that you too may perceive the difference more clearly — how man, differing from the other creatures [in] each and every respect, and enrolled in the rank of honour next to the angels, is found to be immortal — come, let us treat this too once more according to the true and orthodox reasoning.

**64,27,2a** For to them it was given to be ensouled and to live from the inhaling of the breath that is in the air, but to him from the immortal and surpassing substance itself.

**64,27,2b** For “God breathed into his face a breath of life, and the man came to be a living soul.”

**64,27,3a** And they were appointed to serve and to be ruled, but he to rule and to be master.

**64,27,3b** And to them are given various shapes and forms of nature, as many as the solid and visible nature brought forth at God’s command;

**64,27,4a** but to him is given what is godlike and resembles God, made exact in every respect to that archetypal image of the Father and Only-begotten.

**64,27,4b** For “God made man; after the image of God he made him”;

**64,27,5** and therefore he took care for the permanence of his own statue, so that it might not be easily overtaken by destruction, just as makers of statues take care of theirs.

**64,27,6** For they take thought not only for the beauty of the statues and their comeliness, that they may be magnificently beautiful, but they also take forethought for the immortality of their works, as far as is possible for them, so that they may be preserved [for] a long age without coming apart, as Phidias also did.

**64,27,7** For after making the image at Pisa (it was of ivory), he ordered oil to be poured around the feet in front of the statue, keeping it immortal as far as he could.

**64,27,8a** And so, if the makers of handmade things act thus, would not God, the supreme craftsman, who can do all things and make them even out of things that are not, much rather have contrived by every necessity that his own rational statue, man, should be altogether indestructible and immortal, instead of letting him, so dishonourably dissolving, be handed over to destruction and corruption — him whom he especially deemed worthy to make with his own hands, forming him after his image and after his own likeness, the adornment of the world, for whose sake the world itself came to be?

**64,27,8b** It is not possible to say so.

**64,27,8c** For to be carried away into thinking this would end in folly.

**64,28,1a** But perhaps, not having given your attention to what has now been said, Aglaophon, you will say: And how, if according to you the living being was immortal from its creation, has it become mortal, when the immortal must unchangeably be what it is, not falling away or departing into the baser and mortal nature?

**64,28,1b** For it is impossible, since it is not [...] immortal.

**64,28,2** Because, I shall say, when it had been made with free will for the choice of the good and had received this ordinance, the hater of good came upon it and bewitched it out of envy.

**64,28,3a** For “God created man for incorruption, and he made him an image of his own everlasting being.”

**64,28,3b** For indeed “God did not make death, nor does he take pleasure when the living perish”; “it was by the devil’s envy that death came into the world”, as Wisdom too testifies through Solomon.

**64,28,4a** Whence, then, is death?

**64,28,4b** This must indeed be asked again, if God did not make death.

**64,28,4c** If it came from envy, how then did envy prevail over the will of God?

**64,28,4d** But this is blasphemous, we shall say.

**64,28,4e** Whence, then, is envy? — the objector will say.

**64,28,5a** For if it is from the devil, why then did the devil come to be?

**64,28,5b** If he came to be, is the one who made him also the cause of evil’s existence?
