**64,28,6a** But God is altogether guiltless of all evils; the devil, then, is unoriginated, and if unoriginated, then also impassible and indestructible and in need of nothing.

**64,28,6b** For all these things must of necessity belong to the unoriginated, but the devil is being brought to nothing and punished.

**64,28,6c** But what is punished is altered and suffers, whereas the unoriginated is impassible.

**64,28,6d** The devil, then, is not unoriginated, but originated.

**64,28,7a** And if he is originated, and everything that has come into being came into being from some beginning and has its maker, then there is some maker of the devil.

**64,28,7b** And is that maker, too, unoriginated or originated?

**64,28,7c** But God alone must be understood to be the one unoriginated; for there cannot be, at all, in any way whatever, another maker besides him; for “I,” he says, “am first, and I am hereafter, and besides me there is no other God.”

**64,28,8a** Nor can anything be refashioned or created contrary to his will.

**64,28,8b** For the Son too confesses that he “can do nothing of himself, unless he sees the Father doing something”; “for whatever the Father does,” he says, “the Son likewise does the same.”

**64,28,9a** Nor indeed is anything hostile or opposed to him or set against God; since, even if something were acting against God, it would have ceased to exist, its constitution dissolved by the power and might of God.

**64,28,9b** For it is possible for the maker alone to dissolve even immortal things.

**64,29,1a** What, then, is the devil, you will say.

**64,29,1b** A spirit concerned with matter, as was also said by Athenagoras, made by God, just as the other angels too were made by him, and entrusted with the administration over matter and the forms of matter.

**64,29,2** For this was the constitution of the angels: to have come into being for God for providence over the things ordered by him, so that God might be the one who holds the complete and general providence over all things, himself holding the authority and might over all and steering the universe unswervingly like a ship with the tiller of wisdom, while the angels appointed to the task hold the providence over particulars.

**64,29,3** But the rest remained in the tasks for which God made and appointed them, while he behaved insolently and became wicked in the administration of what had been entrusted to him, having conceived envy against us, just as those also did who afterwards fell in love with flesh and kept company, for the sake of pleasure, with the daughters of men.

**64,29,4** For God appointed that they too should have a self-chosen will towards either side, as with men also, so that either, obeying the Word, they might be with him and enjoy blessedness, or, disobeying, they might be judged.

**64,29,5a** And the devil too was a dawn-bearing star (“How has the dawn-bearer fallen from heaven, he who rose in the morning!”); he was rising among the angels of the light, he was a morning star; but he fell and was dashed to the earth, having acted as man’s guardian to contrary ends.

**64,29,5b** For the divine is indignant with the proud and cuts down arrogant thoughts.

**64,29,6** And it comes to me to speak in verse as well: O serpent, beginning and end of evils for all, and you, Error, who give birth to a heavy treasure of evils, guide of the blind life of ignorance, rejoicing in the laments and groans of mortals — you two, arming for lawless outrages against kin the hands that hated a brother, first persuaded Cain to defile the earth with bloody gore, and you contrived that the first-formed should fall from the undefiled ages to the ground.

**64,30,1a** So much for the devil; but death was devised for conversion, just as blows are for the correction of children newly learning their letters.

**64,30,1b** For death is nothing other than the separation and parting of soul from body.

**64,30,1c** What then?

**64,30,1d** Is God, you will say, a contributing cause of death?

**64,30,2a** For the same argument comes back to us.

**64,30,2b** Far from it, since neither are teachers the primary cause of children being pained by blows.

**64,30,3** Death, then, is a good thing, if it was devised, like blows for children, for conversion — not the death of sin, most wise sirs, but that of the disjoining and separation of the flesh.

**64,30,4** For man, being possessed of free will and his own master, and having received, as I said, a will under his own control and freely choosing for the choice of the good, and having heard, “From every tree that is in paradise you shall eat, but from the tree of knowing good and evil you shall not eat of it; and on whatever day you eat of it you shall die the death”,

**64,30,5** yielding, so as to eat of it, to the devil, who with a baited wisdom persuaded him in various ways to disobedience, set aside the commandment of God; and this became for him a stumbling-block and a snare and an offence.

**64,30,6a** For God did not make evil, nor is he in any way whatever at all the cause of evil; but whatever has been made free by him in this way, to keep and observe the law which he justly laid down, is called evil when it does not observe it.

**64,30,6b** And it is a most grievous harm to disobey God, overstepping the bounds of the righteousness that belongs to free will.

**64,30,7** Hence, since man had been soiled and defiled by revolting from the decree of God, and had taken the impress of the stains of much wickedness, which the ruler of darkness, who is also the father of error, brought forth, having conceived toil, as the Scripture says, so that he might always be able to fill man with imaginings towards injustice and to move him, God the Almighty, seeing that through the plotting of the devil man had become an immortal evil, just as the devil too was a deceiver,

**64,30,8** for this reason made the garments of skin, clothing him as it were with mortality, so that through the dissolution of the body all the evil that had come to be in him might die.

**64,31,1a** It has already been discussed before and demonstrated that the garments of skin are not the bodies.

**64,31,1b** Yet let us declare it again (for it should not be said only once).

**64,31,2a** The first-formed man himself, before they were made, confesses that he has both bones and flesh, when, on seeing the woman brought to him, “This now is bone,” he cried out, “from out of my bones, and flesh from out of my flesh; she will be named woman, because out of her man she was taken.

**64,31,2b** On this account a man will forsake his father and mother and will cleave to his wife, and the two will be one flesh.”

**64,31,3** For I will not tolerate certain people who babble and shamelessly do violence to the Scripture, so that, forsooth, they may succeed in making the resurrection not one of flesh, positing intelligible bones and intelligible flesh and shifting them now one way, now another, up and down, with allegories;

**64,31,4** and this although Christ confirms the Scripture, that these things are to be taken as it is written, where, when the Pharisees inquire about divorcing a wife, he answers, “Have you not read that from the beginning the one who created made male and female, and said, On this account a man will forsake his father and mother”, and what follows.

**64,31,5a** For how is “Increase and fill the earth” to be taken of souls alone?

**64,31,5b** Or “God took dust out of the earth and moulded man”, which plainly appears to have been said in its proper sense of the body; for the soul did not acquire its substance from the dust and the heavier elements.

**64,31,6a** So it is established on every count, most truly, that man had been given a body before the garments of skin.

**64,31,6b** For all these things were said before he fell away, but what concerns the garments after he fell away.

**64,31,7** Hence let us go back to examine the matter in hand, having sufficiently shown that the garments of skin are not the bodies, but the mortality fashioned from the irrational among living creatures; for this is what remains.

**64,31,8a** Indeed, it is for this reason that he is banished outside the bounds of paradise.

**64,31,8b** For God did not cast him out because he did not wish him to pluck from the tree of life and eat (for he could have lived for ever, eating again of life), but so that the evil, as we have supposed, might not become immortal.

**64,31,9** Otherwise, why was he sending Christ from the heavens to the earth, if he wished at all that man, having never tasted life, should die for good?

**64,31,10a** But if the objector should say that God did this out of regret, his argument is weak, since it brings in a God who changes his mind.

**64,31,10b** Yet God is neither without understanding of what is to be nor a doer of evil, but both supremely good and foreknowing what is to come.

**64,31,11** So then it was not to keep him from being preserved for ever, once he had eaten from the tree of life, that he cast him out, but in order that sin might first be put to death through death, so that in this way, after the dying, when sin had melted away, the man might rise pure and taste of life.

**64,32,1a** Let no one, being dull-witted, run the risk of supposing that these things were said in some other sense.

**64,32,1b** For whoever lays it down outright that this flesh is not capable of receiving immortality is truly guilty of folly and blasphemes in his sickness.

**64,32,2** For why, after the making of the coats of skins, is Adam cast out, being prevented from eating from the tree of life, and from living, if it was altogether impossible for man to live for ever with a body?

**64,32,3a** For it is as one able not to die, should he take and taste of the life, that he is hindered.

**64,32,3b** For it says: “And the Lord God made coats of skins for Adam and for his wife, and he clothed them. And God said, ‘See, Adam has become as one of us, in knowing good and evil; and now, lest he should ever reach out his hand and take from the tree of life and eat, and he will live for ever.’

**64,32,3c** And the Lord God sent him out of the paradise of delight to work the earth out of which he had been taken, and drove Adam out.”

**64,32,4a** So then the body was able to live for ever and to be immortal, had it not been prevented from tasting of the life.

**64,32,4b** But it was prevented, so that sin, slain together with the body, might die, and the body might rise when sin had perished.

**64,32,5** So then, in order that man might not be, as I said, an immortal evil, having within himself sin ever-living and holding the mastery, inasmuch as it had sprouted within a body that was immortal and possessed food that was immortal, God for this reason declared him mortal, clothing him round with deadness.

**64,32,6** For this is what the coats of skins meant: that through the dissolution and the separation of the body sin might be wholly destroyed from below, root and stock, so that not even the smallest part of a root might be left, from which new shoots of sins might spring up again.

**64,33,1** For just as, when in the fine buildings of temples a fig tree [has] sprung up and has thriven to a great spread and size and has spread itself with its many-branching roots about all the joints of the stones, it does not cease from growing until it is torn out entirely, the stones being loosened in the places where it sprouted

**64,33,2** (for it is possible to fit the stones back again into the same places once the fig tree has been removed, so that the temple is preserved, no longer bearing within itself any of the evils that were destroying it, while the fig tree, torn out root and all, dies),

**64,33,3** in the same way God too, the craftsman, dissolved his own temple, man, which had sprouted sin like a wild fig tree, by the temporary assaults of death, “killing”, as it is written, “and making alive”, so that the flesh, after the sin has withered and died, may rise again from the same parts, like a temple renewed, immortal and unharmed, sin having perished utterly from its foundations.

**64,33,4** For while the body is still alive, before it dies, sin too must live along with it, hiding its roots within us, even if it was being checked from without by the prunings of correction and admonition; since otherwise it would no longer happen that one does wrong after being enlightened, sin having been altogether and cleanly removed from us.

**64,33,5a** But as it is, even after believing and coming to the water of purification, we are often found to be in sins.

**64,33,5b** For no one will boast of being so far outside sin as not even to have so much as thought of wrongdoing at all.

**64,33,6** So it has come about that sin is now curbed and lulled to sleep by faith, so as not to bear harmful fruits, yet it has not been taken away, even to the roots.

**64,33,7a** For now, here, we check its shoots, such as evil thoughts, “lest any root of bitterness springing up cause trouble”, not allowing the closed openings to open and unclose for the outgrowths, while the word, like an axe, cuts at its roots that grow beneath.

**64,33,7b** But then even the very thought of evil will be done away.

**64,34,1a** For the argument is not without the witness of the Scriptures, for those who wish to hear the truth sincerely, since the apostle is aware that sin’s root has not yet been wholly taken away from men, saying at one time, “For I know that there dwells in me, that is, in my flesh, no good; for the willing lies close at hand for me, but the accomplishing of the good does not; for I do not do the good that I will, but the evil that I do not will, this I practise.

**64,34,1b** But if what I do not will, this I do, it is no longer I who accomplish it, but the sin that dwells in me”;

**64,34,2** and at another time, “I delight in the law of God as regards the inward man; but I see a different law in my members, at war with the law of my mind and taking me captive to the law of sin that is in my members”.

**64,34,3** Thus it has not yet come about that sin is cut off root and stock from its roots, but that it lives (for it did not die completely; for how could it, before man had been clothed round with death?), so that, withering and wasting away together with him, it may itself perish utterly and be destroyed, like a plant, when [the stone] is loosened, in [the place] where, hiding its roots, as I said, it kept itself safe, while man rises again no longer having a “root” of “bitterness” coaxing within him.

**64,34,4** For death was taken up, in the manner of an antidote, by God, truly our Defender and Physician, to root out and do away with sin, so that the evil might not continue for ever in us, being immortal inasmuch as it had sprung up in immortals, and that we ourselves, maimed and fallen away from our own virtue like the sick, might not linger so for long, nursing the great sickness that comes from sin in bodies ever subsisting and immortal.

**64,34,5** Therefore God did well to devise this death, in the manner of a medicinal purging, for the salvation of both, of soul and of body alike, so that in this way we might be made wholly blameless and unharmed.

**64,35,1** Come then, since many examples are needed on matters of this kind, let us consider them chiefly from this point, not leaving off until the argument has first come through to a clearer interpretation and demonstration.

**64,35,2** It appears, then, just as if some consummate craftsman were to melt down again a beautiful statue fashioned by him of gold or some other material, with all its members harmoniously arranged for beauty, on suddenly seeing it maimed by some most wicked man who, unable from envy to bear that the statue should be comely, damaged it, reaping the vain pleasure of his jealousy.

**64,35,3** For observe, most wise Aglaophon, that if the craftsman wishes what he has laboured over with so much zeal and care not to be damaged at all and open to blame, he will be moved to melt it down and make it again such as it was before.

**64,35,4** But if he does not melt it down again nor refashion it, but lets it remain as it is, [only] treating and correcting it, then necessarily the statue, heated in the fire and hammered, can no longer be kept the same, but will be altered and pulled out of shape.

**64,35,5** For this reason, if he wishes the work to be wholly beautiful and faultless, it must be broken up and recast, so that all the disfigurements and alterations that came upon it from treachery and envy may themselves perish through the refashioning and recasting, while the statue is restored again to its own form, undamaged and unadulterated, most like itself.

**64,35,6** For it is not possible for the statue to perish while it is under the same craftsman, even if it is dissolved again into material, but it is possible for it to be restored; whereas the disfigurements and maimings can perish (for they melt away), but cannot be restored, because in every art the best craftsman does not look to what is uglier or to what may chance to result, but to the proportion and rightness of the thing being made.

**64,35,7a** For God appears to me to have ordered things in the same way in our case too.

**64,35,7b** For seeing man, the most comely work of his art, damaged by the envious plots of jealousy, he could not bear, being a lover of mankind, to leave him in that state, so that he might not be blemished for ever, having the fault immortal within himself, but dissolved him again into material, so that through the remoulding all the blemishes in him might be melted away and obliterated.

**64,35,8** For what the recasting of the statue is there, the dying and the dissolution of the body is here; and what the reshaping or re-adorning of the material is there, the rising is here,

**64,35,9a** just as the prophet Jeremiah too has himself already urged the same things in these words, saying thus: “And I went down to the potter’s house, and see, he was at work on the stones; and the vessel that he was making fell apart in his hands, and he made it again into another vessel, as it seemed good to him to make it.

**64,35,9b** And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: ‘Shall I not be able to do this to you as this potter does, O house of Israel?

**64,35,9c** Behold, like the potter’s clay, you are in my hands.’”

**64,36,1** For observe how, as I said, after man had transgressed, the great Hand was not content to leave its own work for ever as a spoiled thing to the evil one who had wrongfully marred it for envious reasons, but melted it down and kneaded it again into clay, as a potter remoulds a vessel, so that all the disfigurements and bruises in it might be done away through the remoulding, and the whole might become anew blamelessly pleasing.

**64,36,2a** “Or has not the potter power over the clay, to make from the same lump one vessel for honour and another for dishonour?”

**64,36,2b** That is to say (for this is what the apostle seems to me to be making altogether plain): has God no power, having remoulded and re-adorned each one individually from each one’s same material, to raise us up either to honour and glory or to dishonour and condemnation?

**64,36,2c** To the dishonour of those who have lived basely in sins, to the honour of those in righteousness.

**64,36,3** Just as was made plain to Daniel too, who says, “And many of those who sleep in the mound of the earth shall awake, these to everlasting life and these to reproach and everlasting shame; and those who understand shall shine forth as the brightness of the firmament”.

**64,36,4a** For what rests with us is not to make the root of wickedness disappear completely, but not to let it grow to its full spread and bear fruit.

**64,36,4b** For its total and complete removal and destruction, roots and all, comes about at God’s hand at the dissolution of the body, as has been said, while the partial one, so that it may bear no shoot, is by us.

**64,36,5** And for this reason the one who has nurtured it rather to growth and size, and has not, as far as lay with him, made it barren and crushed it down, must pay the penalty, because, though he was able and had the power for this, he chose to honour what harms in preference to what is profitable.

**64,37,1a** So let no one, being himself the cause, blame the divine nature with an unbridled tongue, as though it did not justly allot to each the recompense of wickedness or of virtue.

**64,37,1b** “For who are you, O man, who answer back to God?

**64,37,1c** Shall the thing moulded say to him who moulded it, ‘Why did you make me thus?’”

**64,37,2a** How so?

**64,37,2b** Since he chose wickedness by a self-governing will.

**64,37,2c** Therefore he will not say to God, who judges [by] the unvarying decrees of righteousness, ‘Why did you make me thus, condemned to suffer pain?’

**64,37,3** For observe how the apostle, like a javelin-thrower, hurling into the midst words formidably compacted, renders their elaboration obscure and veiled in the depth of what is read, yet most true and orthodox, and having nothing careless or ill-sounding.

**64,37,4** For to those who pore over the words not with diligence but in an ignoble way he sometimes seems to say things disconnected and discordant, but to those, on the other hand, who do so earnestly and with sober reasoning, of how much order and truth they are full.

**64,37,5a** To go through these matters with precision at present would be enough to make a work on its own.

**64,37,5b** For indeed it would be absurd to leave aside the examination of your case, for the sake of which we set out upon this one, and to pass on to other matters.

**64,37,6a** For these things have been said by us on account of the avenging justice upon those who do evil willingly.

**64,37,6b** Since, then, it has been shown most plainly that death was devised for man not for any evil but [...], he who does not pore over these matters carelessly must henceforth consider the resurrection of the body.

**64,37,7a** For how is death not beneficial, destroying the things that plunder our nature?

**64,37,7b** Even if, at the moment when it is applied, it seems [to be] burdensome, like a very harsh medicine to one who is sick.

**64,37,8** And so that we may not discuss the same things about the same matters many times over, come, let us confirm the argument from the Song of Deuteronomy as well, and so go on to treat what follows.

**64,38,1** For the saying spoken by God, “I will kill and I will make alive; I will strike and I will heal; and there is none who shall snatch out of my hands”, what else does it mean to teach than that it is for this reason that the body is first slain and dies, so that it may be raised again and live?

**64,38,2** It is struck first and shattered, so that it may be moulded again whole and sound.

**64,38,3** And nothing whatever at all has the strength to snatch out of his great and mighty hand for destruction and ruin: neither fire nor death nor darkness nor chaos nor corruption.

**64,38,4a** “For who,” it says, “will part us from Christ’s love” (who is interpreted as the hand and Word of the Father): “affliction, or hardship, or persecution, or hunger, or nakedness, or danger, or the sword?

**64,38,4b** As it is written, ‘For your sake we are being put to death all the day long; we were reckoned as sheep for slaughter’; but in all these things we more than conquer through him who loved us.”

**64,38,5a** Most truly; so that there may be fulfilled, as I said, the saying, “I will kill and I will make alive; I will strike and I will heal; and there is none who shall snatch” “us away from God’s love which is in Christ” to destruction.

**64,38,5b** Therefore also “we were reckoned as sheep for slaughter”, so that “having died to sin we may live to God”.

**64,38,6** And let these matters have been examined thus far; but what is connected with them must next be taken in hand again, as follows.

**64,39,1** If everything that is begotten is sick (for so the opponent puts it forward), both in its generation and in its nourishment (for from what is added, he says, it grows, and from what departs it is diminished), while the unbegotten things are healthy, because they are not sick, nor stand in need nor desire; and if begotten things desire both intercourse and food, and to desire is to be sick, while to have no need and no desire is to be healthy; and if begotten things are sick because they desire, while unbegotten things are not sick; and if things that are sick suffer either from an excess or from a deficiency of what is added and what departs, and what suffers both wastes away and perishes, which is why it is also begotten, and man is begotten — then man cannot be impassible and immortal.

**64,39,2a** Now the argument collapses even on these very grounds.

**64,39,2b** For if everything that comes to be or is begotten perishes (for it makes no difference to put it this way, since the first-formed too were not begotten but came to be), and the angels and the souls too have come to be (“who makes,” for it says, “his angels spirits”), then according to them the angels and the souls perish.

**64,39,3a** But neither the angels nor the souls perish.

**64,39,3b** For these are immortal and indomitable, as he who made them has determined that they should be.

**64,39,3c** Therefore man too is immortal.

**64,39,4a** And that [too] is not acceptable: to say that the universe will perish utterly, and that earth and air and heaven will not exist.

**64,39,4b** For the whole world will indeed be set on fire for purification and renewal, deluged by a fire coming down, yet it will not come to complete destruction and corruption.

**64,39,5a** For if it were better for the world not to be than to be, why did God choose the worse in making the world?

**64,39,5b** But God did nothing in vain or for the worse.

**64,39,5c** So God ordered the creation so that it should be and should abide,

**64,39,6** just as Wisdom too establishes, saying, “For God created all things that they might be, and the generations of the world are wholesome, and there is no poison of destruction in them”.

**64,39,7a** And Paul too testifies clearly, saying, “For the eager expectation of the creation awaits the revelation of the sons of God.

**64,39,7b** For the creation was subjected to futility, not willingly, but because of him who subjected it, in hope, because the creation itself also will be set free from the bondage of corruption into the freedom of the glory of the children of God”.

**64,39,8** “For the creation,” he says, “was subjected to futility”, but it looks to be freed from bondage of this kind, since by creation he here means this world; for it is not the things unseen that are in bondage to corruption, but these very things that are seen.

**64,39,9** So then the creation remains, renewed for the better and more comely, exulting and rejoicing over the children of God [at] the resurrection, for whose sake it now groans and travails together, itself too awaiting our redemption from the corruption of the body,

**64,39,10** so that, when we have been raised and have shaken off the deadness of the flesh, according to what is written, “Shake off the dust and arise and sit down, O Jerusalem”, and have been released from sin, it also will be freed from corruption, no longer in bondage to futility, but to righteousness.

**64,39,11a** “For we know,” it says, “that the whole creation groans together and travails together until now.

**64,39,11b** And not only so, but we ourselves too, having the firstfruits of the Spirit, we too groan within ourselves, awaiting the adoption, the redemption of our body”.

**64,39,12a** And Isaiah too: “For as the new heaven and the new earth, which I make,” he says, “remain before me, says the Lord, even so will your seed and your name be”.

**64,39,12b** And again, “Thus says the Lord who made the heaven, this God who formed the earth and made it, he himself marked it out; he did not make it for emptiness, but to be inhabited”.

**64,39,13a** For truly God did not create the universe for emptiness, nor to no purpose, for destruction, as is held by those whose thoughts are vain, but so that it should be and be inhabited and endure.

**64,39,13b** Therefore the earth and the heaven must exist again after the burning and the seething of all things.

**64,39,14a** Why this must be so would take a still longer argument than what has been said.

**64,39,14b** For neither will the universe be dissolved and resolved into its elements, into inert matter and into such a state as it was in before the ordering of the world, nor again into complete destruction and corruption.

**64,40,1** But if those on the opposing side should say: how then, if the universe does not perish, did the Lord say that the heaven and the earth will pass away, and the prophet that the heaven will perish like smoke and the earth will grow old like a garment?

**64,40,2** We shall say: since it is the custom of the Scriptures to give the name of destruction to the world’s change out of its present condition into something better and more glorious, inasmuch as its former form perishes in the change of all things to greater splendour; for there is no contradiction or absurdity in the divine words.

**64,40,3a** For “the form of this world passes away” is what was said, and not the world.

**64,40,3b** So it is the custom of the Scriptures to call destruction the change of the former form to what is better and at times more shapely,

**64,40,4a** just as when someone might speak of the destruction of the infant’s form in its change into the full-grown man, when the stature of the infant turns to size and beauty.

**64,40,4b** “For when I was an infant, I talked like an infant, I thought like an infant, I reasoned like an infant; but now that I have become a man, I have put an end to the things of the infant”.

**64,40,5** For one must expect the creation to be thrown into confusion, as though about to die, at the conflagration, so that it may also be created anew, but not to perish, so that we, made new, may dwell in the world made new without tasting grief, according to what is said in the hundred and third Psalm, “You will send forth your Spirit and they shall be created, and you will renew the face of the earth”, God thereafter governing the good temper of the surrounding air.

**64,40,6** For since there will be an earth after this age too, there must by every necessity also be those who will dwell on it, no longer to die, nor to marry and be born, but, like angels, doing what is best unchangeably in incorruption.

**64,40,7** Therefore it is idle talk to ask in what manner of existence the bodies will then be, when there will be neither air nor earth nor the other things.

**64,41,1** And in addition to what has been said, it is worth examining this too, how great an error it contains, if one must speak boldly, Aglaophon, when discussing matters of such weight.

**64,41,2** For having said that the Lord declared that those who obtain the resurrection will then be as angels, where the Sadducees test him, you went on, “But the angels, being without flesh, are at the summit of blessedness, and for this reason of glory too; and so we too, who are to be made equal to angels, must necessarily be stripped of flesh as they are”, without having considered, my excellent friend,

**64,41,3** that he who made and ordered the universe out of things that were not has distributed and ordered the nature of the immortals not only into angels and ministers, but also into principalities and authorities and thrones.

**64,41,4a** For the race of the angels is one thing and that of the principalities and authorities another, because the immortals are not one order and one constitution and tribe and clan, but there are races and tribes and distinctions.

**64,41,4b** And neither do the Cherubim, departing from their own nature, change into the form of the angels, nor again the angels into another.

**64,41,4c** For they must be both what they are and what they have become.

**64,41,5** Moreover man too, appointed [from] the ordering at the beginning to inhabit the world and to rule over all things in it, being immortal, will never be changed from being man, neither into the form of the angels nor into that of the others, since not even the angels are changed into that of the others, being transformed from their original form.

**64,41,6** For Christ came proclaiming not that man would be remoulded or refashioned into another nature, but into what he was from the beginning before he fell, when he was immortal.

**64,41,7** For each of the things that have come to be must remain in the proper place of its own order, so that everything may be full of everything: the heavens full of angels, the thrones full of authorities, the lights full of ministers, and the more divine places and the unmixed and pure lights full of the Seraphim, who stand in attendance on the great counsel that holds the universe together, and the world full of men.

**64,41,8** If, however, we allow that human beings are transformed into angels, the moment has come to say that the angels in their turn are transformed into authorities, and these into one thing and another, until the argument, having climbed upward, falls into peril.

**64,42,1** But neither did God, as though he had fashioned man as something poor or had failed in his moulding, afterwards decide to make him an angel, having changed his mind, as the poorest of craftsmen do; nor did he, wishing at the beginning to make an angel and being unable, mould a man.

**64,42,2a** For this is weak.

**64,42,2b** Why ever then did he make man a man and not an angel, if indeed his will was that man should become an angel and not a man?

**64,42,2c** As not being able?

**64,42,2d** [This] is blasphemous.

**64,42,3a** Or did he put off the better, making the worse?

**64,42,3b** This too is absurd.

**64,42,3c** For he neither fails in making what is good, nor puts it off, nor lacks power, but he has the ability in whatever way and at whatever time he wills, since he is power.

**64,42,4a** So then he created man from the beginning wishing him to be man.

**64,42,4b** But if he did so willingly, and what he wills is the good, and man is good, and what is called man is the compound of soul and body, then it follows that man will exist not apart from a body but with a body, so that man may not turn out to be something other than man.

**64,42,5a** For with God all the immortal kinds must be preserved.

**64,42,5b** And man too is immortal.

**64,42,5c** For Wisdom says, “God created man for incorruption, and he made him an image of his own everlastingness”.

**64,42,5d** So then the body does not perish; for man is of soul and body.

**64,43,1** Consider therefore how the Lord too, wishing to teach these very things, when the Sadducees refused belief in the flesh’s resurrection — for this is a doctrine of the Sadducees; and so, having concocted the parable about the woman and the seven brothers in order to repudiate the teaching on the resurrection of the flesh, they came to him; indeed the evangelist himself noted it, saying, “And there came to him also Sadducees, saying that there is no resurrection” —

**64,43,2a** Christ, then, if there were no resurrection of the flesh but only the soul were saved, would have agreed with them as people who thought well and rightly.

**64,43,2b** But as it is, he rebuffs them with the words, “In the resurrection they do not marry, nor are they given in marriage, but they are as angels in heaven”, not by lacking flesh,

**64,43,3** but by not marrying and being married, and by being henceforth in incorruption and lights, likened to angels in this respect, so that, as the angels in heaven, so we too in paradise, no longer giving our leisure to marriages or feasts, but to beholding God and cultivating life, with Christ presiding over us.

**64,43,4** For he did not say, “they will be angels”, but “as angels”, that is, “with glory and honour”, as it is written, “crowned, and differing by a little from the angels”, and being near to being angels.

**64,43,5a** As if he were to say, when there is fair order in the air and calm of night, and all things are adorned with the ethereal light of the moon: the moon shines like the sun.

**64,43,5b** And we would not say that he was testifying at all that the moon is the sun, but that it is like the sun,

**64,43,6a** just as that too which is not gold, but is by nature near to being gold, is said to be like gold and not gold.

**64,43,6b** But were it gold, it would be called, not like gold, but gold; since, however, it is not gold but [near] to being gold and to presenting the appearance of gold, it is called not gold but like gold.

**64,43,7** So too, when he states that in the resurrection the saints will be as angels, we do not take him to promise that in the resurrection the saints will be this very thing, angels, but near to being angels.

**64,43,8** So it is most unreasonable to say that, since Christ declared that the saints would appear as angels in the resurrection, these bodies therefore do not rise, although the word itself plainly indicates the very action of what takes place.

**64,43,9a** For resurrection is said not of what has not fallen, but of what has fallen and rises up; just as when the prophet too says, “And I will raise up David’s tent that has fallen”.

**64,43,9b** And the beloved tent of the soul fell, sinking down into “the earth of the mound”.

**64,43,9c** For it is not what does not die, but what dies, that is laid low.

**64,43,10a** But what dies is the flesh; for the soul is immortal.

**64,43,10b** And so, if the soul is immortal and the body is what is dead, those who assert a resurrection, yet not one of the flesh, deny that there is any resurrection, because it is not what stands but what has fallen and been laid low that rises up, according to what is written, “Shall he who falls not rise up, or he who turns away not turn back?”

**64,44,1** And that the soul is immortal the Lord taught openly, speaking both through himself and through Solomon: through himself in the narrative about the rich man and the pauper Lazarus, showing the one at rest in the bosom of Abraham after the laying aside of the body, and the other in torments, whom he brought in conversing with Abraham;

**64,44,2a** and through Solomon in what is entitled Wisdom, where it is written, “The souls of the just are in God’s hand, and torment shall never touch them.

**64,44,2b** In the sight of the foolish they appeared to be dead, and their exit was counted as affliction, and their journey from us as a shattering; yet they are at peace, and their hope is filled with immortality”.

**64,44,3a** Hence the resurrection is of the body, and not of the soul.

**64,44,3b** For one does not raise up the man who is standing, but the one who is lying down, just as they do not treat the man who is healthy either, but the one who is suffering.

**64,44,4a** But if anyone strains to say that the resurrection will be of the soul and not of the flesh, this is great folly and unreason.

**64,44,4b** For such a man must first show a corruption and dissolution of the soul, so that he may then show the resurrection as well, and appear not to be babbling but to be saying something clearly.

**64,44,5a** Nevertheless, let it be granted him to define the soul as mortal.

**64,44,5b** For one of two things must be posited here: either that the Lord declared the truth in teaching that the soul is immortal, and that it is a falsehood to say that it is destroyed; or that it is destroyed, and that Christ [speaks] falsehood in teaching that it is indestructible and immortal, both in the account concerning the rich man and the pauper and in the vision concerning Moses and Elijah.

**64,44,6a** But the Lord neither spoke anything idly nor lied.

**64,44,6b** For it was not a phantom or an apparition that he showed the apostles on the mountain, wishing to deceive them, Elijah and Moses, but what they truly were; so that, so to speak, even the slowest learner may learn that he firmly affirms the soul to be immortal and indestructible.

**64,45,1** So then it is of the flesh that the resurrection takes place, and not of the soul, so that David’s tent which fell into corruption may rise up, and, having risen and been rebuilt as in the days of old, may abide henceforth unharmed and never to fall,

**64,45,2** since God was not concerned to build the stone house of David, so that he might have a fine house in the kingdom of heaven, but the flesh, the tent of the soul, which he himself fashioned with his own hands.

**64,45,3a** And this is how you must consider it, most wise Aglaophon.

**64,45,3b** And you will certainly learn this most easily if you attend to the image of sleeping and rising.

**64,45,3c** For if sleeping comes about from being awake, and rising from sleeping, and this is a rehearsal of death and resurrection (“for to Sleep and Death, the twins”), then there must also be the returning of the flesh to life, from being dead to living, if indeed there is also the waking up from among the sleeping.

**64,45,4** For just as waking comes about from sleeping, and the sleeper does not by any means remain in the same posture, sleeping for ever, but rises again, so too living will come about from dying, and the one who has died, once he has died, does not by any means remain in the same state.

**64,45,5a** For if from sleeping comes waking, and from falling being raised up, and from being thrown down being rebuilt, what means is there of not expecting, also from what has been laid low, that it will rise, and from what has died, that it will come back to life?

**64,45,5b** For we too, not being deceived, acknowledge that the returning to life comes about from dead bodies.

**64,45,6a** And consider this, if you wish, not only from sleeping and rising, but also from seeds and plants, how in all of them the resurrection is proclaimed.

**64,45,6b** For observe the seeds, how they are cast into the earth “bare”, it says, and fleshless, and are given back again brought to fruition.

**64,45,6c** For if the seeds died and rotted, and from the seeds there no longer came the reviving and growing, what lot would there be but that all things should be used up in death?

**64,46,1a** But we shall pass over saying more on these matters for the present, most excellent Theophilus, and the rest of you who judge the discourse.

**64,46,1b** And let us take also what follows next upon these, how far it stands from what is fitting.

**64,46,2** For according to him, as he expounds the prophecy in the sixty-fifth Psalm in a forced and improper manner, God in turn puts the souls themselves, as into a snare, [into] the flesh, to undergo punishment for their sins; which belongs to absurdity rather than to orthodoxy.

**64,46,3** For if before the transgression, as we have already set out in what went before, the souls possessed a body, how are they put afterwards, after the transgression, into a body [as] into a snare, when there was no time in which they had sinned before taking the body?

**64,46,4** For it is not the part of a sensible man to say at one time that the souls sinned because of the body, and at another that, since they had sinned, it was for this reason that it became a bond and a snare for condemnation.

**64,46,5a** For if it was because of the body that they sinned, then the body was present with them from the beginning, even before the sin.

**64,46,5b** For how did they sin because of this, which did not yet exist?

**64,46,6a** But if the body itself is in turn considered a snare and fetters and a bond, then the cause is no longer the two together, but the soul alone.

**64,46,6b** For it is for one who has sinned, after the sinning, that bonds and snares and fetters are prepared.

**64,46,7** But in fact we acknowledged that the body is unable to be a bond of the soul, since the body co-operates towards both, towards the just and the unjust, whereas a bond restrains from doing wrong.

**64,46,8** So, as I say, one of two things: either it was from the beginning that we sinned along with a body, and no time appears in which we existed outside a body, and the body is a joint cause for the soul of both good things and evil; or we sinned while living without a body, and the body is entirely without responsibility for wickedness.

**64,46,9** But without a body the soul is not overcome by irrational pleasure; and the first-formed were overcome, lured by irrational pleasure; so the soul was with a body even before the sin.

**64,46,10** For as to its being inadmissible to consider it a bond that came about for punishment because of the transgression, so that the soul, carrying a corpse about, might have unmixed and unbroken torment, as they hold, I think I have already made this fully plain with every demonstration.

**64,46,11** Hence it is inconsistent and inadmissible to make the body a snare and bonds, and God the one who brings the souls into the snare to undergo punishment, casting them out from above, from the third heaven, because they disobeyed the commandment.

**64,46,12a** For looking to what would anyone believe the things so rashly said by them?

**64,46,12b** Though the Psalm is not so, even if they expound it by force.

**64,46,12c** But I will set out the very words it contains, so that the fabulous character of their exegesis may appear, since they are unwilling to understand the Scriptures rightly.

**64,46,13a** For the words spoken run, I suppose, somewhat as follows: “You tested us, O God; you refined us, as silver is refined.

**64,46,13b** You brought us into the snare; you laid afflictions on our back.

**64,46,13c** You set men over our heads.

**64,46,13d** We went through fire and water, and you brought us out into refreshment”.

**64,46,14a** And immediately they add: these things are spoken by the souls that were carried down into the snare, the body, as into an arena of contest, from the third heaven, where paradise is.

**64,46,14b** For “we went through fire and water”, he says, signifies either the passage of the soul through the womb into the world, since it abides in such great heat and moisture, or its fall from the heavens into this life, as [it] passes through the springs of fire and the waters above the back of the firmament.

**64,46,15a** Against these men I thought fit to make objection.

**64,46,15b** Come then, Aglaophon, make the reply yourself on their behalf, as to what they will say.

**64,47,1** For first, [that] paradise, from which we too were cast out in the first-formed man, is evidently a place singled out upon this earth, appointed for the saints for rest and a life free from pain, [this] is clear from the fact that the Tigris, the Euphrates and the other rivers which flow forth from there appear here, flooding our mainland with the outflows of their streams.

**64,47,2** For they do not come rushing down, pouring from the heavens above; since the earth would not have endured to receive so great a mass of water carried down all at once from on high.

**64,47,3a** But neither does the apostle suppose that paradise is in the third heaven, for those who know how to listen to subtle words; for he says, “I know [such a man] to have been caught up as far as the third heaven.

**64,47,3b** And I know a man of this sort – whether in the body or apart from the body, God knows – that he was caught up into paradise”.

**64,47,4a** He indicates that he saw two great revelations, having been plainly taken up twice, once as far as the third heaven and once into paradise.

**64,47,4b** For “I know such a man caught up as far as the third heaven” establishes that a revelation appeared to him individually when he was taken up to the third heaven;

**64,47,5** while “and I know”, added again, “a man of this kind, whether in the body or outside the body, [caught up] into paradise” shows that another revelation was in turn made manifest to him in paradise.

**64,47,6** So it is mere twittering and tragic bombast to say that the souls are thrown down from the heavens and pass through the springs of fire and the waters above the back of the firmament as they are carried into our world.

**64,47,7a** Since not even Adam was cast out of the heavens, but from the paradise planted eastward in Eden.

**64,47,7b** For the fall did not come before the embodiment, as we have sufficiently shown, nor again is this body a snare; but the fall comes about after the soul’s growing together with the body, because man is the two together; and the falling out of paradise is here.

**64,47,8** But he paid no careful attention at all to the argument, Aglaophon, but strained himself over matters whose understanding is not free of danger, having attempted to expound the Psalm according to the opinions of base men; about which we shall forgo saying more.

**64,48,1** For I wish, since we have once come to setting their depravity right, to lay bare for them the pretext of this prophecy too: “You tested us, O God; you refined us, as silver is refined”.

**64,48,2** The martyrs, tested thoroughly in the assaults of torments during their trials (for the prophecies are for the most part fulfilled with a view to our faith), and having contended gloriously and most mightily, give thanks, saying to God, “You tested us”, inasmuch as, in order that they might gain greater glory with him, he proved them through many toils, setting before them the truly Olympic contest to win.

**64,48,3** And observe how Solomon too speaks out about martyrs in plain harmony with these words (for the argument is not without the witness of other Scriptures as well), that “God put them to the proof and found them deserving of himself; like gold in the smelting-furnace he tested them, and like a whole burnt offering of sweet savour he accepted them, and at the time when they are visited [they will shine forth]”;

**64,48,4** having said before, “For even if in the sight of men they are punished, their hope is full of immortality, and having been chastened a little they will receive great benefits”.

**64,48,5a** But also in the hundred and twenty-third Psalm, the words “Had it not been that the Lord was among us, when men rose up against us, then they would have swallowed us up alive; then the water would have drowned us; our soul passed through a torrent; then our soul passed through the irresistible water.

**64,48,5b** Blessed be the Lord, who did not give us as prey to their teeth.

**64,48,5c** Our soul was rescued like a sparrow from the snare of the hunters.

**64,48,5d** The snare was broken, and we were rescued” — those who sing them are the martyrs.

**64,48,6** And the two choirs of the gloriously victorious martyrs, the one of the new covenant and the other of the old, sending up in concord an antiphonal hymn to God, the champion and King of all: “You tested us, O God; you refined us as silver is refined; you brought us into the snare; you laid afflictions on our back” — the tribunal of the nations, or the trials, in which, pressed hard and refined by fire, they were mightily tested;

**64,48,7** for “Test me,” it says, “O Lord, and try me; refine my reins and my heart”.

**64,48,8** Let Abraham too say it, his heart burned within him over his only son, yet preferring the command of God to [the child], after hearing, “Abraham, spare your son”, as he threw away the knife: “You tested us, O God; you refined us as silver is refined”.

**64,48,9a** Let Job too say it, after the corruption had oozed away from him, and he had been reproached by his friends and had suffered pain in his body, having heard God speaking to him through the whirlwind, “Or do you think that I have dealt with you otherwise than that you might be shown to be righteous?

**64,48,9b** You laid afflictions before us, O Lord, so that you might test us like gold in a furnace”.

**64,48,10a** Let the three young men in the furnace say it too, refreshed with dew so that they might not be burnt up by the fire: “You tested us, O God; you refined us, as silver is refined.

**64,48,10b** We went through fire and water, and you brought us out into refreshment”.

**64,48,11** Grant to me as well, Methodius, O God almighty, great and everlasting, Father of Christ, to say, when in your day I have passed through the fire without pain and have escaped the onrush of the waters changed into a burning nature: “I went through fire and water, and you brought me out into refreshment”.

**64,48,12a** For yours is this promise to those who love you: “If you pass through water, I am with you, and rivers shall not overwhelm you; and if you pass through fire, you shall not be burnt; flame shall not burn you”.

**64,48,12b** But this is enough on the exposition of the Psalm.

**64,49,1** Further, that point must be examined in which, under the illusion of manifold deceptions, like men asleep, they declare that the apostle said, “But I was once alive without the law”, and they cry out [that] by the life before the commandment he meant the existence in the first-formed man [in paradise] before the body, as what follows establishes, “But I am fleshly, sold under sin”;

**64,49,2a** since man could not otherwise be ruled and lorded over by evil, having been sold to it through the transgression, unless he had become fleshly.

**64,49,2b** For the soul by itself, he says, cannot be seized by sin.

**64,49,2c** Therefore he went on pointedly with, “But I am fleshly, sold under sin”, having said before, “But I was once alive without the law”.

**64,49,3** Now when they said these things, wonder and amazement then stole over the many; but now, since the truth has already peeped forth into the clearest light, they are seen not only to be wandering far off, but even to be climbing up to the very height of blasphemy;

**64,49,4a** for having granted that the souls lived bodilessly before the commandment, and having supposed that by themselves they are wholly unseizable by sin, they overturned their argument again, and themselves much more; for they contrive that bodies [were given] to them afterwards for punishment, because they sinned before the body.

**64,49,4b** And indeed revilings too occurred to them: they liken the body to bonds and fetters, and other senseless things to say [...].

**64,49,5a** But as it is, what has been said holds entirely the opposite.

**64,49,5b** For the soul must exist with a body before the sin; since if the soul were by itself unseizable by sin, it would not have sinned at all before the body.

**64,49,6a** But if it sinned, it is no longer by itself unseizable by sin, but rather easily caught and easily seized.

**64,49,6b** Therefore it will also sin again, even if it does not take this body, just as it sinned even before taking it.

**64,49,7a** And why did it take a body at all afterwards, after it had sinned?

**64,49,7b** Or what need had it of a body?

**64,49,7c** If, then, it was in order to be tormented and suffer pain, how is it that it rather lives in luxury and licentiousness with the body?

**64,49,8a** And how does free will too appear to have its being in this world?

**64,49,8b** For it rests with us to believe and not to believe here; with us to do right and to sin; with us to do good and to do evil.

**64,49,9** But how can the judgment too still be put forward as something to be expected in the future, in which God renders to each according to his works and according to his practices, and not be considered as already present, if indeed for the soul to be born and to come into a body is already to be judged and to receive requital, and to die and to be separated from the body is to be set free and to come into refreshment, because, according to you, having sinned before the body, it is put into the body as into condemnation and sentence?

**64,49,10** But the argument has shown sufficiently and abundantly that it is inadmissible to consider the body a place of torture and fetters of the soul.

**64,50,1a** It would have been enough, then, having shown from the Scripture itself that the first-formed man was of soul and body [even] before the transgression, to end the discussion of these matters at this point.

**64,50,1b** And I am now going through these things summarily, [...] striving only so far as to set straight the starting-points of their arguments, so that I may not overstep the due proportion of my discourse.

**64,50,2** For you may now see, judges, that the saying “But I was once alive without the law”, spoken in the Epistle to the Romans, cannot apply to the existence of the soul before the body, as they hold, as the words that follow it show; even though this noble physician of texts, removing what follows, forcibly tried to remould the apostle’s meaning to suit his own opinion, having suffered something quite incurable and childish.

**64,50,3** For instead of keeping the members of the bodies in their proper joinings and fittings, so that the whole shape of the body might be complete according to nature, he mutilated it, passing over the sequence of the Scripture, like a Scythian unsparingly cutting up the limbs of some enemy to destroy him.

**64,50,4a** So be it: how then, they will say, did the apostle declare these things, if you have shown that they do not stand so?

**64,50,4b** Because, I would say, having laid down that the commandment is law (for let it first be granted, on your supposition, that he called the commandment law).

**64,50,4c** He did not on that account also suppose that the first-formed lived bodilessly before the commandment, but without sin.

**64,50,5a** For short too was the time from their creation until the commandment, in which they lived without sin, not outside a body but with a body.

**64,50,5b** At any rate, immediately after the commandment they were cast out, having spent a very short time in paradise.

**64,50,6** But if anyone, seizing on the saying which runs, “For when we were in the flesh, the passions of sins which were through the law were at work in our members”, should think that he disparages the flesh and rejects it, and should adduce whatever else of the same sort has been said,

**64,50,7a** such as “that the law’s righteous decree might be fulfilled in us who walk not according to the flesh but according to the Spirit”, or “For those who are according to the flesh mind the things of the flesh, but those who are according to the Spirit the things of the Spirit.

**64,50,7b** For the mind of the flesh is death, but the mind of the Spirit is life and peace, because the mind of the flesh is enmity towards God; for it does not submit itself to God’s law, for neither can it; [and those who are in the flesh are unable to please God].

**64,50,7c** But you are not in the flesh, but rather in the Spirit”, it must be said to him:

**64,50,8** was the apostle, then, already departed from life, and those to whom he was writing these things, if it was not the life according to the flesh that he was now rejecting but the flesh, or was he still in the flesh?

**64,50,9a** But it must not be said that he sent these things while not being in the flesh.

**64,50,9b** For it is plain that both he himself and those to whom he was writing these things were in the flesh.

**64,50,9c** How then does he say, “For when we were in the flesh, the passions of sins which were through the law were at work in our members”, as if neither he nor those to whom he was writing these things were still in the flesh?

**64,50,10a** So then he is speaking of the life lived in incontinence and not of the flesh itself.

**64,50,10b** For it is his custom to call the man who lives thus fleshly, just as he also calls soulish the man who has grown callous to gazing upon the truth and the illumination of the mystery.

**64,50,11** Otherwise let them say that not even the soul can be saved at all; for it is written, “But the soulish man does not accept what is of the Spirit of God; for to him it is folly; but the spiritual man examines all things”.

**64,50,12** [So], just as in that place a soulish and a spiritual man are brought forward, and the spiritual belongs to those being saved [...], and the soulish to those perishing, and yet not on this account does the soul perish while [the] other things besides the soul are saved, so here too, in saying that those who are fleshly and are in the flesh perish and cannot please God, he contends for the doing away not of the flesh but of the life according to the flesh.

**64,50,13** And proceeding further, when he has said, “And those who are in the flesh are unable to please God”, he at once adds, “But you are not in the flesh, but rather in the Spirit,

**64,50,14a** if indeed the Spirit of Christ dwells in you”; and a little after, “But if the Spirit of him who raised Jesus from the dead dwells in you, he who raised Christ from the dead will also give life to your mortal bodies through his Spirit that dwells in you.

**64,50,14b** So then, brothers, we are under obligation not to the flesh, to live according to the flesh.

**64,50,14c** For if you live according to the flesh, you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the deeds of the body, you will live”.

**64,50,14d** For it must be noted that it was the impulse of the body towards pleasures, and not the body, that he affirmed is put to death.

**64,51,1a** But if again they object, saying: how then is “the mind of the flesh” said to be “enmity towards God?

**64,51,1b** For it does not submit itself to God’s law, for neither can it”, it must be said that here too they fall short.

**64,51,2** For it was not the flesh itself, but the mind of the flesh, that he laid down as unable to be subject to the law of God, this being something other than the flesh.

**64,51,3a** As if he were saying: the dross of silver that has not been well purified, so as to become a serviceable vessel, is not subject to the craftsman.

**64,51,3b** For neither can it be; for it must first be cast out and melted away.

**64,51,4** And not on that account was he declaring that the silver cannot be worked into a useful vessel, but the brassy matter in the silver and its other hardness.

**64,51,5** So too, in saying ‘the mind of the flesh’, it was not the flesh that he declared unable to submit to God’s law, but the mind that is in the flesh, meaning, that is, its impulse towards its incontinences; which elsewhere he at one time called the “old leaven” of wickedness and evil, urging that it be utterly done away from us, and at another time “a law warring against the law of the mind and taking captive”.

**64,51,6a** Since, if it were the flesh itself that he called unable to submit to God’s law, then first of all we could incur no blame before a just judge when living licentiously or robbing or practising or doing any of the other things done through the body, since the flesh would be unable to be subject to the law of God.

**64,51,6b** For how could the body be blameworthy for living according to the nature that belongs to it?

**64,51,7a** But neither could the body otherwise be brought to chastity or virtue, being by nature not subject to the good.

**64,51,7b** For if the flesh is by nature such as to be unable to submit to God’s law, and the law of God is righteousness and self-control, then it ought to be wholly unable either to keep virginity or to practise continence in any way at all.

**64,51,8** But if they keep virginity and practise continence, and they practise continence when they subject the body (for otherwise it is impossible to abstain from sin), then it is not after all true that the body is unable to submit to God’s law.

**64,51,9a** Otherwise how could John have subjected his body to chastity?

**64,51,9b** Or how Peter to holiness?

**64,51,9c** Or those that came after, to righteousness?

**64,51,9d** And how does Paul too say, “So do not let sin reign in your mortal body so as to obey its desires, and do not offer your members to sin as weapons of unrighteousness, but offer yourselves to God as those who are alive from among the dead, and your members to God as weapons of righteousness”

**64,51,10** and again, “For as you presented your members as slaves to uncleanness and to lawlessness for lawlessness, so now present your members as slaves to righteousness for sanctification”?

**64,52,1** So then he knew that this tabernacle can be set straight and incline towards what is good, so that the sins within it may be put to death.

**64,52,2a** Since how else can a man, in our case too, be enslaved to righteousness, unless, having first subjected the members of his flesh so that they obey not sin but righteousness, he lives worthily of Christ?

**64,52,2b** Since sinning and not sinning come about through the body, the soul using it as an instrument either for virtue or for vice.

**64,52,3** For if “neither fornicators nor idolaters nor adulterers nor the effeminate nor men who lie with males nor thieves nor the covetous nor drunkards nor revilers nor robbers can inherit the kingdom of God”,

**64,52,4** and these things are accomplished and strengthened through the body, and no one is justified unless he first masters them, and he masters them who turns towards self-control and faith, it follows that the body submits to God’s law; for the law of God is self-control.

**64,52,5** So it was not the flesh that the apostle said is not subject to the good, but the mind of the flesh, that is, doing away with its impulse towards incontinences, just as also with the soul’s inclination towards injustice.

**64,52,6** At any rate, striving that we be cleansed even of the incontinence of gluttony, and teaching that such appetites and pleasures be utterly destroyed

**64,52,7** and putting to shame those who think that this luxury and extravagance is living, who “consider the belly a god”, [saying], “Let us eat and let us drink, for tomorrow we are to die”, like gluttonous beasts busied below with fodder alone and the tables, and having said, “Food is for the belly and the belly for food;

**64,52,8a** and God will bring to nothing both the one and the other”, he added, “The body, however, is not meant for fornication but is for the Lord, and the Lord is for the body.

**64,52,8b** And God both raised the Lord and will raise us up through his power.

**64,52,8c** Or do you not know that our members are members of Christ?

**64,52,8d** Shall I then take Christ’s members and make them a harlot’s members?

**64,52,8e** Far from it.

**64,52,8f** Or do you not know that he who cleaves to a harlot is one body?

**64,52,8g** For the two, it says, shall become one flesh.

**64,52,8h** But he who cleaves to the Lord is one spirit.

**64,52,8i** Flee fornication.

**64,52,8j** Every sin that a man commits is outside the body.

**64,52,8k** But he who commits fornication sins against his own body.

**64,52,8l** Or do you not know that your bodies are a temple of the Holy Spirit within you, whom you have from God?

**64,52,8m** And you are not your own; for you were bought with a price.

**64,52,8n** Glorify God, then, in your body”.

**64,53,1** For observe that it was because this body is able [to submit] to God’s law and to receive immortality, if it is cleansed of the incontinence of the things that inflame it, not being stained at all by the lawless chafings of lust, that the apostle uttered these things.

**64,53,2** For what else is “that which cleaves” “to the harlot”, and approaches her and becomes one flesh with her through the joining of members and the union, besides this outward body, through which all these sins of intercourse and burning are accomplished?

**64,53,3** Hence also “every sin that a man commits is outside the body, but he who commits fornication sins against his own body”.

**64,53,4** For pride and unbelief and wrath and hypocrisy are the soul’s sins, while fornication and lust and luxury are the body’s; with which neither will the soul fly up to the truth nor the body be subjected to the decrees of self-control, but the two alike will slide away from Christ’s kingdom.

**64,53,5** And so, if our bodies, kept firmly in sanctification, are “a temple of the Spirit that dwells within us”, and if “the Lord is in the body”, while the body’s members are “members of Christ”, then the body submits to the divine law and “is able to inherit the kingdom of God”.

**64,53,6** For “he who raised Christ from the dead will give life”, it says, “also to your mortal bodies through his Spirit that dwells in you”, “so that this mortal may put on immortality and this corruptible incorruption, and death be swallowed up in victory”.

**64,53,7** For the apostle was not here treating of some other body of ours, but of this one which dies and is put to death, through which it is possible both to commit fornication and to sin in other ways.

**64,54,1** But if they suppose that body differs from flesh (so that we may allow them to reason on these matters too), and should consider the body to be something else, such as [that proper] to the soul itself, which is not seen, and the flesh to be this outward thing that is seen, it must be said that not only Paul and the prophets know that this flesh is named body, but also other [outside] philosophers, among whom above all the exactness of names has been carefully observed.

**64,54,2a** For this whole bulk of our tabernacle is not, properly speaking, called flesh at all, if they wish to examine these matters too with knowledge, but rather some part of the whole is, just as bones or sinews or veins are, while the whole is called body.

**64,54,2b** Hence physicians too, who have treated the nature of bodies with precision, recognise this visible thing as body.

**64,54,3a** But Plato too himself recognises this very [body] as body.

**64,54,3b** In the Phaedo, at any rate, Socrates said, “Do we consider death to be anything other than [the] separation of the soul from the body?”

**64,54,3c** And that [the] being dead is this: that the body, released from the soul, has come to be apart, alone by itself, and the soul apart [from] the body?

**64,54,4** For does not blessed Moses (for we have now come to the Lord’s Scriptures as well) know how to name as body this which we see, saying in the purifications, “And he shall wash his garments”, he who has touched the unclean thing, “and shall bathe his body in water” “and shall be unclean until evening”?

**64,54,5a** And what of Job?

**64,54,5b** Does not he too know how to call this which is put to death body, saying, “And my body is befouled with the rottenness of worms”?

**64,54,6a** But Solomon too says that “into a soul that devises evil wisdom will not come, nor will it dwell in a body that is in debt to sin”.

**64,54,6b** And in Daniel it is said concerning the martyrs, “The fire did not prevail over their body”, “and the hair of their head was not scorched”.

**64,54,7a** And the Lord in the gospel: “Therefore I say to you: do not be anxious about what you shall eat or what you shall put on.

**64,54,7b** Is not the soul more than food, and the body more than clothing?”

**64,54,8** And the apostle too, saying, “So do not let sin reign in your mortal body”, establishes that he knows this which we wear about us as body; and again, “But if the Spirit of him who raised Jesus dwells in you, he who raised Christ from the dead will also give life to your mortal bodies”;

**64,54,9a** and again, “Should the foot say, ‘Because I am not a hand, I am not of the body’, it is not for that reason not of the body”; and again, “And not being weak in faith, Abraham did not consider his own body, now dead”.

**64,54,9b** And again, “For we must all be made manifest before the judgment seat of Christ, so that each may receive the things done through the body, according to what he has done”;

**64,54,10** and again, “The letters are weighty and strong, but the presence of the body is weak”; and again, “I know of a man in Christ fourteen years back; whether in the body, I do not know; whether outside the body, I do not know”; and again, “So husbands ought to love their own wives as their own bodies”; and again, “May the God of peace himself sanctify you wholly, and may your spirit and soul and body be kept whole, blamelessly, at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ”.

**64,54,11** But they, having assuredly understood none of these things, supposed that the apostle is carried about in tossing and confusion, as though the meanings of his words had no standing and settled seat, but were floating up and down and uttering contradictory things, at one time that the flesh rises, at another that it does not rise.

**64,55,1** Hence, so that not even one of the points put forward may be left out, in order that we may cut out [the] hydra completely, I will return again to the matter in hand; for in order, as I promised, setting down also the other heads on which they raise further difficulties, and showing what must be said in reply to them, I will present him as having said these things too consistently and in agreement with the faith concerning the resurrection of the flesh.

**64,55,2a** Let us see, then, what we were first led to say with regard to the apostle.

**64,55,2b** For the saying spoken by him, “But I was once alive without the law”, signifies our life of old in paradise, as we supposed from the beginning, before the commandment, not outside a body but with a body, in the first-formed,

**64,55,3a** since before the commandment was given, “God took dust from the earth and moulded man”.

**64,55,3b** And we lived without desire, not knowing at all the assaults of irrational desire forcing us towards incontinence by the alluring distractions of pleasures.

**64,55,4a** For that which has no defined rule according to which it must live, nor the self-governing power of reasoning as to which way of life it chooses to follow, so as to be justly either honoured or blamed, must be said to be free of every charge, because it cannot desire those things from which it has not been restrained.

**64,55,4b** And even if it does desire, it will not be accused.

**64,55,5a** For desiring does not apply to things present and lying within one’s power, but to things present indeed, yet not within one’s power.

**64,55,5b** For how does anyone desire and long for that from which he is not restrained and of which he is not in want?

**64,55,5c** Hence it was for this reason that “I had not known desire”, [he said], “if the law had not said, ‘You shall not desire’”.

**64,55,6a** For having heard, “But of the tree of knowing good and evil you shall not eat of it; and in the day that you eat of it, you shall die by death”, then they conceived the desire and came to crave it.

**64,55,6b** For the one who desires does not desire what he has and what he controls and uses, but what he is restrained from and deprived of and does not have.

**64,55,7a** Therefore it was well said, “I had not known desire, if the law had not said, ‘You shall not desire’”, that is, if it had not been said, “You shall not eat of it”.

**64,55,7b** For from this sin gained a place and an occasion to enter in, to make sport of me and to lead me astray.

**64,56,1** For when the commandment had been given, the devil gained a hold through the commandment to work desire in me, urging me on and skilfully calling me forth to fall into longing for the forbidden things.

**64,56,2** “For without the law sin is dead”; that is, the commandment being neither given nor as yet in existence, sin was inactive; “but I was alive” blamelessly before the commandment, not having a defined rule and ordinance according to which I had to live, slipping away from which I would sin.

**64,56,3** “But when the commandment came, sin came alive again, and I died; and the commandment that was for life, this was found to be for death to me”, because after God had given the law and distinguished what must be done and what must not be done, the devil worked desire in me.

**64,56,4** For the exhortation and command of God, which was given to me for life and incorruption, so that by obeying it and living in accordance with it I might have a life unharmed and most blessed for ever, ever flourishing towards immortality, and joy, turned out, for me who set it aside, to be for death and condemnation,

**64,56,5** since the devil, whom he has here called sin because he is the maker and inventor of sin, taking occasion through the commandment for disobedience, deceived me and, having deceived me, killed me, bringing it about that I became liable to the sentence, “In whatever day you eat of it, you shall die by death.”

**64,56,6a** “So the law is holy, and the commandment of God is holy and just and good”, since it was given not for harming but for saving.

**64,56,6b** For let us not suppose that God does anything at all that is useless or harmful.

**64,56,7a** What then?

**64,56,7b** “Did that which is good become death to me” – the ordinance that was given to me as one that would be the cause of the greatest good?

**64,56,7c** “By no means.”

**64,56,7d** For it was not the commandment of God that became the cause of my being enslaved to corruption and of my taking the impress of the dooms of destruction, but the devil, who, so that he might be made manifest, contrived the evil for me through the good, so that the inventor and craftsman of sin might become and be shown to be “sinful beyond measure”, and that he who [wickedly] directed what was contrary to the commandment of God might be set apart from the good.

**64,56,8a** “For we are aware that the law is spiritual”; hence it is in no way the cause of harm to anyone, since spiritual things dwell far away from irrational desire and sin.

**64,56,8b** “But I am of flesh, sold under sin”, that is:

**64,56,9** but I, being fleshly and set between the evil and the good as one possessing free will, so that the choosing of what I wish might rest with me (for “I have set,” he says, “before your face life and death”), inclining towards disobeying the spiritual law, that is, the commandment, and obeying the material one, that is, the counsel of the serpent, have been sold to the devil through such a choice, having fallen under sin.

**64,56,10** Hence from that point evil, having besieged me, both settles in and takes up its citizenship, having made its home within my flesh, just as a drone in a bee’s honeycomb, often flying round about it and buzzing, since the penalty laid upon me for setting the commandment aside was to be sold to evil.

**64,56,11** Therefore also “I do not know what I do”, when I think what I do not wish; for the saying “What I do I do not know”, and “I do what I hate”, is to be taken not of carrying the evil through to completion and committing it, but of merely having it in mind, since unbecoming thoughts very often fly at us and fill us with fancies of what we do not wish, the soul being drawn round about many things by its thoughts.

**64,57,1a** For the thinking or not thinking of unseemly things does not lie wholly in our power, but the using or not using of the thoughts does.

**64,57,1b** For we cannot prevent the thoughts, which are breathed into us from outside to test us, from falling upon us; but we are able not to be persuaded by them and not to make use of them.
