**64,57,2** Otherwise how could the apostle have done, in no small measure, the evil that displeased him, and done least of all the good that pleased him, unless he was speaking of alien thoughts, which we sometimes think even when we do not wish to, not knowing from what cause?

**64,57,3a** These must be overturned and muzzled, so that they may not spread and gain the mastery over the bounds of the soul.

**64,57,3b** For it is impossible for the good to spring up in us while these find room at leisure within us.

**64,57,4a** Rightly, then, did he say, “For what I do I do not know”, “for I do not practise what I wish, but what I hate, this I do.”

**64,57,4b** For our wish is that things unfitting and unlawful should not even enter our thoughts, since this is the perfect good: to restrain oneself not only from doing it but also from thinking of it.

**64,57,5a** And what we wish, this good, does not come about, but what we do not wish, the evil.

**64,57,5b** For often, even against our will, countless [thoughts] about countless things visit our heart and tread upon it, filling us with curiosity and irrational meddlesomeness.

**64,57,6** Therefore the wish not to think of them is present, but the accomplishing of their removal, so that they may not come up again into our reasoning, [is not,] since this does not lie in our power, as I said, but only the making some use of them or not making use of them.

**64,57,7a** So the sense of the saying “For I do not do the good that I wish” is this: for I wish not to think of what harms me, since this is a good wholly without blemish, according to the saying, “wrought without blame, four-square in hands and mind.”

**64,57,7b** And this is “The good that I wish I do not do, but the evil that I do not wish I practise”: I do not wish to think, and I think whatever I do not wish.

**64,57,8a** And [we must] consider whether it was not for these very reasons that David too pleaded with God, distressed that he also thought what he did not wish: “From my hidden faults cleanse me, and from alien things spare your servant.

**64,57,8b** If they do not gain the mastery over me, then I shall be blameless and shall be cleansed from great sin.”

**64,57,9** And the apostle himself too, in another place: “casting down reasonings and every height that is lifted up against the knowledge of God, and taking captive every thought into the obedience of Christ.”

**64,58,1** But if anyone, daring to come to close quarters with the argument, should reply that the apostle in fact teaches that we practise the evil which we hate and which we do not wish not only in thinking it but also in doing it and carrying it out,

**64,58,2** because it has been said, “For I do not do what I wish, the good, but what I do not wish, the evil, this I practise”, we shall surely ask him, if he who says this speaks the truth, to make clear what the evil was which the apostle hated and did not wish to do, yet did, and [the] good which he wished to do, yet did not do, but, the other way about, as often as he wished to do the good, so often did not the good which he wished, but the evil which he did not wish.

**64,58,3** For was it that, not wishing to worship idols but to worship God, he was unable to worship God, which he wished, but able to worship idols, which he did not wish; or that he did not practise self-control, which he wished, but practised licentiousness, which he loathed?

**64,58,4** And, in short, carousing, and indeed debauchery and rage, wrongdoing and all the other works of wickedness – did he do these, which he did not wish, and not do what he wished, righteousness and holiness?

**64,58,5a** And yet he himself in the churches, eagerly rousing everyone not to transgress, charges that not only those who do and practise wicked things are kept for destruction and wrath, but also those who approve of them in these things, striving that righteousness be practised in us unmixed with sin.

**64,58,5b** For every sin and practice attains its completion through the flesh.

**64,58,6** At any rate, often in his epistles, openly teaching us to shun and hate these very things, he says, “Do not be deceived: neither fornicators nor idolaters nor adulterers nor the effeminate nor men who lie with males nor thieves nor the greedy nor drunkards, no revilers, no robbers will inherit the kingdom of God”;

**64,58,7** and, most important of all, urging us to flee sin altogether and in every way and to shake it off, he declares, “Be imitators of me, just as I also am of Christ.”

**64,58,8a** So it was not the doing and practising of the things mentioned before, which he did not wish, that he put forward, but merely the thinking of them; otherwise how could he have been an exact imitator of Christ?

**64,58,8b** But as it is, since bestial thoughts often fall upon us, filling us, now in one way and now in another, with desire and irrational curiosity, “like the many tribes of thronging flies”, he said, “For what I do not wish, this I do”; and these we must boldly scare away from the soul, in no way inclining to do what they whisper to us.

**64,58,9** For in order that we may be admitted into the kingdom of heaven, having been tested in all pleasures and griefs, to see whether we do not change but everywhere, like pure gold tried through fire, do not depart from our own virtue, this comes about: [the] troubling of the mind by many things.

**64,58,10** Hence we must stand in array against them nobly, like the best soldiers, who, when they perceive that they are besieged by the enemy, caring nothing for the archery and their other missiles, rush eagerly upon them to save the city, sparing no zeal, until they turn their massed ranks to flight and drive them out beyond the borders.

**64,58,11** For you see how the thoughts, because of the sin that dwells in us, gather together against us from outside, like rabid dogs or fierce and bold robbers, ever urged on against us by the tyrant and ruler of unrighteousness, who tests us whether we are able to resist them and to stand in array against them.

**64,59,1a** Come then, O soul, lest you give way and be snatched away by them, and we shall have no ransom to give for you.

**64,59,1b** “For what will a man give,” he says, “in exchange for his soul?”

**64,59,2** It would indeed be good, and most delightful, if we did not have those who work against us and fight us; but since this is impossible, and what we wish we cannot have (for we wish not to have those who drag us into passions) – for then one could be saved without sweat – and what we wish does not come about, but what we do not wish (for we must be tested, as I said), let us not give way, O soul, let us not give way to the evil one,

**64,59,3** but, “taking up the whole armour of God”, which shields us and fights before us, “let us clothe ourselves in the breastplate of righteousness and shoe our feet with the preparation of the gospel, and in all things let us take up the shield of faith, with which we shall have power to put out all the flaming arrows of the wicked one, and salvation’s helmet and the Spirit’s sword, which is God’s word, so that we may be able to stand firm against the devil’s scheming”, “and to cast down reasonings and every height that is lifted up against the knowledge of God”: “for our wrestling is not against blood and flesh.”

**64,59,4a** I say these things for this reason, that this is the manner of the apostle’s writings.

**64,59,4b** For there is much that could be shown about each of the things said in this epistle, how orthodox and carefully considered it is; but it would be a long task to go through each of them in this way.

**64,59,4c** For I have chosen now to show only its pattern and its intention.

**64,59,5a** “For what I wish, this I do not practise, but what I hate, this I do.

**64,59,5b** [But if what I do not wish, this I do,] I consent to God’s law, that it is good; but now it is no longer I that do it, but the sin that dwells in me.

**64,59,5c** For I know that the good does not dwell in me, that is, in my flesh”, rightly saying so.

**64,59,6a** For you remember how we defined it in what went before; even though, in my haste to go through everything quickly, I am rather lingering, since the course of my discourse has become longer than I expected – a course which I must now be eager to complete.

**64,59,6b** For otherwise we shall never come to the end of what lies before us.

**64,60,1** Well then, we were saying that from the time when it happened that man, led astray, set the commandment aside, if you remember, from then on sin, having taken its origin from the disobedience, made its home in him.

**64,60,2** For in this way discord first broke in upon us, and we became full of convulsive throbbings and unbecoming thoughts, emptied of the inbreathing of God and filled with material desire, which the many-coiled serpent breathed into us, when we fell short of completing the circuit of God’s command.

**64,60,3** Therefore God also devised death on our behalf for the destruction of sin, so that, having sprung up in us while we were immortal, it might not, as I said, be immortal.

**64,60,4** Hence the apostle, in saying, “For I know that the good does not dwell in me, that is, in my flesh”, means to point to the sin that made its home in us from the transgression through desire, from which, like young shoots and branches, the pleasure-loving thoughts ever spring up around us.

**64,60,5** For the thoughts in us are of two kinds: the one arising from the desire that coaxes within the body, which came about, as I said, from the inbreathing of the material spirit; the other from that which accords with the commandment, which we received to have as an implanted and natural law, rousing our reasoning towards the good and setting it right.

**64,60,6** Hence with the legislation of God we “delight” according to the mind (for this is the inner man), but with the legislation of the devil according to the desire that dwells in the flesh; for the law that is “warring against and working against the law of God”, that is, against the impulse towards the good, the desire of the mind, is the one that ever puts forth the passionate and material distractions towards lawlessness, being wholly given to drawing us towards pleasures.

**64,61,1** For Paul appears to me here to be openly positing three laws: one that accords with the good implanted in us, which he also plainly called the law of the mind; one that arises from the assault of the evil one and often drags the soul into passionate fancies, which he said wars against the law of the mind;

**64,61,2** and another, the one that accords with sin, which has hardened in the flesh as a result of desire, which he called the law of sin dwelling in the members; mounting upon it and riding on it, the evil one often urges it on against us, driving us together into unrighteousness and evil deeds.

**64,61,3** For the law that is breathed in from outside by the evil one and poured, like a stream of pitch, through the senses into the soul itself within, is strengthened by the law that exists in the flesh in accordance with desire.

**64,61,4** For it is evident that within us ourselves there is both the better and the worse; and when the better by nature becomes stronger than the worse, the whole mind is borne towards the good; but when the worse, which is said to war against the good in us, increases and gains the upper hand, then, the other way about, the man is led towards fancies of every kind and worse thoughts.

**64,62,1** It is on this account that the apostle prays to be delivered, considering it to be death and destruction, just as the prophet too does, saying, “From my hidden faults cleanse me.”

**64,62,2a** At any rate the very words show this, when he says, “For I delight in the law of God as regards the inward man, but I see a different law in my members, at war with the law of my mind and taking me captive to the law of sin that is in my members.

**64,62,2b** Wretched man that I am, who will deliver me from the body of this death?”

**64,62,3** He was not declaring the body to be death, but the law of sin, which lurks within us in the members [of the body] through the transgression and ever beguiles the soul with fancies towards the death of unrighteousness.

**64,62,4a** At any rate he adds at once, plainly resolving from what death he longed to be delivered and who it was that delivered him: “Thanks be to God through Jesus Christ.”

**64,62,4b** For it must be noted that, if he were calling this body death, Aglaophon, as you have supposed, he would not have brought in Christ as the one who afterwards delivers him from such an evil; for otherwise what more extraordinary or greater thing have we had from the coming of Christ?

**64,62,5** And why would the apostle have spoken at all as though through the coming of Christ he could be freed by God from this death, when it befell all to die, even before Christ came into the world?

**64,62,6** For all were delivered, being separated from their bodies at their departure from this life; indeed all souls alike, those of unbelievers and of believers, of the unrighteous and of the righteous, were parted from their bodies when the day of their end arrived.

**64,62,7a** What more, then, was the apostle eager to receive than the others who had spent their lives in unbelief?

**64,62,7b** Or why did he pray to be delivered from the body, a thing which would certainly have befallen him even if he did not wish it, just as it is appointed for all to die and for their souls to be separated from their bodies, if indeed he supposed the body to be the death of the soul?

**64,62,8** So it is not this body, Aglaophon, that he calls death, but the sin that has taken up its dwelling [within] in the body through desire, from which God delivered him through the coming of Christ.

**64,62,9** “For in Christ Jesus the law of the Spirit of life has freed us from the law of sin and of death”, so that “he who raised Jesus from the dead may, on account of his Spirit that dwells in us, give life also to our mortal bodies”,

**64,62,10** “sin in the body having been condemned” to destruction, “so that the just requirement of the” natural “law”, which draws us towards the good in accordance with the commandment, might be shown forth, kindled anew – the requirement which, having grown weak while the flesh was mastered by sin, was buried under material cares before the coming of Christ.

**64,62,11** “For what was impossible for the natural law in us, in that it was weak”, being worsted by the desire that lies in the body, God restored to strength, sending his own Son, who had taken up flesh like the flesh of sin (for what appeared was in truth and not in seeming),

**64,62,12** so that, sin having been condemned to destruction, “so as” no longer “to bear fruit” in the flesh, the just requirement of the natural law might be fulfilled, abounding in obedience in those who walk not according to the desire of the flesh, but according to the desire and the guidance of the Spirit.

**64,62,13** For it is “the law of the Spirit of life”, that is, the gospel, which, being different from the laws mentioned before and laid down through the preaching for obedience and the forgiveness of sins, “has freed us from the law of sin and of death”, having utterly conquered the sin that reigned over the flesh.

**64,62,14** What they put forward, then, from the sayings of the apostle as well, interpreting them wrongly, I have made plain and stated, Theophilus; and I shall turn also to the other matters, if indeed we find one who will help us through the course of the discussion.

**64,62,15a** For the matters that follow are difficult and not at all easy to demolish.

**64,62,15b** Therefore I take them in hand with some reluctance, seeing that their proof will be long and hard, unless some breeze of understanding, suddenly breathing down upon us from heaven, brings us, as though swimming in the midst of the sea, to some calm harbour and to a surer proof.

**64,63,1** These, then, are the passages, given in part and in sequence, [from] what was said by the blessed Methodius, also called Eubulius, about the aforesaid Origen and his false belief arising from sophistical error, in his discourse On the Resurrection [...]; and having set them out here we have considered them sufficient against that man’s nonsense and his overthrow [...] of human life through a malice fabricated out of Greek superstition.

**64,63,2** For many other things too have been said by the aforesaid Methodius, a learned man who contended vigorously for the truth, in the course of his treatment of the subject – surely as many again besides.

**64,63,3** But since we promised to say a few things against each heresy for its overthrow (for they are not few), we have been content to quote from his treatise up to this point.

**64,63,4a** And when we ourselves have added a few things more out of our poverty against this same nonsense of the man, we shall bring our contest against him to an end, having rendered the prize to God, who gives the victory and who in his own love for mankind ever adorns his church with unfading garlands, the proclamations of the truth.

**64,63,4b** We shall speak to him, then, ourselves as well, as far as we are able.

**64,63,5a** You say, you there, mockingly, as I have also shown above: Was God then a tanner, to make tunics of skin for Adam and those with him, when no animals had yet been slaughtered?

**64,63,5b** And even if animals were slaughtered, [...] they were not, he says, tunics of skin, but the earthly body which we wear.

**64,63,6** And you are refuted on every side, following the inspiration of the devil and the guile of the serpent, who works the corruption of unbelief for mankind, who deceived Eve, and who ever corrupts the minds of innocent souls by his craftiness in their thoughts.

**64,63,7** Let us see, then, whether what you have said can stand, you who toiled so much and drove the contest of writing so many books to no purpose.

**64,63,8** For if what is sung about you is true, that you wrote six thousand books, you toiler in vain, having spent all your labour on reproaches and deceits of vanity to no purpose and having made your work empty, stumbling in the essential matters through which you falsified the resurrection, you have gained for yourself a hopeless labour in your trade.

**64,63,9a** For if the body does not rise, neither will the soul inherit anything.

**64,63,9b** For the fellowship of body and soul is one and the same, and so is their labour.

**64,63,9c** And the faithful toil in body and soul for the hope of the inheritance after the resurrection, which according to you will not come to pass.

**64,63,9d** Then our faith is [empty], and empty too is our hope, according to the apostolic and true word that comes from the Spirit.

**64,63,10a** And you too, confessing a resurrection in a shifting way, holding it to be an imagined semblance and not a true one, are compelled to speak of the name only.

**64,63,10b** For what resurrection can be spoken of for a soul, which does not fall and is not buried?

**64,63,11a** But it is clear from the name that the resurrection preached everywhere and in all scripture by the sons of the truth is that of the body that has fallen and been buried.

**64,63,11b** But if it does not rise, there is no such thing as the resurrection preached through all the scriptures.

**64,63,12a** And if there is no resurrection, vain is the looking forward to the resurrection of the dead.

**64,63,12b** For the resurrection is not of souls, which have not fallen, but of bodies that have been buried.

**64,63,13a** And if one part of the body is raised and another part is laid aside, how then will this part fare?

**64,63,13b** For there cannot be in a body parts that are raised and parts that are laid aside and left behind.

**64,63,14** But that there is a natural body and there is a spiritual body – not one body spiritual and another natural, but the same body natural and the same spiritual – [...].

**64,63,15** For when we are in the world and do the corruptible works of the flesh, we have natural bodies; for we serve the soul in the world in unseemly things, as you too have said in part.

**64,63,16** But when we are raised, there is no longer the service of the soul, but the following of the Spirit, which even from here already has the pledge, through the saying, “If we have life by the Spirit, by the Spirit let us also keep in step; and if we do keep in step by the Spirit, then, putting the deeds of the body to death, we shall live.”

**64,63,17a** For there are no longer marriages.

**64,63,17b** No longer desires, no longer contests for those who profess continence, no longer lapses opposed to chastity, no longer the activities that are carried on here; but, as the Lord says, those who have been deemed worthy of that resurrection do not marry, nor are they given in marriage, but they are as angels.

**64,64,1a** Just as Enoch too was translated, that he should not see death, and he was not to be found.

**64,64,1b** But he was not translated leaving behind the body or part of the body.

**64,64,1c** For if he had left the body behind, he would then have seen death; but he who was translated with the body did not see death.

**64,64,1d** For he exists in a living body, borne along spiritually and not naturally because of his translation, though he is indeed in a spiritual body.

**64,64,2** And likewise concerning Elijah [...], that he was carried up in a fiery chariot and is in the flesh still, but in spiritual flesh, which has no need always, [as] when he lived in this world, of being fed by ravens and drinking from the brook Cherith and wearing a sheepskin; but he is nourished with some other, spiritual food – its provider is God, who knows hidden things and created the invisible – having as his food a kind of ambrosia, undefiled.

**64,64,3** And you see that the very body that is natural is itself spiritual, just as our Lord too rose from the dead, raising not another body but the very one that existed, and not a different one besides the one that existed, but changing that very one into spiritual fineness and uniting it wholly into one spiritual whole, entering when the doors were shut,

**64,64,4** a thing which cannot be the case with our bodies here, because of their grossness and their not yet having been united into spiritual fineness.

**64,64,5a** What, then, was it that entered when the doors were shut?

**64,64,5b** Was it something other than what was crucified, or the very thing that was crucified?

**64,64,5c** You will surely not be able, Origen, to avoid confessing that it was the very thing that was crucified.

**64,64,6a** For he refutes you through the precise inquiry of Thomas, saying to him, “Do not be unbelieving, but believing.”

**64,64,6b** For he showed the print of the nails and the print of the lance, and he left the very scars on his body, although he had united the body into one spiritual unity.

**64,64,7a** He could, then, have wiped away the scars as well, but he does not wipe them away, so as to refute you, you god-smitten man.

**64,64,7b** So then, it was the body that was buried in the tomb for the three days and rose with him in the resurrection.

**64,64,7c** For he showed bones and skin and flesh alike, as he said, “Observe that a spirit has no flesh and bones, as you see that I have.”

**64,64,8a** How, then, did he enter when the doors were shut?

**64,64,8b** It was to show that what was seen is a body and not a spirit, but a spiritual body and not a material one, though with soul and Godhead and the whole incarnation,

**64,64,9** being the same body but spiritual, the same that was once gross now fine, the same that was crucified [...], and the same that was seized now not to be seized, united and blended with the Godhead and no longer dissolved, but abiding for ever, no longer dying.

**64,64,10** “For Christ rose from among the dead, the first-fruits of those who have fallen asleep”; [and having risen] “he dies no more, death no longer has dominion over him.”

**64,65,1** And so that you may know this too, for what reason Christ is called “the first-fruits of those who have fallen asleep” – although it was not he who first began to rise; for before him Lazarus and the widow’s son rose through him, and others too through Elijah and through Elisha.

**64,65,2a** But since all those, having risen, died again, so it is that Christ is the first-fruits of those who have fallen asleep.

**64,65,2b** For having risen “he dies no more”, so that the resurrection to come is through his life and his love for mankind.

**64,65,3** But if he is the first-fruits of those who have fallen asleep, and his whole body rose with the Godhead, [it is evident that the whole of] his incarnation rose, nothing of it being left behind, neither body nor anything else; “for you will not abandon my soul to Hades, nor will you let your holy one see corruption.”

**64,65,4** And the words about the soul signify that it was not left behind in Hades, while “your holy one” is said to show the holy body that did not see corruption, but rose incorrupt after the three days and is united with incorruption for ever.

**64,65,5** But you say, you there, that these bodies are the tunics of skin, though the sequence of the text nowhere has this, but because of the seeds of the heathen and fabulous teaching of the Greeks that were sown in you from the beginning, and because of the malicious reasoning of unbelief about the resurrection that was stirred up in you from that source – I mean, because the Greeks led you to this and taught it to you.

**64,65,6** “For the natural man,” he says, “does not accept the things of the Spirit; for to him they are folly, because they are judged spiritually.”

**64,65,7a** For if the tunics had been made for Adam and those with him before the disobedience, your lie would have been plausible and able to deceive; but since it is evident at the forming of Eve [...].

**64,65,7b** From what, then, was she formed?

**64,65,7c** Plainly from a body; for “he cast,” it says, “a trance upon Adam, and he slept, and he took one of his ribs.”

**64,65,8a** And a rib is nothing other than bone.

**64,65,8b** For he built up “flesh in its place”.

**64,65,8c** And since flesh is named, how is its making still to be awaited?

**64,65,9a** And higher up it says, “Let us make man according to our image and according to likeness.”

**64,65,9b** “And he took,” it says, “dust from the ground and moulded the man.”

**64,65,9c** And dust and flesh are nothing other than body.

**64,65,10** Then afterwards, “And Adam, rising from his sleep, said, ‘This is bone from my bones and flesh from my flesh.’”

**64,65,11a** But there were not yet tunics of skin; the lie allegorised by you did not yet exist.

**64,65,11b** Bone, then, from my bones and flesh from my flesh: it is clear that they were bodies, and Adam and Eve were not bodiless.

**64,65,12a** “And she took from the tree and ate”, when she had been seduced by the serpent and had fallen into disobedience.

**64,65,12b** “And he heard the voice of God walking in paradise towards evening, and Adam and Eve were hidden among the trees.”

**64,65,12c** And God says to Adam, “Where are you?”

**64,65,12d** And he, being reproved, answers, “I heard your voice and hid myself, because I am naked.”

**64,65,13a** What, then, did he mean by naked?

**64,65,13b** Was he speaking of the soul or the body?

**64,65,13c** And what did the leaves of the fig tree cover, the soul or the body?

**64,65,14a** “And who told you,” he says, “that you are naked, unless you have eaten from the tree from which alone I commanded you not to eat?”

**64,65,14b** And he says, “The woman whom you gave me gave to me, and I ate.”

**64,65,14c** And from where was the woman given, if not from the rib, that is, from the body, before the tunics were given to them?

**64,65,15a** And to the woman he says, “Why have you done this?”

**64,65,15b** And she said, “The serpent deceived me, and I ate, and I gave to my husband too.”

**64,65,15c** And God brings the curse upon the serpent, and pain upon the woman, and upon the man the eating of bread in sweat.

**64,65,16** And after this he says, “See, Adam has become like one of us; lest he ever put forth his hand and lay hold of the tree of life, and he will live for ever.”

**64,65,17** And do not take it, hearer, that the Lord spoke by way of declaration when he said, “See, Adam has become like one of us”; for he says it by way of reproof, finding fault with the conceit of Adam, who had been persuaded by the serpent’s deceit: Did he become what he thought he would become? – that is, Has Adam become like one of us? For Adam, desiring to become higher, fell into the lower state.

**64,65,18** And the words “Let us cast him out, lest he ever reach his hand towards the tree of life and eat, and he will live for ever” God speaks not out of envy; rather, he was taking care of the vessel that had been impaired through its own fault, so that it might not remain impaired for ever;

**64,65,19** but that, like a most excellent potter, having returned the vessel damaged through its own fault to its own clay, the earth, he might in the resurrection refashion the righteous man impaired in nothing, immortal in glory and able to enjoy the kingdom, and the unrighteous man, in the perfect resurrection, able to bear the punishment of the judgement.

**64,65,20** For God planted nothing evil – far from it! – but he planted the tree itself as well, permitting him to take from it at the due time by his own command.

**64,65,21a** But you will say in reply: How then is “In whatever day you eat of it, you shall die by death” fulfilled, if indeed he was to eat of it?

**64,65,21b** For in eating, at whatever time it might be, he would certainly have fulfilled “you shall die by death”.

**64,65,22** And to him who says this I say in turn: God, “[who] knows the things to come before they happen”, knowing before his command that he would be deceived and would eat of it, appointed death because of the transgression that was to be committed by him.

**64,65,23** For in this the heresies go astray and blaspheme God, saying, “A fine God, the God of the law, seeing that he envied Adam and cast him out, saying, ‘Let us cast him out, lest he ever stretch out his hand and take from the tree of life, and he will live for ever.’”

**64,65,24a** But their folly is exposed as slander.

**64,65,24b** For not only did he not forbid eating from the tree of life from the beginning, but he even urged it, saying, “Of every tree that is in paradise you shall eat for food.”

**64,65,24c** And the tree of life too was one among all the trees of paradise [that were] before Adam’s eyes.

**64,65,25a** But only of the tree of knowing what can be known of good and evil did he say not to eat.

**64,65,25b** But Adam’s insatiable mind, being innocent and [...], when his wife Eve had been deceived by the devil, acted rather against the commandment.

**64,65,26** Since, then, Adam had come into an impaired state through his own fault, God did not wish him to live for ever in an impaired state; but, like a most excellent potter, he was pleased to turn the vessel spoiled through its own fault back into its own clay, so that in the regeneration he might once more, as on the wheel, transform it out of its own clay, reshaping and renewing the vessel without impairment, so that it can live for ever.

**64,65,27** Hence on the first occasion he threatens death, but on the second he no longer speaks of death, but “Earth you are, and to earth you shall depart”, not having handed the man over to death.

**64,65,28a** [...] And after other things: “And God made tunics of skin and put them on Adam and those with him, and drove them out of paradise.”

**64,65,28b** And you see that the empty talk newly invented by you is vain, Origen.

**64,65,28c** For how long a time before that did Adam and those with him already have bodies!

**64,66,1** But if this puts you to the question and you cannot, with your natural way of thinking, receive the grace of the Spirit, you unbeliever who are even beyond the unbelievers, tell me: each thing that has come into being from God – how much wonder-working and amazement does it hold?

**64,66,2a** Whence has the heaven, out of things that were not, been stretched out, spread wide aloft?

**64,66,2b** Whence was the sun made to shine, and the moon and the stars created?

**64,66,2c** From what original matter was the earth taken, coming into being out of things that were not?

**64,66,2d** From what substances were the mountains hewn?

**64,66,3a** Whence the framework of the whole world, provided by God out of things that were not?

**64,66,3b** Whence the formation of the clouds, which covers the heaven at a touch?

**64,66,4a** Whence came the supply of the gnat and the midge to his servant Moses at the command of God?

**64,66,4b** How did he change the wooden rod into a serpent, alive and creeping?

**64,66,4c** Whence came the changing of the hand of Moses into snow?

**64,66,5a** So too at that time God willed, you unbeliever, and at the very moment of willing he made for Adam and those with him tunics that were of skin by nature, without animals, without any human craft and elaborate workmanship, just as from the beginning, as soon as he willed, both heaven and all things came into being.

**64,66,5b** And we have spoken of these things often, both in other places and above.

**64,66,6** And for those who wish [...] to live, salvation is brief and false belief is readily refuted; but for those who do not wish to accept the school of salvation, not even the whole of eternity would suffice for discussion, “the hearing being ever deaf, like that of a [deaf] asp that stops its ears, so as not to admit a charmer’s voice and a drug compounded by the wise”, according to the divine word; but to the sons of the truth, I think, the argument is of no small benefit, even if it is [not] set down here by me at length.

**64,67,1a** And I shall come to your discourse on the resurrection from the first psalm.

**64,67,1b** For when you deceive the ignorant, slipping in your own way of thinking, you toiler in vain, and say [that] some of the simpler folk think that the ungodly will not obtain resurrection, and afterwards show the same simpler folk being questioned again by you as to what sort of body the resurrection will be of, and how you will raise counter-difficulties by yourself in the person of those whom you call the simpler [...] (for this too must needs be said plainly), to call good those whom you call the simpler;

**64,67,2** [for] the statement does not come from you, nor is the credit due to your statement, but to the truth, which compels you to utter proofs of the excellence and goodness of the servants of God.

**64,67,3a** For the writing of those outside also says, “Simple is the word of truth.”

**64,67,3b** And we are accustomed to call the guileless simple, for whom praise is found with the Saviour in many places, [as] in “Be innocent as doves” and in “Let the children come to me, since the kingdom of heaven belongs to such as these”, that is, to the simplest.

**64,67,4a** And the simpler folk answered you, as you say, that the resurrection is of this body which we wear.

**64,67,4b** And when they were met with objections by you and asked: Of the whole, or of a part?

**64,67,4c** They answer in reply, saying: Of the whole body.

**64,67,5** But when you, following the method of your great nonsense, say that this cannot be at all, because of the blood and flesh and hair that have flowed out of bodies, and the other things that flow from us through spittle and nostrils and excrement, great is the trickery of your medico-sophistical and false-believing opinion, as indeed that more excellent elder of ours, the most blessed man Methodius, has already exposed your myth-making by many arguments.

**64,67,6a** But you shall hear a few things from us of little account as well.

**64,67,6b** For everything that is sought is always sought in its purest state, and what is superfluous, being cast off from what is pure, is not sought for.

**64,67,7a** For the garment is whole from the time it received the weaving of its web, and it is the very thing that is cut from the loom, with neither [anything] added to it nor taken away.

**64,67,7b** And when handed to a fuller, it will be demanded back from the fuller no less, but it is received back from the fuller himself in its most perfect state;

**64,67,8** so that it has become plain to all that it is the same garment in its perfection, and has become no smaller a body, except for the casting off of the dirt and the uncleanness; and surely it is not because it has cast off the dirt that the craftsman will be asked by us for it together with the dirt, but for the garment itself, safe and sound and most clean.

**64,67,9** And again, another example: for by you the question was brought forward about what is discharged through blood-lettings and diseases and excrement and the discharge of spittle and of the nostrils; but you will be overturned by the very things you have said.

**64,67,10** For not only is this so in the body, but creatures, so to speak, grow from us, lice and nits, which are neither outside the body nor reckoned as part of the body.

**64,67,11** And no one ever sought out a nit discharged from the body in order to keep it, but rather to destroy it, nor a louse produced from the flesh itself, nor would anyone count its loss as damage.

**64,67,12** [So too] the things that have flowed from us, even if it is so according to your argument, will not foolishly be sought by us, nor would God bring them into our composition; but, like dirt that comes from the garment itself and has been cast off from it for the sake of neatness, he would leave them aside as of secondary account, while that complete garment, plainly, lacking nothing and having nothing superfluous, the Creator would bring forth by the goodness of his art; for all things are possible to him.

**64,67,13** But if it were not so, you whose wits have been injured by your wordy invention, then our Saviour and Lord, God’s Son, he who came [in order] to make our life wholly secure, and who in himself modelled our hope in the fullest measure so as to commend his truth to us, would have had to lay aside part of himself and raise up part, according to your fabulous reconstruction and your gathering of a multitude of vain reasonings, you lover of strife.

**64,67,14** For he himself, refuting your way at once, declares, saying, “Unless the grain of wheat falls into the ground and dies, it remains alone; but if it falls and dies, it bears many grains.”

**64,67,15a** And what grain, then, did he mean?

**64,67,15b** It is plain to everyone and acknowledged by the whole world that it was of himself he spoke, that is, of the body of holy flesh that he took from Mary, and of his whole incarnation.

**64,67,16** And the falling and dying – as he says, “Where the carcass is, there the eagles will be gathered” – he spoke of with reference to the three days’ sleep of his body, as you yourself will confess; for his Godhead is sleepless, unfallen, not to be seized, unchangeable.

**64,67,17a** The grain of wheat, then, died and rose.

**64,67,17b** Did the whole grain rise, or a remnant of it?

**64,67,17c** Was it some other grain that rose instead of the one that existed, or did the very one that was rise into being?

**64,67,17d** [...] that what Joseph prepared for burial in a linen cloth and laid in a new tomb rose, you will surely not deny.

**64,67,18a** Whom, then, did the angels announce to the women as raised?

**64,67,18b** As they say, “Whom are you looking for?

**64,67,18c** Jesus the Nazoraean?

**64,67,18d** He is raised; he is not here.

**64,67,18e** Come and look at the place.”

**64,67,18f** As if to say: come and look at the place, and teach Origen some sense, that no remnant lies here, but the whole has risen.

**64,67,19** And so that you may know that the whole has risen, [he says], “He is raised; he is not here,” to beat back your nonsense, that no remnant of him was left behind; but the very body that was nailed, the very body that was pierced by the lance, the very body that was seized by the Pharisees, the very body that was spat upon, [rose].

**64,68,1a** And why should I say a great deal to refute this pitiable man and the nonsense that arose in him?

**64,68,1b** So then, as he rose and raised his own body, so too he will raise us.

**64,68,2a** For from this the holy apostle showed our hope, saying, “How is it that some among you say that resurrection of the dead does not exist?

**64,68,2b** But if resurrection of the dead does not exist, Christ has not been raised either.

**64,68,2c** And if Christ has not been raised, then our preaching too is empty, and our faith also is vain.

**64,68,2d** And we are found to be false witnesses of God too, because we said that he raised Christ, whom he did not raise”, and so on.

**64,68,3** And later he goes on to say, “It is needful for this corruptible to clothe itself in incorruption, and for this mortal to clothe itself in immortality”; and he said not just “the mortal”, nor just “the corruptible”, nor “the immortal soul”, but “this corruptible”, the word “this” being added, and “this mortal”, the word “this” being added.

**64,68,4** His very grain, then, rose whole: not a part rose, but the whole rose; and not another grain besides the one that existed rose, but the very one that had fallen in the tomb rose whole.

**64,68,5a** And how can your empty talk prevail?

**64,68,5b** For the divine scripture knows of two grains spoken of, the one in the gospel, the other in the apostle.

**64,68,6a** And the one makes the matter entirely clear by what was perfectly accomplished in him, which is the prototype of [our] resurrection.

**64,68,6b** For in both teaching and doing this, the Saviour accomplished everything securely as a demonstration for us.

**64,68,7** For no sooner had he spoken about the grain than he raised the grain, so that he might confirm for us in truth the faith of the hope of our resurrection.

**64,68,8** Taking it up from this, the apostle, borne along by the Holy Spirit, recounting to us the glory of the saints that will be after the resurrection, shows again, by means of the grain of wheat, the [hope] of the enjoyment of good things,

**64,68,9a** making proclamation against the unbelievers: “But you will say to me, How are the dead raised?

**64,68,9b** And with what kind of body do they come?”

**64,68,9c** And he says to the one who says such things, “Fool.”

**64,68,9d** For he who has any doubt at all about the resurrection is a fool and without understanding.

**64,68,10a** Then he says, “Fool! What you sow is not brought to life unless it dies.

**64,68,10b** And what you sow, you do not sow the body that is to be, but a bare grain, perhaps of wheat or of some other seed, and it is not brought to life unless it dies.

**64,68,10c** But God gives it a body as he has willed, and to each of the seeds its own body.”

**64,68,11a** And you observe that there is no exchanging of the body.

**64,68,11b** For no one who has sown barley looks for wheat, nor has anyone sown black cumin and found barley; but what is sown is the very thing that is raised.

**64,68,12** And even if [something] of it is left down below in the earth and the shoot springs up from it, here, in this perishable wheat which does not come to judgement, what is left behind is useless, while what springs up from it is finer.

**64,68,13a** But it was on account of the unbelief of those not eagerly awaiting the hope of God that he wished to show the splendour.

**64,68,13b** For in truth the grain of wheat is very small.

**64,68,13c** But where, in such a very tiny grain, are the roots and bases and stalks and joints and so many tubes and heads and husks and awns and multiplying grains?

**64,69,1a** And that we may speak more clearly still, recounting things like these: whence was it given to Moses, son of Jochebed, son of Amram, to strike the rock with a rod and to bring forth water from matter without resource, to change the dry into the wet?

**64,69,1b** To strike the sea and divide it into twelve roads for journeying in the sea at the command [of God]?

**64,69,1c** To change the river into blood?

**64,69,2a** To gather so great a multitude of frogs at a touch?

**64,69,2b** To send the midge upon the Egyptians?

**64,69,2c** To mix hail with fire?

**64,69,2d** To pour upon the Egyptians a black and moonless night, full of gloom?

**64,69,3a** To slay the firstborn of the Egyptians in death?

**64,69,3b** To guide with a pillar of fire a people shepherded through him?

**64,69,3c** To bring down angels’ bread by prayer and entreaty?

**64,69,3d** To send up the quails and satisfy so many myriads of men by the command of God?

**64,69,4a** To hear the voice of God?

**64,69,4b** To be counted worthy, in the midst of so many myriads, to hear the voice of God and to converse with God?

**64,69,4c** For forty days and forty nights not to seek the things needful to the nature of our make?

**64,69,4d** To have his flesh changed into the brightness of the sun and a shining ray, dazzling the people so that the sons of Israel could not gaze upon his face?

**64,69,5a** To have his hand, which was of flesh, changed into snow?

**64,69,5b** To command the earth to open its mouth to receive Korah and Dathan and Abiram and Onan?

**64,69,6a** To hear at the end, “Go up to the mountain, and die there”?

**64,69,6b** That no man should know his burial place?

**64,69,6c** The divine writing signifies that the body of Moses was not buried by men but, as the sense suggests, by holy angels;

**64,69,7** and all these things while he was still in this world, still in this natural body, which at the same time had been made perfectly spiritual.

**64,69,8** And having received from here the pledge [...] as a pattern of the perfect sprouting that will be then, when the saying is fulfilled, “It is sown in dishonour, raised up in glory; sown in weakness, raised up in power.”

**64,69,9a** For how is that which is sown not weak, not knowing where it is sown?

**64,69,9b** How is that not without honour which is cast into a tomb and heaped over with dust, torn apart and crumbling and without feeling?

**64,69,10** How is that not honoured which is raised and abides for ever and, in the hope of God’s love for mankind, gains a kingdom in heaven, where “the righteous will shine like the sun”, where they will be “equal to angels”, where they will dance with the bridegroom, where Peter and those with him “will be seated upon twelve thrones, passing judgement on the twelve tribes of Israel”, where the just will receive “what eye has not seen and ear has not heard, and what has not entered into the heart of man, what God has prepared for those who love him”?

**64,69,11** Our resurrection, then, is in God, and that of every man, righteous and unrighteous, unbeliever and believer, some being raised to eternal life, others to eternal judgement.

**64,70,1** And be silent, Babylon, the ancient confusion renewed again for us; be muzzled, Sodom, and the cry of your great wickedness that goes up to God;

**64,70,2** for “the deliverer will come from Zion and turn away ungodliness from Jacob”, and “he will sound the trumpet, and the dead will rise”, and “we shall be snatched up into the air to meet him”; as that more excellent [elder of ours and] blessed Methodius said, and we ourselves too, building [upon] the same things, have added.

**64,70,3a** For from the words joined to each expression one can see the upshot.

**64,70,3b** For the holy apostle, distinguishing the form of the two ways, brought them together into one hope from “we shall be snatched up in clouds to meet him”, to show that it is truly this body, and not another besides this, [that rises];

**64,70,4a** for he who is snatched up has not yet died.

**64,70,4b** And when he shows that “we shall not come before the resurrection of the dead”, so as to prove that what is impossible with men is readily provided and possible with God (“for we who are alive shall by no means come before those who have fallen asleep and their resurrection”), he showed the living and those snatched up, so as to show from the living that the bodies of those others are raised entire, and to prove the power of God from the fact that those others come before the living who remain.

**64,70,5a** “For the dead will rise, and those in the tombs will be raised,” says the prophet.

**64,70,5b** And so that I may not pass over in silence what was said about the resurrection by the prophet Ezekiel in his own apocryphon, I shall set this down here too.

**64,70,6** For, telling it in a riddle, he speaks about the just judgement in which soul and body share: that a certain king had everyone in his kingdom enrolled as soldiers, and had no civilian except two only, a lame man and a blind man, and each [of them] sat on his own and dwelt on his own.

**64,70,7** And the king, making a wedding feast for his own son, invited all who were in his kingdom, but slighted the pair of civilians, both the lame and the blind; and they were indignant within themselves and devised to work a plot against the king.

**64,70,8a** Now the king had a garden, and from a distance the blind man spoke to the lame man, saying, “How much would our morsel of bread have been, among the crowds invited to the festivity?

**64,70,8b** Come then, as he has done to us, let us take our revenge on him.”

**64,70,8c** And the other asked, “In what way?”

**64,70,9a** And he said, “Let us go into his garden and destroy there what belongs to the garden.”

**64,70,9b** And the other said, “And how can I, being lame and unable to get about?”

**64,70,9c** And the blind man said, “Can I myself do anything, not seeing where I am going?

**64,70,9d** But let us contrive something.”

**64,70,10a** Plucking the grass nearby and plaiting a rope, he threw it to the blind man and said, “Take hold, and come along the rope to me.”

**64,70,10b** And when he had done what he was urged to do, when he arrived, he says, “Come, become feet for me and carry me, and I become eyes for you, guiding you from above to right and to left.”

**64,70,11** Having done this, they went down into the garden; and then, whether they did harm or did no harm, their footprints at any rate were visible in the garden.

**64,70,12a** And those who had feasted, departing from the wedding and going down into the garden, were astonished to find the footprints in the garden, and reported this to the king, saying, “All in your kingdom are soldiers, and there is no civilian.

**64,70,12b** Whence, then, are there footprints of civilians in the garden?”

**64,70,13a** And he marvelled – as the parable of the apocryphon says, that is, speaking in riddles as though of a man; but God is ignorant of nothing.

**64,70,13b** And the narrative says that he summoned the lame and the blind man and put the question to the blind man, “Was it you who went down into the garden?”

**64,70,13c** And he said, “Alas, lord, you see our infirmity; you know that I do [not] see where I walk.”

**64,70,14a** Then, coming to the lame man, he asked him too, “Did you go down into my garden?”

**64,70,14b** And he answered and said, “O lord, you wish to embitter my soul over my infirmity.”

**64,70,14c** And so the judgement comes to a standstill.

**64,70,15a** What, then, does the just judge do?

**64,70,15b** Perceiving in what way the two had been yoked together, he sets the lame man on the blind one and examines them both with scourges, and they cannot deny it.

**64,70,16a** Each convicts the other, the lame man saying to the blind, “Did you not carry me and bear me off?”

**64,70,16b** And the blind man to the lame, “Did you yourself not become my eyes?”

**64,70,17** In this way the body is linked to the soul, and the soul in turn to the body, for the conviction of their common work, and the judgement becomes complete concerning both, body and soul, [...] the works that have been done, whether good or bad.

**64,70,18a** And you see, you who care for your own life, that all who have been carried away against the truth have procured unrighteousness for themselves.

**64,70,18b** As the holy prophet David too says, “He conceived toil and brought forth lawlessness.”

**64,70,19** For everyone who gathers to himself the toil of alien thoughts brings forth unrighteousness for himself and for those who are persuaded by him: “He dug a pit and hollowed it out, and he will fall into the hole that he made.”

**64,70,20a** But if anyone has anything to say against all this, let him come forward; if anyone [wishes] to oppose God, let him dare.

**64,70,20b** For God, the mighty one, “will not grow weary nor hunger nor thirst, nor is there any searching out of his understanding”, in which he raises the bodies that have been dissolved, in which he saves what is perishing,

**64,70,21** in which he brings the dead to life, in which he clothes the corruptible with incorruption, in which he brings the grain that fell to resurrection, in which he leads what was sown and died to greater splendour, renewing it; as is found in many scriptures, which speak in riddles of our resurrection.

**64,71,1** And in the psalm [of] David concerning the dedication of the house of David, the prophet, as one eagerly awaiting it and looking by the Holy Spirit to what was to come, spoke fittingly about the resurrection: “I will exalt you, Lord, for you have taken me up, renewing my house” – that is, the body that had fallen – “and you have not made my enemies rejoice over me.”

**64,71,2** And Solomon likewise, in the Proverbs, preparing everything of hope for the readiness required there, urged, saying, “Prepare your works for the departure” (calling the release from here a departure), “and make yourself ready,” he says, “for the field”, extending the admonition to all alike, to those in the country and those in the town, to the learned and to those who take up the mechanical crafts, for whom no work in the field is required;

**64,71,3a** for whence would linen-weavers and silversmiths, poets and prose-writers have any readiness for the ploughland?

**64,71,3b** For the saying has called all together without distinction in saying, “Make yourself ready for the field”, hinting at [what] other than this, that for every man, townsman and countryman alike, the field is the laying down of the body, the end through burial?

**64,71,4a** Then afterwards, indicating the same hope of the resurrection, he says, “And you will rebuild your house.”

**64,71,4b** He did not say, “You will build the house”; for it was built once, in the forming in the womb, when the mothers conceived each of us in the forming; but the resurrection is from the earth, or from the field, no longer a building but a rebuilding, because of the demolition brought upon it in the fall of burial;

**64,71,5** as the Saviour too declared, “Pull down this temple, and within three days I raise it up”, or, I build it, being Wisdom and in such “understanding” [...], of which “there is no” “searching out” by men, in which, when our bodies have been scattered, some into ashes, some in the sea, some destroyed by flesh-eating birds, some by wild beasts, some by worms, [...] he brings them back from places beyond reach.

**64,71,6a** For if he made [the things that are] out of things that were not into being, how much more easily will he restore what exists to its own condition?

**64,71,6b** So that he may decree a just judgement, so that he may not judge one in place of another, so that he may not defraud what has toiled.

**64,71,7** For if the enjoyment and the inheriting of the heavens’ kingdom belong to the soul, let the body partake of whatever it wishes; let Gideon and those with him live in luxury and not be ill-treated “in sheepskins and goatskins”; let John not toil in vain, having his clothing from camels; let us not buffet the flesh in the life of withdrawal; let us not subdue our bodies in chastity.

**64,71,8** But if the body shares with the soul in ways of life, in chastity, in fasting and the other virtues, “God is not unjust”, to defraud the labour of the one who has laboured and not to pay the reward to the body that has laboured together with the soul.

**64,71,9** For at once the judgement will be found idle; for if the soul is found entirely alone, when judged it might reply that the cause of the sin is not from me, but from that corruptible and earthly body came the fornicating, the adultery, the licentiousness; for since it departed from me, I have done none of these things; and it will have a ready defence and will undo the judgement of God.

**64,71,10** But even if God should bring the body by itself – for he can, as has also been shown by me above through Ezekiel; even if the deed was done through a parable, yet the form was completed as an allegory of the truth then set forth in a parable, how bone was gathered to bone and joint to joint, and while the bones were dry and there was not yet any soul or spirit moving in them, at once the bodies were gathered and, according to the word of the prophet, were made firm.

**64,71,11** And if God wills, he is able to make this be present and move without a soul, just as the blood of Abel, which is body, spoke after death, and not the soul; for the blood is not soul, but what is seen is body.

**64,71,12a** But a body will not be able to be judged without a soul.

**64,71,12b** For it too will reply, saying: It was not I that sinned, but the soul; since it was separated from me, did I commit adultery, did I fornicate, did I worship idols?

**64,71,12c** And the body will be contradicting the righteous judgement of God, and contradicting it with good reason.

**64,71,13** For the sake of this, then, and for many other necessary reasons, God leads, in his wisdom, our dead bodies and the souls to regeneration in his promise of love for mankind, so that he who has laboured in holiness may receive from God the full and good recompense, and those who have done vain things may likewise be judged, body together with soul and soul together with body.

**64,71,14** And for the fuller assurance of our life, having come in the flesh and become man most perfectly [...] to establish faith in him within us, foreknowing the unbelief that would arise in you, Origen, [...] what is doubted most of all by you and in many heresies like your unbelief, those of the Manichaeans and Marcionites, and so having perfected all things in himself, he did it manifestly, for the confirmation and firm grounding of faith in him and of the truth.

**64,71,15** For when he rose from the dead he raised with him many bodies of the saints, and they entered with him into the holy city, as I have related in other places too.

**64,71,16** And he did not say, Saints rose, so as not to give a pretext to the method of malice; but he was at pains [...] that very thing which by its nature is doubted among the unbelievers, for the confirmation of our knowledge concerning life, by saying bodies of the saints.

**64,71,17a** And not only that he raised them, but also that they made [themselves] manifest to many in the city, in which was fulfilled through his power the saying, “Leading out the prisoners in might”, meaning the souls of the bodies that had been raised.

**64,71,17b** For it was these who were the camp’s captives, those held in fetters in Hades;

**64,71,18** and he says, “Likewise those who provoke, who dwell in tombs”, meaning the bodies of those who rose; and he did not say, those who were provoked or are being provoked, but those who provoke.

**64,71,19** For those who had died recently, being seen by many in the city together with the most ancient – for I think that he began to make the resurrection from Adam, and the recent ones [...] of the same bodies, [...] them buried in the place of Golgotha, over him [...] having been crucified and fulfilling what is written, “Awake, sleeper, and arise from the dead, and Christ will shine upon you” – he who was crucified above you – and others with them from among their own people recognising [them], at first astonished those who saw them.

**64,71,20a** For if a father chanced upon his child who had risen, or a brother upon his brother, or [a kinsman] upon a kinsman who had died twenty or ten years before, they asked in their amazement: Are you not so-and-so, who was buried by us in such-and-such a place?

**64,71,20b** How did you rise and come?

**64,71,21** And when they were asked in turn by the one who had risen: What took place in your midst here three days ago, when the ground was shaken? – and said: We seized a certain deceiver who was leading the people astray, Jesus by name, and crucified him, and the deception has ceased,

**64,71,22** and when he then confessed the grace of the Lord and the truth and said, “Woe to you, because the originator of the world’s salvation is the one you have denied and crucified; for he raised us by his mighty power of Godhead and incarnation”, then indeed the divine scripture was fulfilled, the saying, “Likewise those who provoke, who dwell in tombs.”

**64,71,23** For hearing from those who had been raised that it was through the Lord Jesus that they had risen, they were provoked, having taken it in hand, even to the point of death, both to deny and to crucify the originator of life.

**64,71,24a** And perhaps it was also for the profit of those who beheld the raised that the Lord, who loves mankind, wrought this.

**64,71,24b** For we think that many, seeing those who had been raised and being pierced to the heart, were both helped and believed.

**64,71,24c** Therefore you too, Origenists, be persuaded and believe, and do not destroy many by your error.

**64,72,1** But these things will suffice me against Origen the would-be wise, who vainly gave himself the name Adamantius and who, in keeping with his own absurdity, wickedly devised a destructive teaching against the truth in many parts of the faith.

**64,72,2** And passing on from this man’s heresy too, I shall examine the others in order, beloved, calling upon God as usual as the helper of our lack of learning, so as to be able to withstand every vain voice raised up against the truth and to defeat it, according to what was said by the holy Isaiah, “Every voice will rise up against you; you will defeat them all,

**64,72,3** and they will be guilty in it”, so that, having thus in God accomplished our promise to those who wish to read with understanding, as an exercise of truth and a healing remedy concerning each beast and venomous reptile – I mean the characters of the heresies – like an antidote, and concerning this one too, that of the Origenists, which is shaped like a toad that churns about in much moisture and cries out with a louder voice,

**64,72,4** taking the resurrection of the Lord as an antidote drunk beforehand, so to speak, we may spit out the slimy venom of the toad and the wickedness of the evil reptile.

**64,72,5** For it has befallen him, together with all who are persuaded by him, to suffer this too, for which reason I also groan over him: alas, how you have been harmed, and how many others you have harmed; bitten as by a terrible viper – I mean the worldly preliminary education – you have become poison to others too.

**64,72,6** For the naturalists say that the dormouse lives in a burrow and bears many young together, up to five and more, and that vipers hunt them.

**64,72,7** And if the viper finds the whole burrow, being unable to devour them all, it eats one or two at a time to its fill, and, piercing the eyes of the rest, it brings them food and rears them, now blinded, until it wishes to bring out each of them and eat it.

**64,72,8** And should it happen that some inexperienced people light upon such creatures and take them to eat, they take poison for themselves, [those] reared on the venom of the viper.

**64,72,9** So you too, Origen, blinded in your mind by the aforesaid Greek education, have vomited venom upon those who were persuaded by you and have become for them a food of poison, wronging the many through the very things by which you yourself have been wronged.
